**Summary.** The paper models the Mpemba effect in a polymer-bead system by coupling underdamped bead dynamics to the polymer's force landscape. It demonstrates that the bead's inertia (mass) acts as a control parameter, allowing researchers to tune the anomalous relaxation time through kinetic energy accumulation across structural plateaus. This provides a theoretical framework for understanding how initial conditions dictate relaxation rates in complex soft matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects non-equilibrium statistical mechanics concepts (like anomalous relaxation) to mesoscopic physical systems (polymers), offering a clear, tunable mechanism based on inertial effects that could be relevant for understanding complex biological or soft matter dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the Mpemba effect—an anomalous relaxation phenomenon where initial conditions lead to faster relaxation times—and proposes that system inertia can control this effect.

**Main result.** Inertia, controlled by bead mass, provides a tunable mechanism for observing the Mpemba effect in polymer dynamics; larger masses allow the bead to retain kinetic energy memory across force plateaus, accelerating relaxation.

**Method.** The authors use numerical simulations solving an underdamped Langevin equation coupled to a polymer's force-extension relation, analyzing time evolution after thermal or mechanical quenches.

**Model / system.** The system models a point mass (bead) attached to a polymer (like DNA) undergoing denaturation. The dynamics are governed by the bead's underdamped motion coupled to the polymer's force landscape, which features a plateau regime.

**Key observables.** Relaxation time ($\tau_{\text{relax}}$), kinetic energy $\langle K(t) \rangle$, and mean extension $\langle x(t) \rangle$ as functions of initial temperature ($T_0$) or force quench magnitude.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bead mass ($m$), initial temperature ($T_0$), applied external force ($f_{\text{ex}}$), and the operational definition of the plateau regime ($|df_{\text{poly}}/dx| < 0.05$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The polymer is assumed to follow an equilibrium force–extension relation even during non-equilibrium bead motion, and hydrodynamic memory effects are neglected.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate the Mpemba effect via overtaking relaxation curves (Fig. 1(c)) and show how $\tau_{\text{relax}}$ exhibits nonmonotonicity with $T_0$ for different bead masses.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model using a Langevin equation, details simulation protocols involving equilibration and quenching, analyzes the role of inertia (mass) in controlling relaxation dynamics across force plateaus, and compares thermal vs. mechanical quench mechanisms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose an experimentally motivated model in which the Mpemba effect can be controlled through the system's inertia. The model describes a polymer undergoing a denaturation transition whose force-extension curve contains a plateau that slows relaxation. When the system is initially prepared at a higher temperature or under a weaker stretching force, the bead accumulates greater kinetic energy, allowing it to cross the plateau more rapidly and thereby producing the Mpemba effect. Increasing the bead mass broadens the range of initial temperatures over which this mechanism operates. A similar mechanism also generates the inverse Mpemba effect.

</details>

**Summary.** The research investigates strongly driven superconducting transmon circuits to push operational limits. By engineering the circuit to suppress unwanted transitions, the authors observe that increasing drive power causes the Josephson potential to first collapse and then invert its sign. This inversion reveals a novel physical mechanism—dynamical stabilization analogous to an inverted pendulum—opening new avenues for quantum state control.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete experimental evidence of non-trivial dynamical phenomena (potential collapse/inversion) in superconducting circuits under strong driving, which has implications for designing robust quantum control protocols beyond simple resonance avoidance.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming dynamic range limitations in strongly driven superconducting circuits caused by unwanted transitions, which obscures fundamental physics.

**Main result.** The authors experimentally demonstrate the collapse of the Josephson potential to zero, followed by a clear sign inversion as drive power increases. This inversion dynamically stabilizes the transmon at its unstable equilibrium point, analogous to an inverted pendulum.

**Method.** The study employs spectroscopy and readout experiments on a carefully engineered transmon-resonator system to track the time evolution of the Josephson potential under increasing microwave drive power.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a transmon-resonator circuit embedding a symmetric SQUID, described by a Hamiltonian involving Josephson energy modulated by an external flux drive. The engineering minimizes spurious modes via native cosine-cosine coupling.

**Key observables.** Time evolution of the Josephson potential (collapse and inversion), qubit frequency shifts ($\chi_i(n)$), and the measured sign reversal of $\langle \cos \hat{\varphi} angle$ during readout.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Drive power ($P$) leading to specific photon numbers ($n$), operating in regimes where $\omega_a/\omega_{01} \gg 1$, and the critical point around $12.5 	imes 10^3$ photons.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The interpretation relies on fitting nonlinear qubit frequency shifts to a renormalized Hamiltonian, with final confirmation of inversion coming from direct measurement of $\langle \cos \hat{\varphi} angle$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transmon circuit concept and potential energy vs. phase; subsequent figures detail the three spectroscopic regimes (flattening, collapse, revival) showing the clear sign reversal in the readout signal.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical system and overcoming spurious mode limitations to performing spectroscopy that reveals potential renormalization, culminating in a direct readout measurement confirming the potential's inversion.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconducting circuits embedding Josephson junctions leverage microwave drives for control and measurement of quantum states. Although increasing the drive power is desirable for improving the efficiency of these operations, it eventually triggers unwanted transitions to uncontrolled states. While careful choice of circuit symmetries and parameters can mitigate these effects, the presence of spurious circuit modes spoils the resilience to high power. In this work, we engineer a transmon-resonator system free of any detrimental unwanted transitions. This resilience enables us to access drive powers at which we uncover a remarkable physical phenomenon: the collapse and inversion of the Josephson potential. Through spectroscopy and readout experiments, we confirm that the driven potential goes to zero and inverts as the power increases. The inversion corresponds to the dynamical stabilization of the transmon at its unstable equilibrium point, directly analogous to an inverted pendulum. This result reveals a new limitation of strongly driven superconducting circuits beyond drive-induced transitions. In addition, the dynamical renormalization of the Josephson potential opens up novel avenues for the control of superconducting circuits, and the autonomous stabilization of noise-resilient quantum states.

</details>

### [Benchmarking quantum simulation at scale](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14212v1)

**Authors:** Jeremy Hartse, Mohsin Raza, Shravan Shravan, Ivan H. Deutsch, Niklas Mueller  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.14212v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `scars & prethermalization` **4/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.14212_figures/2607.14212_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Benchmarking Quantum Simulations at Scale. A pro- tocol for benchmarking the fidelity of quantum simulations of non-equilibrium dynamics in regimes beyond classical simula- bility. We consider models which are non-integrable, for which typical states, |ψH⟩, thermalize. Such dynamics are not effi- ciently classically simulable, and benchmarking performance for such states is intractable. For models that host stabilizer scars, under consideration here, we can exploit the fact that for initial states in this subspace, |ψS⟩dynamics are classi- cally simulable and we can perform direct fidelity estimation efficiently. **Summary.** This paper introduces a novel benchmarking protocol for non-equilibrium quantum simulation using 'quantum many-body scars.' By restricting the simulation fidelity measurement to a special, classically simulable subspace, the authors create a quantifiable benchmark. This allows researchers to test if quantum hardware can simulate dynamics that are intractable for classical computers.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, mathematically rigorous framework for testing quantum advantage in dynamics by isolating measurable quantities (fidelity) that are sensitive to computational hardness.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors propose a scalable verification scheme for non-equilibrium quantum simulation to benchmark performance beyond classical computational limits.

**Main result.** They show that fidelity estimation within the specialized 'scar subspace' provides a quantifiable benchmark against the full Hilbert space, even under local depolarizing noise.

**Method.** The protocol uses Direct Fidelity Estimation (DFE) applied to states confined to a quantum many-body scar subspace, leveraging stabilizer properties for efficient measurement scaling.

**Model / system.** The study utilizes an Ising model on $n=2L$ qubits with periodic boundary conditions. The key feature is the existence of a polynomially large 'scar subspace' embedded in the exponentially large Hilbert space.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($F$), Stabilizer Rényi entropy ($\mathcal{M}_\alpha$), and expectation values of Pauli observables ($	ext{Tr}[ho W_k]$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Local depolarizing noise strength ($p$), system size ($L$ or $n$), and required measurement precision ($\epsilon, \delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dominant error is approximated by local depolarizing noise, and the method relies on the existence of a polynomially large scar subspace.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare fidelities in the full vs. scar subspaces under noise (Fig 2), demonstrate MCMC estimation accuracy independent of system size (Fig 3a), and show how shot counts affect statistical uncertainty estimates (Fig S4).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a three-part benchmarking protocol: defining the QMBS, showing DFE efficiency via stabilizer entropy bounds, and deriving quantitative relationships between scar and full-space fidelities under noise.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The applications for which quantum computers will clearly outperform classical computers are still being identified and benchmarking such an advantage is challenging. We propose a scalable verification scheme for non-equilibrium quantum simulation based on stabilizer scars, a special class of quantum many-body scars, whose structure ensures both classical simulability and efficient direct fidelity estimation. Assuming a physically motivated error model, we show that the fidelity of quantum simulating these states bounds the fidelity of classically intractable simulations, providing a benchmark for quantum-advantage experiments in non-equilibrium dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel pre-dissipative geometric mode-steering protocol to accelerate the relaxation of open quantum systems toward equilibrium. By strategically interleaving coherent rotations with nonselective measurements, the protocol suppresses coupling to slow Liouvillian modes. This suppression enables the realization of the quantum Mpemba effect in various qubit platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, experimentally implementable quantum control scheme (measurement-based) to overcome fundamental relaxation bottlenecks in open systems, which is highly relevant for quantum computing hardware characterization and optimization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research addresses how open quantum systems approach their steady state, specifically aiming to realize the quantum Mpemba effect where a state farther from equilibrium relaxes faster.

**Main result.** A pre-dissipative geometric mode-steering protocol was developed that suppresses overlap with slow Liouvillian modes, enabling accelerated relaxation dynamics in driven qubit and multiqubit systems.

**Method.** The core method involves interleaving coherent unitary evolution segments with nonselective projective measurements to geometrically steer the state's Bloch direction along geodesic paths before dissipation begins.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as an open quantum system governed by a Lindblad master equation, analyzed using generalized Bloch representations for qubit and multiqubit systems. The dynamics are characterized by Liouvillian operators and spectral gaps.

**Key observables.** Slow-mode Overlap Ratio ($R$), initial distance to stationarity ($\kappa_0$), trace distance $D_T(ho_t, ho_{ss})$, and the final excited-state population $	ilde{p}_e$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Mpemba regime requires two conditions: reduced slow-mode overlap ($|R|<1$) and a larger initial distance to stationarity ($\kappa_0 > 1$). The analysis covers both real and complex spectral gaps.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol is distinct from Zeno protocols as measurements are preparatory. The framework treats real and complex spectral gaps uniformly, though some derivations rely on approximations for the moderately driven regime.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometric steering sequence (Fig. 1a), Bloch trajectories (Fig. 1b), parameter dependence of the Mpemba regime in $(\Omega, \Delta)$ planes (Fig. S2), and time evolution of relaxation dynamics showing slow mode suppression (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem using the Lindblad master equation, details the pre-dissipative geometric steering protocol involving unitary steps and measurements, derives the necessary conditions ($|R|<1$ and $\kappa_0>1$), and demonstrates robust Mpemba acceleration across various qubit architectures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The slowest Liouvillian mode often bottlenecks the relaxation of an open quantum system to its steady state. Standard strategies circumvent this bottleneck by selecting special initial states or engineering the dissipator. Here we show that neither is necessary. We introduce a pre-dissipative geometric steering protocol that reshapes any given pure or mixed state before relaxation begins -- coherent rotations interleaved with nonselective projective measurements -- at fixed Lindblad generator. By steering the state's Bloch direction along geodesic paths, the protocol suppresses its overlap with the slowest Liouvillian modes. The prepared state then starts farther from equilibrium yet relaxes faster, realizing the quantum Mpemba effect, whenever two computable conditions hold: reduced slow-mode overlap and a larger initial distance to stationarity. Our framework treats real and complex spectral gaps uniformly, and we demonstrate robust Mpemba acceleration in driven qubit and multiqubit systems using operations available in trapped-ion and superconducting platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates an advanced technique to create multiple, independently controllable quantum simulation units (QSUs) using ultracold atomic gases. This multiplexed setup allows researchers to simultaneously study complex phenomena like the superfluid transition and Josephson junction dynamics across an array of junctions. It establishes a powerful tool for investigating transport physics in strongly correlated matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly versatile, scalable experimental platform for studying non-equilibrium dynamics in strongly correlated quantum matter, directly addressing fundamental questions about transport and phase transitions in mesoscopic arrays.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To study strongly correlated quantum systems, particularly dynamics and transport Hamiltonians, beyond classical computational reach.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated parallel analog quantum simulation in two settings: characterizing the superfluid transition across multiple units and simulating Josephson junctions with independent control over local parameters.

**Method.** They utilized a multiplexed configuration of ultracold atomic gases confined in configurable optical box potentials, allowing for independent manipulation of multiple quantum simulation units (QSUs).

**Model / system.** The platform uses homogeneous quantum gases (e.g., Fermi gas) to simulate models like the unitary Fermi gas and Josephson junctions. Control is achieved by shaping optical traps and controlling local cooling/phase.

**Key observables.** Density, Temperature ($T$), Superfluid fractions, population imbalance ($\Delta z$), and relative phase ($\Delta\phi$) measured via in situ imaging and time-of-flight interference.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Tunneling strength ($E_J$), charging energy ($E_C$), local temperature ($T/T_F$), and barrier height ($V_0/\mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The process assumes quasi-adiabatic evolution during trap reshaping, and the RF thermometry method supports the assumption of local thermal equilibrium.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup using DMDs for confinement, show density distribution verification across multiple units (multiplexing), detail local temperature control via RF spectroscopy, and map out Josephson junction dynamics showing frequency/damping evolution with barrier height.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the multiplexed QSU architecture, then demonstrating independent local control over temperature and superfluidity. Subsequently, it applies this framework to simulate coupled Josephson junctions, analyzing the crossover between weak-link and tunneling regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Analog quantum simulation offers a powerful way to study strongly correlated quantum systems that are beyond the reach of classical computation. In this context, ultracold atomic gases have been demonstrated to be an exceptionally versatile and well-controlled platform for implementing various quantum Hamiltonians. In this work, we extend this level of control to a multiplexed configuration in which distinct quantum-simulation units are independently controlled and engineered starting from a single atomic cloud. We demonstrate multiplexed operation in two representative settings. First, by shaping box-trap potentials and separately controlling the evaporative cooling trajectories, we prepare subsystems at various temperatures across the superfluid transition of the unitary Fermi gas. Second, we demonstrate parallel quantum simulation of the Josephson Hamiltonian across distinct Josephson-junction quantum simulation units with individually tunable parameters, including local phase control to initialize the dynamics. Our scheme provides a versatile route toward systematic studies of dynamics and transport Hamiltonians in strongly correlated ultracold matter. Moreover, it is readily extendable to a wide range of atomic species, geometries, and dimensionalities.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper models entanglement distribution across simulated and real quantum networks to build multipartite graph states. It rigorously tests the impact of noise sources, including depolarizing channels and classical communication delays, on network fidelity. The work is important because it demonstrates that hardware implementation details can drastically alter expected performance compared to idealized theoretical models.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides concrete benchmarks for resource estimation in distributed quantum computing architectures, which is crucial for theorists designing fault-tolerant protocols that must account for realistic physical constraints.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum computers can emulate complex quantum networks and assess their performance when subjected to practical noise sources like degraded entanglement and communication latency.

**Main result.** Hardware constraints lead to profound differences in results compared to simulations, highlighting that the choice of error model (e.g., QPD vs. Pauli) significantly impacts achievable network fidelity.

**Method.** The authors modeled imperfect Bell-pair sources using depolarizing noise and classical delays by implementing various depolarization channels (Stinespring dilation, random Pauli errors, QPD).

**Model / system.** The system involves emulating entanglement distribution networks to construct multipartite graph states, tested on superconducting quantum hardware (IQM Emerald) and in simulation.

**Key observables.** Bipartite-entanglement fidelity, stabilizer expectations for the resulting graph state, and the required network-distributed entanglement fidelity ($\ge 90\%$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Classical communication delay (tested up to 10 ns and conceptually up to tens of kilometers); initial ZALM source fidelity (e.g., 99.6%).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes mathematical equivalence between noise models but finds hardware implementation introduces discrepancies; rate investigation is left for future work.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the ring graph state structure and circuit, compare stabilizer values across different depolarization channels (simulation vs. hardware), show fidelity sweeps versus network-distributed entanglement, and map fidelity loss against classical delay.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the problem of noisy quantum networking, detailing multiple noise models for Bell pairs, presenting comparative results on graph state construction fidelity under these noises, and finally discussing the implications of hardware vs. simulation discrepancies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how quantum computers can be used to emulate quantum networks and study their performance under practical impairments. In particular, we evaluate how degraded entanglement and communication latency affect teleportation-based distributed multipartite-entanglement-state construction. We model imperfect Bell-pair sources using depolarizing noise channels and classical communication delays using thermal relaxation. We implement the depolarization using Stinespring dilation, randomly applied Pauli errors, and quasi-probability decompositions, evaluating the latter two on IQM quantum hardware and all three in simulation. We then study the performance of the entanglement distribution under noise generated by the aforementioned models. Although these noise models are mathematically equivalent, we find that hardware constraints result in profound differences in the corresponding results, highlighting the importance of careful experiment design.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents an experimental demonstration of a monolithic platform integrating superconducting microwave circuits with low-loss piezoelectric phonons using $	ext{AlScN}$ grown on $	ext{SiC}$. By selectively etching the material, they create a structure that supports both high-quality superconducting resonators and long-lived acoustic waveguides. This establishes a promising starting point for building complex quantum processors utilizing phonon mediation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the integration challenge in quantum hardware by proposing a material system ($	ext{AlScN/SiC}$) that allows for coexisting superconducting and phononic elements on one chip, which is crucial for realizing scalable hybrid quantum processors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to develop a scalable, monolithic platform that can efficiently couple superconducting microwave circuits with low-loss microwave phonons for quantum applications.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated the feasibility of an Al-on-SiC/AlScN-on-SiC architecture, achieving measurable lifetimes ($	ext{T}_1 \approx 2.9 \mu	ext{s}$, phonon $	au \approx 7.6 \mu	ext{s}$) and a coupling coefficient ($eta \approx 4.3\%$) suitable for hybrid quantum systems.

**Method.** The methodology involves fabricating superconducting resonators directly on exposed SiC regions while preserving adjacent AlScN-on-SiC regions for phonon transduction, followed by cryogenic characterization of both components.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a monolithic heterostructure combining aluminum superconducting circuits (CPW resonators) with piezoelectric Aluminum Scandium Nitride ($	ext{AlScN}$) grown on a Silicon Carbide ($	ext{SiC}$) substrate. The coupling mechanism relies on the piezoelectric effect to mediate interaction between microwave photons and acoustic phonons.

**Key observables.** Superconducting resonator coherent lifetime ($2.9 \mu	ext{s}$), low phononic propagation loss at $4.05 	ext{ GHz}$, estimated phonon lifetime ($7.6 \mu	ext{s}$), and electromechanical coupling coefficient ($\approx 4.3\%$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating frequency around $4.175 	ext{ GHz}$; coherence times in the microsecond regime; theoretical upper bound for $eta$ of $8\%$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that selective etching and material deposition can create a stable, low-loss interface supporting both superconducting metal films and piezoelectric phononic layers simultaneously.

**Figures summary.** Figure 5 conceptually illustrates the potential application of this platform for quantum acoustic circuits, such as Gaussian boson sampling, within the AlScN-on-SiC structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for a monolithic coupling platform, details the proposed $	ext{Al}$-on-$	ext{SiC}/	ext{AlScN}$-on-$	ext{SiC}$ architecture via selective etching, and presents characterization results showing compatible lifetimes and measurable electromechanical coupling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hybrid superconducting-phonon quantum processing is promising for cavity QED, measurement-based quantum computing, and other quantum applications. Relative to microwave photons at the same frequency, phonons can provide ultra-compact footprints, extremely low losses, and greater connectivity. Phonons can also couple strongly to superconducting circuits through the piezoelectric effect. However, this promise rests on scalable platforms that achieve these benefits without degrading superconducting circuit performance. This motivates a monolithic platform combining low phononic loss, strong electromechanical coupling, and superconducting-circuit compatibility without requiring suspended phononics. Here, we characterize a monolithic quantum acoustic platform combining aluminum superconducting circuits on exposed silicon carbide (SiC) with piezoelectric aluminum scandium nitride (AlScN) on SiC for integrated phononics. This architecture is enabled by selective removal of AlScN from selected chip regions, allowing aluminum superconducting microwave resonators to be fabricated directly on the SiC while preserving adjacent AlScN-on-SiC regions for phonon transduction. The resulting Al-on-SiC resonators exhibit a coherent lifetime of 2.9 μs, demonstrating compatibility with aluminum superconducting quantum devices. In parallel, cryogenic surface acoustic delay-line measurements on the retained AlScN-on-SiC regions show low phononic propagation loss at 4.05 GHz, corresponding to an estimated phonon lifetime of 7.6 μs. Together with a previously demonstrated electromechanical coupling coefficient of about 4.3% and a theoretical upper bound of 8%, these results establish Al-on-SiC/AlScN-on-SiC as a promising monolithic platform for integrating superconducting microwave circuits with piezoelectric phononic components for quantum acoustic networking and hybrid quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates whether 'No Signaling in Time' (NSIT), which requires that outcome-erased measurements leave marginal statistics unchanged, implies a classical temporal history. By analyzing the Common Fixed Points of a qutrit ring system, the authors prove that NSIT is merely a statement about marginal signal erasure and does not preclude violations of macrorealist inequalities like the Leggett–Garg inequality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical tool to distinguish between purely marginal constraints (like NSIT) and deeper assumptions about quantum reality, such as macrorealism or noninvasive measurement.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the interpretational ambiguity of No-Signaling-in-Time (NSIT): whether satisfying NSIT implies noninvasive measurability and thus a classical temporal history.

**Main result.** They show that exact pairwise NSIT is insufficient to guarantee a classical temporal history, as states satisfying NSIT can still violate Leggett–Garg inequalities (LGI).

**Method.** The authors analyze the Common Fixed Points (CFPs) of nonselective measurement channels on a specific quantum system and test these points against LGI violations using various diagnostics.

**Model / system.** The model is a qutrit ring subject to local degenerate Lüders measurements, analyzed by considering the full manifold of CFPs derived from time evolution and measurement processes.

**Key observables.** Pairwise NSIT ($\eta_{	ext{NSIT}}$), Leggett–Garg inequality violation strength ($K_1$), Hidden-Variable Negativity (NHV), and Branch Displacement ($B_\lambda$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parameter $\lambda$ characterizing the state on the CFP manifold, and time intervals for sequential measurements.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on a specific protocol involving equal spacing of measurement times; the conclusion is limited to pairwise NSIT conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the analytic separation on the CFP manifold showing LGI margins vs. $\lambda$, and show NHV/entropic LGI violation strength over the CFP-time grid, confirming violations for non-trivial states.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes NSIT via CFPs, then analyzes the resulting state manifold ($\hat{ho}^*(\lambda)$). It demonstrates that while all $\lambda>0$ satisfy NSIT, they violate LGI, contrasting this with the unique classical point at $\lambda=0$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

No signaling in time (NSIT) has often been treated as the clean operational remnant of noninvasive measurability. If an earlier measurement leaves every later marginal unchanged, the temporal process appears classical. We show that this inference is false. We introduce common fixed points (CFPs) of nonselective measurement channels as an exact mechanism that erases all marginal evidence of invasiveness while preserving disturbed outcome conditioned branches. For a qutrit ring subject to a local degenerate Luders measurement, we solve the full CFP manifold analytically. Every state on this manifold satisfies exact pairwise NSIT, yet every nontrivial member violates a Leggett Garg inequality, including the maximally mixed state. The violation is governed by finite branch displacement, not by residual signaling. Hidden variable reconstruction, entropic witnesses, protocol landscape scans, noise robustness, and finite shot simulations show that exact NSIT certifies only the disappearance of marginal signals after outcome erasure, not the existence of a classical temporal history.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper experimentally validates a generalized Variational Quantum Linear Solver (VQLS) using a photonic platform to solve linear equations over both complex numbers and the finite field $\mathbb{F}_2$. The authors successfully tackle practical challenges, such as system singularity in the complex domain via regularization, while developing a robust modulo-2 approach for applications like stabilizer coding. This work establishes an experimental foundation for applying VQLS in real quantum computing scenarios.

**Why it may be interesting.** The direct experimental implementation of quantum algorithms like VQLS on photonic hardware provides crucial physical validation. The extension to finite fields ($\mathbb{F}_2$) is particularly relevant for applications in coding theory and quantum computation over non-real fields.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of solving linear equation systems ($A|	ilde{x}angle = |	ilde{b}angle$) in both the complex field and the finite field $\mathbb{F}_2$. It aims to develop a robust Variational Quantum Linear Solver (VQLS) applicable across different mathematical domains.

**Main result.** The VQLS was experimentally validated on a photonic platform for 4D systems, successfully handling non-singular cases in the complex field and demonstrating inherent immunity to singularity issues when adapted for $\mathbb{F}_2$.

**Method.** The core method involves variational optimization using a cost function minimized via gradient descent. For ill-conditioned problems, Tikhonov regularization is introduced; for $\mathbb{F}_2$, the cost function is redesigned using modulo 2 arithmetic.

**Model / system.** The work utilizes a photonic computing platform employing telecom-band photon pairs encoded in polarization to implement quantum gates (e.g., CNOT). The mathematical models involve solving linear systems over $\mathbb{C}$ and $\mathbb{F}_2$.

**Key observables.** Deviation $D = ||A |	ilde{x}angle - |	ilde{b}angle||^2$, minimum cost value, and the convergence of the trial state $|	ilde{\mathbf{x}}angle$ to the expected solution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The regularization parameter $\delta$ (for Tikhonov), the field type ($\mathbb{C}$ or $\mathbb{F}_2$), and system dimensions (e.g., 4D systems).

**Assumptions / limitations.** In the complex case, singularity requires regularization; in $\mathbb{F}_2$, constraints are necessary because minimizing cost alone is insufficient due to indeterminate phases.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 details the experimental setup using photonic components. Figure 2 illustrates VQLS performance for non-singular, singular, and no-solution cases in the complex field, showing regularization effects. Figure 3 demonstrates the successful application of Modulo 2 VQLS.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the VQLS framework, first applying it to solve linear systems over the complex field (addressing singularity via Tikhonov regularization). It then extends and adapts this methodology for the finite field $\mathbb{F}_2$, concluding with a demonstration of its practical potential across both domains.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Based on a photonic computing platform, we experimentally validate a generalized variational quantum linear solver (VQLS) by systematically solving four-dimensional linear equation systems across different fields. In the complex field, in addition to solving non-singular systems that admit a unique solution, we investigate ill-conditioned problems arising from singularity--an issue frequently encountered in practical applications. To tackle these challenges, we introduce perturbation terms, a treatment inspired by Tikhonov regularization, and develop an algorithm capable of handling a wide range of systems. Furthermore, we extend the VQLS to the finite field F2 by redesigning the cost function to incorporate modulo 2 and imposing several constraints on the solution vector. This modulo 2 VQLS is inherently free from singularity. It is adapt to stabilizer coding theory and may find applications in areas such as decoders and the design of quantum gate sequences. Therefore, our work demonstrates the practical potential of VQLS in quantum computing, providing a solid experimental foundation and methodological guidance for its real-world applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a geometric framework using weighted hypergraphs to analyze fermion-to-qubit mappings. It shows that these encodings encode more than just spectral information by defining measurable geometric structures. By comparing connectivity metrics (BK) and transport measures (XBK), the authors provide a universal tool to track structural changes in complex quantum Hamiltonians like the Hubbard model.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides powerful, non-spectral diagnostic tools (hypergraph geometry) to analyze how quantum information encoding schemes map physical interactions into structural constraints, which is highly relevant for understanding complex many-body physics simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if fermion-to-qubit encodings reveal physical structure beyond merely reproducing the spectrum of the original many-body Hamiltonian.

**Main result.** The framework establishes hypergraph geometry as a general tool, showing that connectivity and transport metrics consistently capture the structural evolution of encoded Hamiltonians across diverse correlated systems.

**Method.** Developing a geometric framework using weighted hypergraphs to analyze two complementary views: spectral connectivity (via graph Laplacians in BK encoding) and coupling-space reorganization (via optimal transport in XBK encoding).

**Model / system.** The analysis applies to general interacting fermionic Hamiltonians, exemplified by the Hubbard model, spinless tV chain, single-impurity Anderson model, and Kitaev chain.

**Key observables.** Geometric observables like $ho(U) = \lambda_2(L_{hop})/\lambda_2(L_{int})$ (BK) and Wasserstein distance $W(\mu_1, \mu_2)$ (XBK).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U$), hopping parameters ($t$), and the characteristic crossover scale $U^*$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the hypergraph to an equivalent weighted graph using standard clique expansion, and that geometric observables capture physical reorganization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structural evolution across multiple correlated models, showing consistent signatures of interaction-driven reorganization via spectral observables (BK) and distance comparisons.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a general hypergraph geometry framework, applies it to specific encodings (BK and XBK), derives key geometric observables that quantify connectivity changes, and demonstrates the consistency of these findings across several canonical strongly correlated models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exact fermion to qubit transformations are conventionally regarded as algorithmic tools that translate many-body Hamiltonians into qubit representations for quantum simulation. Here we show that they also define intrinsic geometric representations whose structure encodes physically meaningful information beyond spectral equivalence. We develop a geometric framework based on weighted hypergraphs and coupling space representations constructed from the Bravyi--Kitaev (BK) and Xia--Bian--Kais (XBK) encodings. Within the BK representation, we introduce a geometric observable that compares the algebraic connectivities of the kinetic and interaction hypergraphs, derive its exact analytical dependence on interaction strength, and uncover two geometric universality classes together with an exact spectral organization originating from the binary tree architecture of the encoding. The complementary XBK representation describes the evolution of encoded Hamiltonians through probability measures in coupling space, where optimal transport quantifies interaction-driven reorganization independently of the spectral analysis. Applications to the Hubbard, spinless tV , single impurity Anderson, and Kitaev models demonstrate that these connectivity and transport based geometric descriptions consistently capture the structural evolution of encoded quantum Hamiltonians across distinct classes of many-body systems. Our results establish hypergraph geometry as a new framework for understanding fermion-to-qubit encodings,revealing that they serve not only as computational mappings but also as geometric representations of quantum many-body Hamiltonians.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate that the dynamics of vortices in a spherical superfluid naturally generate an effective magnetic monopole field, confirming the system's utility for studying Dirac-monopole physics. They analyze vortex motion analogous to charged particles in this field and further study the instability growth of related vortex structures. This establishes spherical superfluids as a versatile platform for exploring fundamental topological quantum phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, controllable quantum fluid realization of fundamental topological physics (monopoles), linking superfluid dynamics directly to concepts from high-energy theory like Dirac quantization and geometric phases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a unified framework realizing the Dirac-monopole paradigm using controllable quantum fluid systems, and to study vortex instabilities related to this structure.

**Main result.** The dynamics of massive vortices in a spherical superfluid naturally generate an effective gauge potential equivalent to that of a magnetic monopole, and the system exhibits predicted cyclotron-like motion and subsequent instability growth rates.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical derivations (effective Lagrangian, normal-mode analysis) with numerical simulations using the Gross-Pitaevskii equation on a spherical geometry.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is an immiscible binary Bose–Einstein condensate confined to a thin spherical shell. The model incorporates vortex interactions and derives effective gauge fields leading to monopole dynamics.

**Key observables.** Cyclotron frequency ($\omega_c$), instability growth rate ($\sigma^*$), superfluid density distributions, and the emergent magnetic field ($B_j$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superfluid density ($ho_a, ho_b$), vortex charge quantization ($q_j g = n\hbar/2$), and effective mass ($M_b$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the immiscible regime for the condensate and relies on mapping dynamics to an effective gauge structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the analogy between vortex dipoles and charged particles in a monopole field, show dipole trajectories, and plot the exponential growth of vortex necklace amplitudes over time.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the monopole emergence from vortex dynamics on a sphere, then analyzes the resulting cyclotron motion via normal modes and GPE simulations. Finally, it investigates the instability of an equatorial vortex necklace configuration related to Wu-Yang gauge patching.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic monopoles lie at the crossroads of gauge fields, topology, geometric phases, and charge quantization, yet they remain elusive as fundamental particles. Here we show that an emergent Dirac-monopole framework arises naturally from the dynamics of massive quantum vortices in a spherical superfluid shell. Their dynamics is formally equivalent to that of interacting charged particles constrained to a sphere in the field of a magnetic monopole. The monopole charge is fixed by the superfluid density and automatically satisfies Dirac's quantization condition. The emergent monopole description predicts cyclotron-like vortex motion, in quantitative agreement with Gross--Pitaevskii simulations. We further show that topological frustration induced by two like-charged polar vortices gives rise to the formation of an equatorial vortex necklace, a configuration reminiscent of the polygonal cyclone clusters observed around Jupiter's poles, before its subsequent breakup through a Kelvin--Helmholtz-like instability. Within this framework, the vortex necklace may be viewed as a quantized analogue of Wu--Yang gauge patching. Our results establish spherical superfluids as a versatile platform for realizing and exploring fundamental aspects of Dirac-monopole physics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the fate of tripartite quantum correlations—including entanglement and nonlocality—in a mixed state situated in the vicinity of a black hole. By incorporating the local Hawking temperature, the authors demonstrate that while all three resources are sensitive to the near-horizon region, genuine multipartite nonlocality is particularly fragile against increasing local thermal effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a theoretical framework for understanding how fundamental quantum correlations are modified by extreme gravitational environments, offering insights into information loss mechanisms beyond standard Hawking radiation calculations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum state texture, genuine multipartite entanglement, and tripartite nonlocality are affected by the local Hawking effect in a black hole's quantum atmosphere.

**Main result.** All three quantum resources show their extrema tracking the same near-horizon region, but tripartite nonlocality is found to be significantly more fragile than entanglement under stronger local Hawking effects.

**Method.** The authors use Bogoliubov transformations and partial tracing techniques to derive physically accessible reduced density matrices for a tripartite mixed state. They quantify correlations using quantum state texture, genuine multipartite concurrence, and the Svetlichny inequality.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a tripartite mixed state ($ho_{ABC}$) near a Schwarzschild black hole horizon, incorporating the Hartle-Hawking local temperature ($T_{HH}$). The analysis focuses on the physically accessible reduced state $ho_{ABICI}$.

**Key observables.** Quantum state texture $R(ho)$, Genuine multipartite entanglement (using CGM), and Tripartite nonlocality (violation of Svetlichny inequality, $S > 4$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Local Hawking temperature ($T_{HH}$), mixing parameter $p$, and the black hole characteristic scale factor $D_{HH}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a specific X-state structure for the density matrix and models the environment using the Hartle-Hawking vacuum approach.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the radial dependence of quantum state texture, CGM, and Svetlichny value ($S$) near the horizon, showing how these quantities track $T_{HH}(r)$ and are suppressed by increasing $D_{HH}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes three distinct measures of quantum correlation (texture, entanglement, nonlocality) across varying radial positions and environmental parameters, culminating in a unified characterization of resource redistribution.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate quantum state texture, genuine multipartite entanglement, and tripartite nonlocality of a tripartite mixed state in the black hole quantum atmosphere. By introducing the Hartle-Hawking local temperature into the Bogoliubov coefficients, we characterize the influence of the local Hawking effect on both physically accessible and inaccessible reduced states. We find that the extrema of these three quantities all lie in the same near horizon region and shift outward with increasing local Hawking temperature, coinciding with the peak region of the local Hawking temperature and indicating that different aspects of tripartite quantum information are most sensitive to the local Hawking effect in the same atmospheric region. In contrast to genuine multipartite entanglement, tripartite nonlocality is more fragile and is suppressed under stronger local Hawking effects. These results provide a unified characterization of density matrix restructuring, entanglement redistribution, and nonlocality in tripartite mixed states affected by the black hole quantum atmosphere.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an efficient quantum adiabatic algorithm to find the ground energy of specific Heisenberg spin systems. It achieves this by proving a novel mathematical inequality that links the spectral gap of these Hamiltonians to their Lee-Yang radius. This work provides a powerful theoretical framework connecting complex analysis tools to fundamental problems in quantum many-body physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection drawn between algebraic properties (Lee-Yang radii) and physical observables (spectral gaps/ground state energy) provides a powerful, non-standard mathematical tool for analyzing hard problems in condensed matter physics, which is highly relevant for understanding computational complexity bounds.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing an efficient quantum algorithm to compute the ground energy of specific, complex Heisenberg spin systems.

**Main result.** The authors prove that for a broad family of Suzuki-Fisher type Hamiltonians, the spectral gap between the first excited and ground state is lower bounded by $2\mu$, enabling an efficient adiabatic quantum computation approach.

**Method.** The proof combines techniques from complex analysis (using Lee-Yang polynomials) to establish a quantitative relationship between the relative spectral gap of an operator and its associated Lee-Yang radius. This mathematical tool is then applied to bound the energy gap for the physical Hamiltonians.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on a family of Heisenberg-type quantum spin systems, including the antiferromagnet on bipartite graphs, governed by anisotropic Heisenberg Hamiltonians (Suzuki-Fisher type). The analysis uses concepts derived from Lee-Yang theory applied to these many-body operators.

**Key observables.** Ground state energy, spectral gap ($\Delta E$), and the relative spectral gap $(\lambda_1 - \lambda_2)/\lambda_1$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The magnetic field strength $\mu$ (specifically $\min \mu_{z,i}$), and the Lee-Yang radius $r$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the operator being positive semidefinite and belonging to a specific class defined by its generating polynomial's zero-freeness property (Lee-Yang tensors).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes a general mathematical inequality relating the spectral gap of a positive semidefinite operator $K$ to its Lee-Yang radius ($r$). This result is then specialized and applied to prove a lower bound on the spectral gap for Hamiltonians in the Suzuki-Fisher family, leading directly to the construction of an efficient quantum adiabatic algorithm.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider a broad family of Heisenberg-type quantum spin systems that were studied by Suzuki and Fisher in 1971. This family includes, for example, the Heisenberg antiferromagnet on any bipartite graph. The ground and Gibbs states of these models are Lee-Yang tensors with radius 1: they are associated with multilinear polynomials that possess an extraordinary zero-freeness property inside the unit polydisk in the complex plane. For each Hamiltonian in this family we show that the spectral gap between the first-excited and ground-state energies is lower bounded by $2μ$, where $μ$ is the magnetic field along the $Z$ direction. Using this result we obtain an efficient quantum adiabatic algorithm for the ground energy of any model in this family. The proof is based on a new inequality that relates the spectral gap of a positive semidefinite operator to its Lee-Yang radius -- a quantitative strengthening of prior work of the authors that may find applications elsewhere.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces novel quantum circuit schemes to directly measure bipartite concurrence and tripartite 3-tangle for pure states. By mapping the analytical definitions of these entanglement measures onto measurable output probabilities using unitary transformations and ancilla qubits, it offers an efficient alternative to resource-heavy full state tomography. This provides a practical technical roadmap for experimental characterization of multipartite quantum resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant because it provides concrete, circuit-level blueprints for experimentally verifying fundamental quantum resources like entanglement, which is crucial for advancing quantum computation and communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing efficient, direct measurement schemes to quantify quantum entanglement (specifically bipartite concurrence and tripartite 3-tangle) for pure states without resorting to full quantum state tomography.

**Main result.** The authors propose a set of quantum circuit protocols that successfully map the analytical expressions for these two key entanglement measures directly onto measurable probabilities from output quantum circuits.

**Method.** The method relies on constructing specific quantum circuits involving unitary transformations and measurements on auxiliary qubits. This maps complex mathematical formulas into observable measurement outcomes, bypassing resource-intensive full state tomography.

**Model / system.** The focus is on two-qubit and three-qubit pure quantum states ($|\psi_2angle$ and $|\psi_3angle$). The protocols are designed to be implementable using standard quantum gates like Toffoli gates, suggesting applicability across platforms like superconducting circuits or trapped ions.

**Key observables.** Bipartite Concurrence ($C_\psi$) for two qubits and the tripartite 3-tangle ($	au_{123}$) for three qubits. These are extracted as measurement probabilities from the quantum circuit output.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The entanglement measures themselves (Concurrence $\in [0, 1]$, 3-tangle $\in [0, 1]$). The efficiency gain is measured against the exponential resource overhead of full state tomography.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes the availability and reliable implementation of necessary quantum gates, such as three-qubit Toffoli gates, on current or near-future quantum hardware platforms.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the quantum circuit diagram for two-qubit entanglement detection, while Figure 2 details the multi-step quantum circuit required for three-qubit entanglement detection.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops protocols: first addressing the general need to bypass tomography; then detailing the specific unitary and measurement sequence for bipartite concurrence (2-qubits); finally extending this methodology through a complex, multi-stage protocol for tripartite 3-tangle (3-qubits).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum entanglement is the fundamental hallmark of quantum mechanics and a core resource for realizing long-distance quantum communication and scalable linear quantum computing. Accordingly, the precise detection and quantitative quantification of entanglement constitute a foundational and critical problem in quantum information theory. To date, researchers have proposed numerous sufficient conditions for entanglement detection as well as a variety of entanglement measures to characterize the entanglement strength of quantum states; nevertheless, efficient and direct measurement schemes for core entanglement parameters remain underdeveloped. Based on unitary transformations and auxiliary measurements, this paper proposes a set of quantum circuit schemes capable of directly measuring the bipartite concurrence and the tripartite 3-tangle entanglement measure. By introducing auxiliary qubits and constructing specific controlled unitary operations, the proposed scheme maps the analytical expressions of the two entanglement measures onto the measurement probabilities of output states from quantum circuits. It enables efficient and direct quantitative measurement of bipartite and tripartite entanglement without performing full quantum state tomography. This work provides a feasible technical route for the experimental characterization of entanglement properties and lays a groundwork for the practical deployment of multipartite entanglement resources in quantum information processing.

</details>

**Type:** experiment · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.14293v1>

**Summary.** The paper presents a Convolutional Neural Network (DCCNN) designed for real-time, online detection of charge jumps in superconducting qubits caused by cosmic radiation. By deploying this model onto FPGA hardware, the authors successfully transitioned error detection from a slow diagnostic tool to a fast control-loop primitive, enabling adaptive quantum error mitigation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical engineering challenge of integrating advanced ML diagnostics into quantum hardware control loops, moving error detection from a post-processing step to an active component of quantum computation protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a real-time method to detect charge jumps in superconducting qubits, which are induced by ionizing radiation and cause correlated errors.

**Main result.** A Dilated Causal CNN (DCCNN) was developed that achieves detection efficiency comparable to established offline methods while operating with fixed, low latency suitable for direct integration into quantum control loops.

**Method.** The authors trained a DCCNN on synthetic Ramsey tomography scans and deployed it onto an FPGA platform using quantization-aware training for high-speed inference.

**Model / system.** The system involves superconducting qubits monitored via charge-sweep scans, utilizing the QICK hardware platform. The detection mechanism relies on classifying time-series data from these scans to identify abrupt changes in the qubit's offset charge.

**Key observables.** Detection efficiency (e.g., $0.843 \pm 0.022$ vs. $\chi^2$'s $0.866 \pm 0.020$), inference latency ($6.19\ \mu	ext{s}$), and classification metrics like Precision, Recall, and F1 score.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Charge jump magnitude $|\Delta q| \in [0.1, 0.5] e$; the target deployment platform is the Zynq UltraScale+ RFSoC ZCU216 FPGA.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The DCCNN architecture is assumed to maintain performance parity with the $\chi^2$ algorithm while providing a fixed-latency forward pass, which is crucial for in-situ control.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the TCN and DCCNN architectures, show example synthetic Ramsey scans (with/without jumps), and tables compare detection efficiencies across different qubits and hardware resource utilization.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of real-time error detection in superconducting qubits. It details the proposed DCCNN architecture over traditional methods ($\chi^2$), presents training procedures using simulated data, and concludes with performance benchmarks demonstrating its viability for control-loop deployment on FPGA hardware.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ionizing radiation from cosmic rays and gammas can induce discontinuous jumps in the environmental charge of superconducting qubits (charge jumps), causing correlated errors that challenge fault-tolerant quantum computing while simultaneously providing a detection signature for quantum sensing applications. Current detection methods operate offline, introducing latency incompatible with in-the-loop qubit control. In this paper, an online detector of charge jumps for superconducting qubits, based on a dilated causal convolutional neural network (DCCNN) designed for in-the-loop deployment on the Quantum Instrumentation Control Kit (QICK) platform, is presented. The network is trained on synthetic Ramsey tomography scans generated from qubit templates measured at the Northwestern Experimental Underground Site (NEXUS) at Fermilab, and translated to FPGA firmware via hls4ml with ap_fixed$\langle 16,6 \rangle$ quantization, reaching a per-inference latency of $6.19 μ$s on the Zynq UltraScale+ RFSoC ZCU216. At this operating point the DCCNN matches the detection efficiency of the established offline $χ^2$ algorithm ($0.843 \pm 0.022$ vs. $0.866 \pm 0.020$ on $|Δq| \in [0.1, 0.5] e$ at matched false-positive rate), while requiring no per-qubit hyperparameter tuning. This shifts charge-jump detection from a post-hoc diagnostic to a control-loop primitive, enabling adaptive protocols that respond to radiation-induced events in situ, with applications to quantum-computing error mitigation and to the use of superconducting qubits as particle detectors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops 'binary Gauss stabilizers' as an alternative mathematical tool to the standard Gauss operators used to define the physical subspace of Abelian lattice gauge theories. This novel set of stabilizers allows researchers to construct practical quantum error-correcting codes directly from the system's inherent gauge symmetries. The work provides new tools for both understanding lattice gauge theory and advancing quantum simulation techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** The direct link between gauge symmetries in lattice field theory and stabilizer codes provides a powerful framework for designing fault-tolerant quantum simulations of fundamental physics models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To find an alternative set of stabilizers for Abelian lattice gauge theories that can be used to build practical quantum error-correcting codes without adding extra qubits, and to develop a new strategy for gauge fixing.

**Main result.** The authors introduce 'binary Gauss stabilizers,' an alternative stabilizer group to the standard Gauss operators, which allows for constructing error-correcting codes exploiting gauge symmetries.

**Method.** The work involves deriving these alternative stabilizers by analyzing the constraints defining the physical Hilbert space and translating them into bitwise equations suitable for quantum circuit representation.

**Model / system.** The system is a discrete Abelian lattice gauge theory described by a Z_N gauge group on a hypercubic lattice, coupled with fermionic matter. The Hamiltonian structure involves material, hopping, electric, and plaquette terms.

**Key observables.** Dimension (D) of the physical Hilbert space; the set of Pauli stabilizers defining the gauge-invariant subspace.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The gauge group order N (specifically Z_{2\eta}); lattice dimension d.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis initially focuses on 1D and 2D lattices, with generalizations to higher dimensions left for future work. The derivation relies heavily on specific circuit identities for Pauli operator conjugation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate notation (controlled-Z, Toffoli), qubit decompositions for operators on links (e.g., Z_8 theory), and the structure of the derived binary Gauss stabilizers in 1D and 2D.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem by comparing standard Gauss constraints to alternative stabilizer methods. It then derives these 'binary Gauss stabilizers' for 1D and 2D systems, showing their equivalence to the physical subspace defined by Gauss operators. Finally, it details the explicit construction of these generators using quantum circuit identities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gauge theories and quantum error-correcting codes share the same underlying structure: both use constraints to identify a specific subspace of the full Hilbert space. In quantum error correction, these constraints are known as stabilizers, while in gauge theories they correspond to Gauss law. In this work, we consider a family of discrete Abelian lattice gauge theories described by a $\mathbb{Z}_{N}$ gauge group with $N$ an arbitrary power of two. In this setting, we find a set of stabilizers for the gauge-invariant subspace which is an alternative to the Gauss operators, and we call them binary Gauss stabilizers. We use this alternative stabilizer group to build practical error-correcting codes exploiting the gauge symmetries of the system without the addition of extra qubits. The applications of our finding are not limited to error correction though. We also provide a new strategy of gauge fixing to remove the redundancies based on our alternative stabilizer, which might provide advantages with respect to already-existing approaches such as the axial gauge. Our results provide new tools to study lattice gauge theories and their quantum simulation, and opens directions for future work at the interface of lattice gauge theory and quantum information.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates the energy spectra of interacting spinless electrons on finite molecular rings using a Hubbard model. It leverages group theory and symmetry analysis to predict spectral features, such as avoided crossings due to magnetic flux and the protection of certain states by particle-hole symmetry at half-filling.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for understanding how fundamental symmetries dictate electronic behavior in low-dimensional quantum materials, which is highly relevant to designing correlated quantum devices or simulating molecular electronics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how lattice geometry, Coulomb interactions, and discrete symmetries govern the many-body energy spectrum of interacting spinless electrons on molecular ring networks.

**Main result.** The analysis reveals qualitative differences in spectra based on whether the number of particles is even or odd, and demonstrates that particle-hole symmetry protects certain spectral sectors from Zeeman splitting at half-filling.

**Method.** Group-theoretical methods are employed to analyze symmetries (using $C_L$ and $D_L$), leading to state classification into irreducible representations. The spectrum is calculated using a tight-binding Hubbard Hamiltonian, often requiring the construction of effective Hamiltonians for coupled symmetry sectors.

**Model / system.** The system models interacting spinless electrons on finite molecular ring networks (size L), governed by a tight-binding Hubbard Hamiltonian incorporating hopping ($t_{st}$), on-site repulsion ($U$), and two-body interactions ($V_{st}$). The effects of an external magnetic flux ($\Phi$) are included via Peierls substitution.

**Key observables.** Many-body spectrum (energy eigenvalues), persistent current, magnetization, and the splitting/avoided crossing patterns as a function of magnetic flux $\Phi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice size L ($3 \le L \le 6$), number of electrons N (1, 2, or 3), external magnetic flux $\Phi$, and interaction strengths $U$ and $V_{st}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes spinless fermions obeying the Pauli principle. The use of group theory heavily constrains the possible spectral degeneracies and splittings.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate typical features of ring networks, Zeeman spectra for small rings ($L=3$), flux-dependent energy levels showing avoided crossings, and scaling plots comparing Coulomb energy across different lattice geometries.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Hamiltonian using group theory to classify states. It then examines how external fields (Zeeman effect) and interactions cause spectral changes, paying special attention to symmetry protection mechanisms like particle-hole duality at half-filling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate interacting spinless electrons on finite molecular ring networks described by a tight-binding Hubbard Hamiltonian. The interplay between lattice geometry, Coulomb interactions and discrete symmetries is analysed for rings with $L=3,4,5,6$ nodes, filled with one, two or three electrons. Special attention is devoted to the role of the network symmetries in determining the structure of the many-body spectrum and the Mulliken classification of the eigenstates. Using group-theoretical methods, we examine the evolution of the spectra in the presence of an external magnetic flux. The Zeeman effect lifts degeneracies and results in combination with the Coulomb interaction to avoided crossings in symmetry sectors. We identify a qualitatively distinction between systems with an even and odd number of particles. At half-filling, particle-hole symmetry (duality) protects selected symmetry sectors against Zeeman splitting.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors derive operational limits for broadcasting multipartite entanglement (GME) using local beam-splitter cloning. They prove a strong no-go theorem showing that it is impossible to simultaneously certify both resulting broadcast copies as genuinely entangled using standard fidelity witnesses. This work delineates fundamental trade-offs between the physical distribution and verifiable certification of quantum entanglement.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides quantitative, fundamental bounds on how entanglement can be distributed or copied in realistic quantum network settings, directly impacting the feasibility assessment of quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes operational limits on broadcasting Genuine Multipartite Entanglement (GME) across quantum networks and investigates the possibility of simultaneously certifying multiple broadcast copies.

**Main result.** A fundamental no-go result is proven: for any reflectivity $R$, the two resulting broadcast copies cannot be simultaneously certified as genuinely multipartite entangled using standard fidelity-based witnesses.

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical derivations based on local optimal 1 $\to$ 2 cloning implemented via beam-splitter interactions, calculating entanglement fidelity decay rates for GHZ and W states.

**Model / system.** The model is an N-partite quantum system where GME states (GHZ, W) are subjected to a broadcasting protocol involving local beam-splitter operations at each party.

**Key observables.** Broadcast fidelity ($F$), entanglement witness thresholds ($eta$), and the exponential decay factor $[c]^N$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Reflectivity $R$ (ranging from 0 to 1), system size $N$, and specific state families (GHZ, W).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are strictly limited to the framework of fidelity-based entanglement witnesses and the specific local beam-splitter cloning protocol used.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the N-party GME broadcasting setup and show that for various $R$, the calculated fidelities for both broadcast copies fall below the required GME certification thresholds.

**Paper structure.** The paper first derives the effective transformation from local beam-splitter interactions, calculates the fidelity decay rates for GHZ and W states, and then presents the core argument proving that no single reflectivity $R$ allows both resulting copies to pass the simultaneous GME certification test.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish operational limits on the broadcasting of genuine multipartite entanglement (GME) in quantum networks. Using a distributed protocol in which each of N parties locally implements optimal 1 $\to$ 2 cloning via beam-splitter interactions, we derive exact expressions for the broadcast fidelity of Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ), W states, and Cluster states in a limited setting. We show that the fidelity decays exponentially with system size as [c(R)]$^N$, providing a quantitative expression of multipartite entanglement monogamy in the broadcasting setting, and that both state families share a universal normalisation factor arising from independent post-selection probabilities. Most significantly, we prove a no-go result for simultaneous GME certification: for all reflectivities and all system sizes, the two broadcast copies cannot be simultaneously certified as genuinely multipartite entangled within the standard framework of fidelity-based witnesses. We further check this behaviour for three- and four-party cluster states, finding consistent results that support the generality of the no-go beyond the GHZ and W families. This obstruction arises from the redistribution of multipartite coherence, which both reduces the achievable fidelity and increases the corresponding certification threshold. Our results reveal a fundamental trade-off between the broadcastability of multipartite entanglement and its operational certifiability, and delineate intrinsic limits on entanglement distribution in quantum networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a unified spectral theory explaining that computational slowdown in constrained quantum optimization stems from entanglement restructuring. It shows that by designing dynamics that respect the algebraic structure of the constraints, one can avoid unnecessary entanglement changes, leading to superior performance over standard penalty methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep theoretical link between the geometric structure of mathematical constraints (polyhedra) and fundamental quantum information measures (entanglement), which is highly relevant for understanding resource scaling in quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a unified theoretical framework for quantum optimization algorithms applied to constrained combinatorial problems.

**Main result.** The computational slowdown in these quantum processes is fundamentally driven by the restructuring of entanglement, which can be mitigated using constraint-aware dynamics.

**Method.** The authors develop a spectral theory connecting constrained optimization structures (like LPs and MIPs) to entanglement dynamics within Hamiltonian evolution frameworks.

**Model / system.** The model involves mapping combinatorial problems onto Hamiltonians ($H_p$) evolving under time-dependent mixers ($H(t)$). The system is analyzed across both continuous-time adiabatic and discrete circuit-based quantum paradigms.

**Key observables.** Entanglement restructuring, spectral gaps, and the overlap between the final state and the optimal solution's basis state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree of entanglement change required by the constraints; the time evolution schedule $s(t)$ in adiabatic processes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that computational complexity is dictated by how much entanglement must be changed to satisfy problem constraints, contrasting with generic penalty-based approaches.

**Figures summary.** Figure 9(a) illustrates the eigenvalue trajectory descending through penalized suboptimal levels during adiabatic evolution for an exact cover problem.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from reviewing classical constrained optimization (LP/MIP), establishing a general Hamiltonian framework, deriving that entanglement restructuring drives slowdown, and finally proposing constraint-aware dynamics to control this restructuring.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Constrained combinatorial optimization underlies many industrial and technological decision problems. We develop a spectral theory that unifies many quantum optimization algorithms. We show that computational slowdown is driven by entanglement restructuring: the creation, redistribution, and destruction of entanglement during system evolution. The severity of the slowdown depends on how much entanglement must be changed. We show that algebraic properties of constraints induce such restructuring, and that constraint-aware dynamics reduce the associated slowdown by avoiding unnecessary restructuring. This framework explains why constraint-aware quantum methods can outperform generic penalty-based approaches. The theory connects constrained optimization, computational complexity, entanglement dynamics, and Hamiltonian spectral structure across continuous-time and circuit-based quantum optimization paradigms.

</details>
