**Summary.** This paper investigates the impact of timing inaccuracies, or scheduling errors, on Dynamical Decoupling sequences used to protect qubits in trapped-ion QCCD quantum computers. Using advanced noise filtering techniques, the authors show that these real-world hardware imperfections limit the effectiveness of standard error suppression protocols. They introduce a novel, adaptive DD protocol that dynamically adjusts refocusing pulses during idle times to maintain high fidelity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it bridges fundamental quantum control theory (DD) with practical hardware limitations (scheduling jitter), providing actionable insights for building fault-tolerant trapped-ion quantum processors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how scheduling errors, inherent in trapped-ion QCCD architectures, degrade the performance of Dynamical Decoupling (DD) sequences designed to suppress memory errors.

**Main result.** Scheduling errors increase DD sensitivity to low-frequency noise, making high pulse frequencies counterproductive; a real-time protocol that opportunistically refocuses pulses is proposed as an improvement.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Filter Function formalism and cumulant expansion techniques to model noise suppression in the presence of stochastic timing deviations from ideal gate sequences.

**Model / system.** The system is trapped-ion QCCD quantum computers, modeled using an effective qubit Hamiltonian subject to low-frequency magnetic field fluctuations ($\propto \sigma_z$). The analysis extends DD theory from deterministic control to stochastic control incorporating pulse timing jitter.

**Key observables.** Survival probability ($P$), the second cumulant $C^{(2)}(T)$, and the Control Filter Function $F(\omega, T)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical threshold for DD pulse frequency (around 2 Hz) where scheduling errors become dominant; the spectral dependence of noise ($S(2\pi f)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include treating $\pi$-pulses as instantaneous and assuming that scheduling errors are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.).

**Figures summary.** Figures show filter functions for CPMG sequences, empirical distributions of timing offsets ($\epsilon$), and survival probabilities demonstrating the plateauing fidelity improvement due to accumulated scheduling error.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing standard DD theory using the Filter Function formalism to incorporating stochastic scheduling errors via cumulant expansions. It culminates in proposing and analyzing a real-time, opportunistic refocusing protocol for improved robustness.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We examine the impact of scheduling errors on dynamical decoupling (DD) in trapped-ion quantum charge-coupled devices (QCCDs) and develop better strategies for reducing memory errors. In the QCCD architecture, qubit transport and control introduce stochastic pulse delays that impact the efficiency of DD. Using the filter-function formalism, we analyze the performance of DD in the presence of scheduling-induced timing errors, showing that they increase the sensitivity of standard DD to low-frequency fluctuations. For typical memory errors in trapped-ion platforms, we numerically demonstrate that increasing the DD pulse frequency beyond 2 Hz is generally counterproductive due to scheduling-induced timing errors. Furthermore, we introduce a real-time DD protocol that inserts refocusing pulses opportunistically during idle periods. We demonstrate our methods on Quantinuum H2-1 with Ramsey delay-type experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a unified, tunable framework to significantly strengthen existing monogamy and polygamy inequalities for multipartite entanglement. By introducing a hierarchy parameter $m$, the authors derive progressively tighter bounds on how entanglement is distributed among multiple subsystems. This offers enhanced mathematical tools for applications in quantum communication and information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced mathematical tools that refine fundamental constraints on entanglement, which are crucial for designing robust quantum communication protocols and understanding multi-partite correlations in quantum computing architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to develop a unified, tunable framework to refine and strengthen existing monogamy and polygamy relations governing entanglement distribution in multipartite quantum systems.

**Main result.** They derive hierarchical families of tightened $\alpha$-power monogamy and $eta$-power polygamy inequalities, which become progressively tighter as the tuning parameter $m$ increases.

**Method.** The approach introduces a parameterized framework indexed by $m \ge 1$, utilizing mathematical tools like analyzing binomial functions to establish generalized lower (monogamy) and upper (polygamy) bounds on entanglement measures.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on multipartite quantum states, particularly involving bipartite entanglement measures ($E$) and their assisted counterparts ($E_a$), applicable to general quantum information theory contexts like quantum networks.

**Key observables.** Entanglement measures such as Concurrence ($C$) and Assisted Concurrence ($C_a$); the degree of entanglement distribution across multiple subsystems.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The hierarchy parameter $m \ge 1$, and exponents $\alpha$ (for monogamy) and $eta$ (for polygamy), which define the validity regimes for the derived inequalities.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that existing entanglement measures satisfy initial power-law monogamy ($\gamma$-power) and polygamy ($\delta$-power) inequalities, and it incorporates state-dependent asymmetry parameters ($\mu, 
u$) to capture physical imbalances.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares multiple lower bounds for the $\alpha$-th power monogamy relation of concurrence, showing that one bound incorporating state-dependent asymmetry is the tightest. Figure 2 compares two upper bounds for the $eta$-th power polygamy relation, demonstrating the new derived bound is strictly tighter.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing a unified parameterized framework ($m$) to derive specific theorems (e.g., Theorem 3.1 for monogamy and Theorem 4.1 for polygamy). It then strengthens these results by applying them to concrete measures like Concurrence, showing how the bounds tighten as $m$ increases.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

For a bipartite entanglement measure $\mathcal{E}$ that satisfies the $γ$th-power monogamy inequality (Eq.~\eqref{e:chap1-ineq1}), and for its assisted counterpart $\mathcal{E}_a$ that obeys the $δ$th-power polygamy inequality (Eq.~\eqref{e:chap1-ineq2}), we introduce a unified, tunable framework indexed by a parameter $m\geq1$. Within this framework, we derive two hierarchical families of refined inequalities:    a tightened $α$-power monogamy relation for $\mathcal{E}$, valid for all $α\geq mγ$;    a tightened $β$-power polygamy relation for $\mathcal{E}_a$, applicable for $(m-1)δ< β\leq mδ$.    As $m$ increases, the bounds become progressively tighter, recovering known results at $m=1$. Notably, the optimal monogamy bound emerges as a piecewise function of $α$, with additional correction terms activated as $α$ crosses successive integer thresholds, thereby offering a sharper characterization of entanglement distribution.   We demonstrate that our results generalize and strengthen existing monogamy and polygamy relations through analytical comparisons and numerical evaluations using concurrence and concurrence of assistance. This hierarchical, parameterized approach offers enhanced and flexible tools for applications in quantum communication, quantum networks, and multipartite quantum information processing.

</details>

<sub>Fig. 2: Numerical values of klow(p). The growth near p = 1/4 is klow(p) ∼1/[12( 1</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the long-standing problem of additivity for minimum output $p$-Rényi entropy of quantum channels. By constructing explicit counterexamples using random projections, the authors prove nonadditivity holds for large ranges of the Rényi order parameter $p$, thereby significantly constraining the remaining unknown regime to $[1/4, 3/4]$. This advances the theoretical understanding of entanglement measures in quantum information theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep theoretical insights into fundamental properties of quantum information measures, which are crucial for understanding channel capacity and entanglement quantification in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining the additivity of the minimum output $p$-Rényi entropy ($	ext{S}_{\min}^p(\Phi)$) for quantum channels, which asks if $	ext{S}_{\min}^p(\Phi \otimes\Psi) = 	ext{S}_{\min}^p(\Phi) + 	ext{S}_{\min}^p(\Psi)$.

**Main result.** The authors prove that additivity fails for all Rényi orders $p$ in the ranges $[0, 1/4) \cup (3/4, \infty)$, significantly narrowing the unresolved interval to $[1/4, 3/4]$.

**Method.** The proof employs sophisticated random-projection constructions and asymptotic analysis, utilizing specific witnesses (product–conjugate Bell-state witness for $p>3/4$ and transpose-complement rank-defect witness for $p<1/4$).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quantum channels ($\Phi$) acting on finite-dimensional systems, specifically those induced by Haar-distributed projections ($P_{AB,n}$) on a bipartite system.

**Key observables.** Minimum output $p$-Rényi entropy ($	ext{S}_{\min}^p(\Phi)$), and the limiting one-channel output body $K_{k,t}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Rényi order parameter $p$, and the asymptotic rank ratio parameter $t \in (0, 1)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on establishing the almost sure convergence of the actual output set $	ext{C}_n$ to a theoretical limit $K_{k,t}$ as system size $n 	o \infty$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the additivity failure by constructing explicit counterexamples for specific ranges of $p$, combining random-projection techniques with asymptotic entropy calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Additivity of minimum output entropies is a central problem in quantum information theory. Nonadditivity is known for every Rényi order $p>1$, at the von Neumann point $p=1$, and near $p=0$, while most of the interval $0<p<1$ has remained open. We prove that for every Rényi order $p$ satisfying either $p>3/4$ or $0\leq p<1/4$, there exist finite-dimensional projection-induced quantum channels such that additivity of the minimum output $p$-Rényi entropy fails. The proof combines two correlated random-projection constructions: a product-conjugate Bell-state witness for $p>3/4$, and a transpose-complement rank-defect witness for $p<1/4$. Thus the unresolved part of $0<p<1$ is reduced to $[1/4,3/4]$. Our estimates also improve the output dimension threshold for additivity violation of minimum output von Neumann entropy, first established in Belinschi, Collins and Nechida.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper uses Quantum Monte Carlo simulations to study trapped superfluid Fermi gases, examining how their shell structure changes as the interaction strength approaches unitarity. The key finding is that strong pairing correlations effectively wash out the characteristic shell gaps observed at weaker interactions. This provides a theoretical model relevant for understanding nuclear physics phenomena in exotic neutron-rich isotopes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges cold atomic physics (ultracold Fermi gases) with fundamental nuclear structure questions (shell evolution in neutron-rich nuclei), providing a clean quantum simulation platform for understanding complex many-body phenomena like pairing and correlation effects.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how shell structure, a characteristic feature of quantum systems, evolves when superfluid Fermi gases are tuned towards the unitary limit.

**Main result.** Shell effects are observed at finite interaction strengths but demonstrably vanish as the system approaches unitarity, suggesting strong pairing correlations suppress single-particle behavior.

**Method.** The research employs Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) methods, specifically Diffusion Monte Carlo (DMC), alongside Variational Monte Carlo (VMC) calculations to determine ground state energies and gaps.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a two-component Fermi gas confined in a harmonic trap. The interactions are characterized by tunable scattering length ($a$) and effective range ($r_e$), allowing exploration across different interaction regimes, including the unitary limit.

**Key observables.** Two-particle separation energies ($S_{2N}$), two-particle shell gaps ($\Delta_{2N}$), and Odd-Even Staggering (OES) quantifying pairing correlations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Scattering length ($a$), effective range ($r_e$), particle number ($N$), and the unitary limit ($a 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a harmonic trap potential, which differs from the finite-depth potentials found in real nuclei; furthermore, discrepancies exist between calculated pairing gap scaling parameters ($\eta$) and experimental values.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how energy gaps and separation energies diminish towards unitarity as interaction parameters are varied, confirming the suppression of shell structure by strong correlations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework using QMC for trapped Fermi gases. It then systematically analyzes observables like $S_{2N}$ and $\Delta_{2N}$ across varying interaction strengths to demonstrate the vanishing of shell effects at unitarity, linking this mechanism to pairing correlations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We provide quantum Monte Carlo calculations of a two-component Fermi system interacting with an attractive interaction confined in harmonic traps. We investigate the role of the interaction's scattering length and effective range and show its important role in modifying shell effects in the structure of these systems. We show that in the strongly interacting regime, where the scattering length is large, the dominant role to shell effects is due to the effective range. These conclusions are very relevant for nuclear physics, in particular for neutron-rich systems, and open the way to perform new atomic experiments that can help to explain the disappearance of shell-effects in nuclei.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates the creation and precise control of a Light-Induced Superconducting (LiPS) state in an Al-Si heterojunction using femtosecond laser pulses. By manipulating the underlying structural superlattice formed by misfit dislocations, the authors can tune the superconducting critical temperature between $1.8 	ext{ K}$ and $8.5 	ext{ K}$. This work opens a pathway for engineering metastable, long-range phase coherent quantum states for future quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum optics (femtosecond pulsing) with condensed matter superconductivity in a highly tunable manner. The concept of using light to engineer metastable, phase-coherent superconducting states is directly relevant to designing novel quantum circuits and local gap tuning mechanisms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central goal is to achieve direct, controllable evidence of a true zero-resistance superconducting state whose macroscopic quantum phase coherence can be created and controlled using light.

**Main result.** The authors report creating and fully controlling a Light-Induced Superconducting (LiPS) state in an Al-Si heterojunction, demonstrating tunable critical temperatures ($1.8-8.5 	ext{ K}$) via tailored laser pulse sequences.

**Method.** The study combines ultrafast optical excitation using femtosecond lasers with electrical transport measurements ($ho(T)$, $V$-$I$, $ho(B)$) and structural characterization using HRTEM.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an aluminum-silicon heterojunction, where the superconductivity arises from light pulse control over a Moiré-like superlattice formed by misfit dislocations (MDs). The state exhibits features of Berezinski-Kosterlitz-Thouless transitions and 2D vortex dynamics.

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), resistivity ($ho(T)$), upper critical fields ($H_{c}$), superfluid stiffness ($\Theta$), and the structural periodicity of MDs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Femtosecond laser pulse duration (e.g., $220 	ext{ fs}$), superconducting temperatures ($1.8-8.5 	ext{ K}$), and applied magnetic fields up to $\sim 3 	ext{ T}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The LiPS effect is attributed to light pulse control of the structural modulation (MD superlattice) at the interface, which imparts metastability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the switching mechanism using pulses, show temperature profiles calculated from optical excitation, and detail superconducting properties like $V$-$I$ curves and magnetic field dependence for two distinct states ($	ext{SC}1$ and $	ext{SC}2$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the difficulty of observing light-controlled superconductivity, presenting the LiPS effect in Al-Si heterojunctions via laser pulses, characterizing two distinct superconducting states ($	ext{SC}1$ and $	ext{SC}2$) through transport measurements, and finally attributing the mechanism to structural control over misfit dislocations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quest for superconductivity created by light extends for more than half a century, yet direct evidence of a true zero-resistance state -whose macroscopic quantum phase coherence is both created and controlled by light -- has remained elusive. Here we report for the first time on a complex but robust light-programmable superconducting (LiPS) state at an aluminium-silicon heterojunction that is created and fully controlled with femtosecond laser pulses. The superconducting critical temperatures -- ranging from 1.8-8.5 K, can be increased or erased at will by the application of tailored pulse sequences. At low temperatures the LiPS state shows features characteristic of a Berezinski-Kosterlitz-Thouless topological transition, but another distinct state appears at temperatures above 2 K, which shows clear signatures of quantum phase disorder. In the presence of a magnetic field we observe behaviour characteristic of vortex pinning and creep consistent with the 2-dimensional (2D) nature of the phase coherent system. The origin of the LiPS effect is attributed to light pulse control of the Moire-like superlattice of misfit dislocations (MDs) that naturally occur as a result of discommensurations between Al and Si lattices at the interface, and is clearly observable by high-resolution electron microscopy. We show how light pulses can be used to control the superlattice periodicity and highlight the appearance of topologically protected soliton-like kinks along the dislocation lines, important for imparting metastability to the system. The demonstration of LiPS opens a route to the design of metastable long-range phase coherent superconducting states, leading to light-engineering of quantum circuits, local gap tuning in quantum processors and novel devices utilizing switchable superconductivity.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes electron transport in a Corbino disk geometry by comparing two effects: the tomographic flow regime and the even-odd effect arising from electron-electron scattering. The authors predict that the even-odd effect should manifest as an enhancement in resistance sensitivity at low magnetic fields, providing a diagnostic tool for understanding quantum kinetic phenomena in 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the detailed analysis of non-equilibrium dynamics using the Boltzmann equation and harmonic decomposition touches upon advanced kinetic theory relevant to open quantum systems far from equilibrium.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates magnetotransport in the Corbino geometry, comparing the influence of geometric constraints (tomographic flow regime) against intrinsic electron-electron scattering effects (even-odd effect).

**Main result.** The authors predict a distinct signature of the even-odd effect—an enhancement of $\partial R/\partial(B^2)$ at small magnetic fields ($B 	o 0$)—which is most visible near the crossover from ballistic to tomographic flow.

**Method.** Numerical analysis solving the linearized Boltzmann equation by expanding the distribution function in angular eigenmodes (Fourier series).

**Model / system.** The study models two-dimensional electron systems confined to a Corbino disk geometry under an applied magnetic field, using the linearized Boltzmann equation to describe non-equilibrium electron flows.

**Key observables.** Resistance sensitivity ($\partial R/\partial(B^2)$) at low fields, and dimensionless conductance ($G/G_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), magnetic field strength ($B$), ratio of scattering rates for even vs. odd harmonics ($\gamma_{e}^{ee}/\gamma_{o}^{ee}$), and the crossover parameter $v_F 	au_{	ext{even}} / r_b$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed in the linearized regime, assuming a degenerate electron gas ($T \ll E_F$), and relies on specific approximations for scattering interactions (e.g., $I_{	ext{ex}} \approx -I_{	ext{dir}}/2$).

**Figures summary.** Figures show normalized conductance and resistance sensitivity ($\partial R/\partial(B^2)$) as a function of the ratio of Larmor radius to disk size ($r_b/R_L$), demonstrating parameter dependence.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theoretical frameworks by deriving scattering rates for odd and even harmonics, solving the linearized Boltzmann equation across different flow regimes (ballistic to tomographic), and finally predicting observable signatures in magnetotransport measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In two dimensions, the geometric constraints due to Pauli blocking and conservation laws lead to the even-odd effect exhibited by the electron-electron scattering lengths: electron-electron collisions are more efficient at relaxing the even angular harmonics of the distribution function than the odd ones. Inspired by a recent experiment on the magnetotransport in the Corbino disk geometry, we numerically analyze the electron flows in this geometry across all the regimes.   We predict a clear signature of the even-odd effect - enhancement of the resistance sensitivity $\partial R/\partial(B^2)$ at small magnetic fields $B\rightarrow 0$. This enhancement is most prominent at the crossover from the ballistic to the tomographic regime, and gradually disappears when the temperature is further increased. Our estimates suggest that in the temperature range of the experiment, the effect should be small. This implies that the attribution of the anomalous scaling of the kinematic viscosity, that was observed in the experiment, to the even-odd effect might need more careful consideration.   As a side note, we show how the method of characteristics can be extended to treat the long-lived odd harmonics, which allows one to recast the linearized Boltzmann equation as a system of integral ones.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel local variance-based method to self-calibrate programmable photonic interferometer meshes. By measuring the statistical variance of output intensity under controlled phase perturbations, the authors can accurately determine operating points for MZIs and phase shifters without needing complex node isolation or global training fields. This makes the technique highly practical for building scalable, self-stabilizing photonic processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly practical, low-overhead calibration primitive for scalable integrated photonics, which is crucial for realizing complex quantum optical circuits and photonic quantum computing architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Accurately calibrating programmable photonic interferometer meshes is difficult using conventional methods due to requirements like node isolation or complex characterization procedures, especially in large, thermally tuned systems.

**Main result.** The authors introduce and validate a local variance-based self-calibration method that uses only intensity measurements to determine operating points for MZIs and phase shifters without requiring dedicated routing paths or orthogonal training states.

**Method.** Calibration is achieved by applying controlled phase perturbations and identifying the calibration point from the minima of the measured output-power variance, which follows characteristic $|\sin 	heta|$ (for MZIs) or $|\cos \phi|$ (for PSs) dependencies.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on programmable photonic interferometer meshes implemented on silicon nitride platforms, featuring Mach-Zehnder Interferometers (MZIs) and phase shifters arranged in large arrays (e.g., $8 	imes 8$ processors).

**Key observables.** Output-intensity variance ($	ext{Var}(P_{out})$), which exhibits characteristic $|\sin	heta|$ or $|\cos\phi|$ minima at operating points.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The method is robust to random phase settings and requires only a small, finite ensemble of measurements per calibration step.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes that local input fields are non-zero and that the variance observable can be used locally without needing global knowledge or perfect isolation between elements.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup on an $8 	imes 8$ processor, showing theoretical signatures of the variance dependence for both MZIs ($|\sin	heta|$) and PSs ($|\cos\phi|$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of existing calibration techniques. It then derives the statistical relationship between output power variance and operating parameters ($\sigma(	heta) \propto |\sin 	heta|$, $\sigma(\phi) \propto |\cos \phi|$). Finally, it validates this method experimentally on a large processor using an automated two-stage procedure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Programmable photonic interferometer meshes enable reconfigurable linear optical transformations, but their performance depends critically on accurate calibration of Mach-Zehnder interferometers and phase shifters. Conventional methods often require node isolation, dedicated routing paths, orthogonal training states, reference channels, or prior phase-voltage characterization, which become increasingly difficult in large thermally tuned meshes.   We introduce a local variance-based self-calibration method using intensity-only measurements. Controlled phase perturbations are applied, and calibration points are identified from minima of the measured output-power variance. For Mach-Zehnder interferometers, the variance follows a characteristic |sin(theta)| dependence, allowing bar and cross operating points to be found without conventional node isolation. For phase shifters, balanced interference produces a complementary |cos(phi)| variance signature, enabling quadrature calibration through the same statistical principle.   We validate the method experimentally on an 8 x 8 silicon nitride programmable photonic processor using a fully automated two-stage procedure. Starting from random phase settings, all Mach-Zehnder interferometers are calibrated first, followed by phase-shifter calibration under balanced-interference conditions. As a system-level test, we implement an embedded 4 x 4 Hadamard transformation on the 8 x 8 processor using a Clements decomposition.   These results establish local output variance as a simple calibration observable for programmable photonic meshes. The method is compatible with discrete random phase ensembles and requires neither conventional node isolation nor orthogonal training fields, making it a practical calibration primitive for scalable self-stabilizing photonic processors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced Parameter-Shift Rules (PSRs) to accurately calculate gradients for boson sampling experiments operating with photon loss in integrated photonics. It provides a robust mathematical framework, showing when and how these rules can be applied to both Fock and Gaussian boson sampling regimes. The results are validated by comparing the method's performance against finite difference techniques on real hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical challenges of implementing quantum algorithms on noisy photonic hardware by providing mathematically rigorous gradient estimation techniques that outperform simple finite differencing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is deriving robust parameter-shift rules (PSRs) to compute gradients of transition probabilities in photonic boson sampling experiments that incorporate photon loss.

**Main result.** PSRs are derived for Fock boson sampling with a lossy model, showing limitations when generalizing to Gaussian boson sampling unless specific factorization conditions on the transmission matrix are met.

**Method.** The authors derive PSRs by relating derivatives of observables to linear combinations of shifted function evaluations, comparing this method's performance against standard finite difference techniques.

**Model / system.** The system involves bosonic modes in photonic quantum experiments, where lossy evolution is modeled by embedding the process into a larger unitary transformation acting on system and environment modes. The core mathematical tools are ladder operators and Fock/coherent states.

**Key observables.** Fock boson sampling transition probabilities, measurement outcome probabilities, average photon number, and photon-number correlators.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The order of the parameter shift rule ($n$), the total number of photons detected, and the loss parameters embedded in the transmission matrix $T$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes that cross-talk between modes is negligible, allowing phase-shift loss to commute with the phase shift. The method also relies on the observable being representable as a finite Fourier series.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes; however, the text refers to figures illustrating the interferometer structure and potentially comparing PSR performance vs. FD.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model of lossy evolution using an enlarged unitary transformation $W$. It then develops the mathematical framework for Parameter-Shift Rules (PSRs) applicable to this lossy setting, deriving specific rules for Fock boson sampling and analyzing the conditions under which Gaussian boson sampling can also be addressed.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using a robust photon loss model for photonic quantum experiments, we derive $n-$th order parameter-shift rules for computing gradients of Fock boson sampling transition probabilities where $n$ photons are sent into a lossy interferometer. We also show that in general it is not possible to generalize this gradient recipe to the case of Gaussian boson sampling. Only in the specific case where the transmission matrix of the interferometer can be factorized as a diagonal loss matrix premultiplied by a pure unitary it is possible to obtain parameter-shift rules with a finite order given by twice the total number of photons detected. We demonstrate the efficacy of the proposed method by comparing its performance against finite differences on real hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a general scaling relation linking the DC Hall conductivity to the high-frequency optical Hall response in time-reversal symmetry breaking systems. By applying this relationship, the authors make a quantitative prediction for the anomalous Hall effect in cuprate superconductors based on their measurable Kerr rotation signals.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, universal diagnostic tool—a scaling law—to connect disparate measurements (DC vs. optical) in complex quantum materials exhibiting broken symmetries, which is relevant for understanding exotic electronic states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives a general scaling relation connecting the low-frequency (quasi-dc) Hall conductivity to the asymptotic high-frequency optical Hall response in Time-Reversal Symmetry Breaking (TRSB) materials.

**Main result.** A scaling relation, $	ext{Re}|\sigma_{xy}(\omega)| \propto |\sigma_{xy}(0)| (\delta/\omega)^2$, is derived and shown to hold for several models. This is used to predict a measurable anomalous Hall effect in cuprate superconductors based on their Kerr effect measurements.

**Method.** The derivation relies on the Kramers-Kronig relations, connecting $\sigma_{1,xy}(\omega 	o 0)$ to $\sigma_{1,xy}(\omega \gg 	ext{gap})$ by analyzing the frequency dependence of the optical conductivity in different limits.

**Model / system.** The study applies to TRSB states like ferromagnets and superconductors (e.g., cuprates), modeled using concepts from cyclotron resonance, Dirac continua, and superconducting gap structures.

**Key observables.** Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$), Kerr effect signal ($	heta_K$), quasi-dc Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{1,xy}(\omega 	o 0)$), and high-frequency optical response ($\sigma_{1,xy}(\omega \gg 	ext{gap})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Characteristic energy scales like the superconducting gap ($2\Delta$) or cyclotron frequency ($\omega_c$); the ratio $\delta/\omega$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes specific functional forms for conductivity in high and low-frequency limits, and the application to cuprates relies on relating the Kerr effect magnitude to the DC Hall response.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the derivation using dirty limit TRSB superconductors (Fig. 1) and compare $\sigma_{1,xy}$ and $\sigma_{2,xy}$ for cyclotron resonance in Drude/Dirac models (Figs. 2 & 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by deriving the general scaling relation using KK relations, testing it against idealized models (cyclotron resonance), validating it with theoretical TRSB models, and finally applying the derived relationship to predict a specific measurable quantity in cuprate superconductors.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Based on the Kramers-Krong relations, we derive and apply a quite general expression that that relates the low frequency quasi-dc Hall conductivity to the asymptotic high frequency optical Hall response (and its manifestations in Kerr and Faraday effects) for time-reversal symmetry breaking (TRSB) states of matter like ferromagnets and time-reversal symmetry breaking superconductors as well as metals in magnetic field. Parametric plots shows this relation is obeyed exactly for the trivial single-mode case of sharp cyclotron resonance, and approximately for theoretical models for ferromagnets and TRSB superconductors. We also apply it to the experimental data from a variety of ferromagnetic systems and show reasonable agreement there as well. We use the relation to predict, from the size of the spontaneous Kerr effect at the pseudogap temperature of the cuprate superconductors that cuprates should exhibit an anomalous Hall effect of approximately 0.1 Ohm$^{-1}\cdot$cm$^{-1}$. This is a small value, but one within experimental reach and we encourage the search for it. Although not explicitly quantum geometric, our treatment has some similarities to efforts to set bounds on physical quantities based on quantum geometric relations and limited physical information.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how nonlinearity affects the transition between long-range and short-range scaling of Lyapunov spectra in quasiperiodically driven unitary maps. By comparing these results to random disorder, the authors find that quasiperiodic potentials exhibit unexpected behavior, suggesting that long-range correlations persist even when interactions break integrability.

**Why it may be interesting.** The investigation bridges concepts from quantum chaos (Lyapunov exponents), localization theory (Anderson/quasiperiodic models), and nonlinear dynamics, which are central to understanding energy transport and thermalization in complex quantum systems relevant to AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the Lyapunov spectrum scaling transition for thermal weakly-nonlinear unitary maps when subjected to quasiperiodic potentials, comparing this behavior against known Anderson disorder.

**Main result.** Contrary to expectations based on linear analysis, the numerical results show that the scaling crossover in the quasiperiodic case is strongly delayed and occurs at anomalously small localization lengths ($\xi$), suggesting a role for long-range correlations.

**Method.** The authors employ numerical analysis of Lyapunov exponents using the tangent-space approach, alongside statistical measures like the Participation Number ($P_N$) to characterize state localization in both nonlinear and linear regimes.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional lattice evolving under a unitary circuit map incorporating quasiperiodic potentials ($\hat{Q}$), nonlinear site-dependent phase shifts ($\hat{G}$), and nearest-neighbor rotations ($\hat{C}$).

**Key observables.** Lyapunov spectrum scaling, Localization length ($\xi$), Participation Number ($P_N$), and Kolmogorov-Sinai (KS) entropy ($\kappa$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonlinearity strength ($g$), localization length ($\xi$), and the comparison between quasiperiodic potentials and random Anderson disorder.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a unique choice of action-angle coordinates and utilizes periodic boundary conditions for simulations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare $P_N$ distributions and $\mu(P_N)$ vs. $\xi$, showing strong localization in the linear quasiperiodic case; they also contrast Lyapunov scaling ($ar{\Lambda}$) and KS entropy ($\kappa$) between disordered and quasiperiodic potentials across varying nonlinearity.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the model (unitary circuit map), analyzing the linear limit ($g=0$) using $P_N$ to characterize localization, then extending the analysis to nonlinear regimes by studying Lyapunov spectrum scaling ($ar{\Lambda}$ and $\kappa$), culminating in a direct comparison between quasiperiodic and disordered potentials.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the Lyapunov spectrum scaling of thermal weakly-nonlinear unitary maps in the presence of quasiperiodic potentials. We search for the crossover from long-range to short-range scaling as the localization length ξ decreases and compare the details to the case of uncorrelated Anderson disorder [Phys. Rev. Res. 6 L012064 (2024)]. A comparative statistical analysis of the eigenstates for the linear case shows that quasiperiodicity has a stronger localization impact at the same value of ξ. Therefore we expect that the scaling crossover should be enhanced as well. However, the numerical analysis shows that it is strongly delayed as compared to Anderson disorder, and is observed at anomalously small values of ξ. These findings hint at the potential impact of long range correlations of quasiperiodic localized eigenstates, which persist in the presence of interactions even in the case of integrability breaking and thermalization.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a holographic framework to model cosmological singularities beyond the standard BKL description. By introducing controlled violations of the Null Energy Condition through non-Hermitian quantum field theories, the authors predict the existence of a Little Rip cosmology in black hole interiors. This work establishes a potential 'Little Rip/CFT correspondence' using advanced diagnostic tools like heavy-operator correlators.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between open quantum systems physics (PT-symmetry, non-Hermiticity) and fundamental gravitational/cosmological endpoints (Little Rip) provides a powerful theoretical tool for studying extreme spacetime regimes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to understand cosmological singularities beyond the standard BKL paradigm by analyzing holographic flows derived from non-Hermitian quantum field theories.

**Main result.** Controlled violations of the Null Energy Condition via non-Hermiticity lead to a novel, isotropic Little Rip cosmology in the black hole interior, distinct from standard Kasner behavior.

**Method.** The study employs holographic techniques (AdS/CFT) to map bulk geometry dynamics onto boundary QFT observables, utilizing analysis of two-sided heavy-operator correlators and thermal state properties.

**Model / system.** The model is a holographic dual to a strongly coupled non-Hermitian PT-symmetric QFT. The system involves analyzing the evolution of black hole interiors under conditions violating the NEC.

**Key observables.** Two-sided heavy-operator correlators, geodesic length $L_{	ext{ren}}(E)$, and the equation of state $p = -\epsilon - 2\alpha$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $\eta = |s_{nh}/s_h|$ governing the balance between non-Hermitian ($s_{nh}$) and Hermitian ($s_h$) sources, and the asymptotic behavior of energy density ($\epsilon \propto \log a$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The connection between the deep interior geometry (bulk) and boundary observables (correlators) via the holographic dictionary is central; approximations are used for near-horizon expansion.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy level spectra in PT-symmetric regimes, compare numerical vs. analytical field values ($	ilde{g}, 	ilde{f}, \psi, R$) across vast scales, and show diagnostic plots related to geodesic length decay rates.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework connecting non-Hermiticity to NEC violation (Chunk 1), then analyzing this using toy models of thermal states and PT symmetry restoration (Chunk 2), culminating in a detailed geometric analysis comparing Little Rip scaling against Kasner metrics (Chunk 3).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spacelike singularities supported by matter satisfying the null energy condition (NEC) are expected to fall within the Belinski--Khalatnikov--Lifshitz (BKL) paradigm. We show that controlled violations of the NEC in holography can lead to different black hole interiors. In a holographic model dual to a strongly coupled non-Hermitian $\mathcal{PT}$-symmetric QFT, we uncover a novel non-Kasner regime within its real, $\mathcal{PT}$-restored phase. The deep-interior geometry describes an isotropic FLRW cosmology undergoing super-accelerated expansion and approaching a Little Rip. This regime leaves a characteristic imprint on two-sided heavy-operator correlators, allowing it to be distinguished from a standard Kasner interior. Our construction provides a concrete holographic realization of a Little Rip cosmology and lays the groundwork for a Little Rip/CFT correspondence, in which such cosmological regimes can be explored through observables in a non-Hermitian quantum field theory.

</details>

### [Analytic gradients for low-rank quantum optimal control](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14217v1)

**Authors:** Leo Goutte, Vincenzo Savona  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.14217v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `QC/QI experiment` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<sub>FIG. 1. Schematic of the LROC algorithm. A forward pass is evaluated with a candidate pulse and the states {Ψj} are stored in memory (blue block). From these, the loss is com- puted and the adjoint state is initialized and λ(T) evolves backward in time following Eq. (9) (red block), computing the gradient at every time tj = (j −1)∆t and subsequently discarding the forward state Ψj. The parameters are up- dated through gradient descent or Adam [34] and the process is repeated until convergence. A similar procedure holds for multi-state objectives by parallelizing over many states (faded dashed arrows). Further details on the numerical implemen- tation are provided in Appendix C 1.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents Low-Rank Optimal Control (LROC), a novel technique to optimize control pulses for open quantum systems like superconducting circuits. By exploiting the low-rank nature of the evolving density matrix, LROC calculates necessary gradients with significantly reduced computational cost compared to standard methods. This breakthrough extends gradient-based optimization to much larger and more complex physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant as it provides a scalable framework for optimizing quantum operations in realistic, dissipative hardware platforms by circumventing exponential scaling issues inherent in full density matrix simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is designing optimal control pulses for open quantum systems where simulating the full density matrix evolution is computationally prohibitive.

**Main result.** The authors introduce Low-Rank Optimal Control (LROC), which achieves an analytic gradient calculation with a quadratic improvement in time and memory scaling compared to full master equation methods.

**Method.** LROC combines the Low-Rank Approximation (LRA) of the density matrix ($\hat{ho} \simeq \Psi\Psi^\dagger$) with the adjoint state method, allowing for efficient gradient computation via an adapted Pontryagin maximum principle framework.

**Model / system.** The work models open quantum systems using superconducting circuits (transmons), governed by a Lindblad master equation. The control Hamiltonian allows for time-dependent external driving fields.

**Key observables.** State preparation fidelity (e.g., GHZ state) and average gate fidelity (e.g., CNOT gate).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The low-rank approximation size ($M$) relative to the Hilbert space dimension ($N$), and the time step ($\Delta t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that quantum protocols preserve purity, enabling the accurate low-rank factorization of the density matrix. The gradient derivation involves approximations in discretization.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the LROC algorithm flow: forward state propagation, loss calculation, backward adjoint evolution for gradients, and parameter update via gradient descent.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the low-rank master equation, then deriving the analytic gradient using the adjoint method (Weak Pontryagin maximum principle), and finally demonstrating its application to various physical tasks like state preparation and gate fidelity optimization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce low-rank optimal control (LROC), a method for designing control pulses in open quantum systems whose full density-matrix simulation is prohibitively expensive. The method exploits a feature of quantum computing itself: because protocols are designed to preserve purity, the density matrix is dominated by a few pure states and admits an accurate low-rank factorization. LROC propagates only this factorized form and, by deriving the corresponding adjoint equation, obtains the gradient of any differentiable objective at the same reduced cost as the simulation, leading to a quadratic improvement in time and memory compared to the full master equation. We illustrate the breadth of the method on four superconducting-circuit tasks: preparation of a five-qubit GHZ state, a CNOT gate, qubit readout, and an error correction primitive, modeled with realistic multilevel transmons, decay, and strong drives, in each case reaching fidelities consistent with the intrinsic dissipation limits. LROC thereby extends pulse-level optimization to system sizes beyond the reach of existing gradient-based methods.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a Thermodynamic Theory of Voting (TTV) that models political vote distribution using principles from statistical mechanics. It shows that the observed inequality in election results closely follows patterns predicted by Rayleigh-Jeans thermalization and condensation phenomena. This suggests that electoral outcomes can be analyzed using tools developed for studying wealth or energy distributions in physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly quantum, the core mathematical machinery—thermalization, energy conservation, and phase transitions leading to condensation—is central to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, which informs open quantum systems dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a Thermodynamic Theory of Voting (TTV) that statistically describes the distribution of party votes in large-scale elections, drawing parallels with established physical models.

**Main result.** The TTV successfully provides a good statistical description for vote distributions in EU and French elections by modeling them using Rayleigh-Jeans (RJ) thermalization and condensation phenomena.

**Method.** The authors adapt concepts from statistical mechanics, particularly the Wealth Thermalization Hypothesis, to model political interactions as an energy conservation system governed by RJ statistics.

**Model / system.** The system models party votes/political power distribution using coupled nonlinear oscillators whose energies are analogous to vote fractions. The theory relies on deriving distributions from conserved total energy and probability norm.

**Key observables.** Lorenz curves, Pareto curves, and the Gini coefficient ($G$) are used as primary diagnostics to quantify inequality in vote shares.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature/Energy ($\epsilon$), number of parties ($N_p$), and model parameters derived from fitting real data (e.g., $a$ parameter for RJE).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes that opinion formation results from complex, classical nonlinear interactions leading to thermalization; the analysis is based on the RJ distribution.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare empirical Lorenz and Pareto curves from EU/French elections (2014-2024) against theoretical predictions derived from the RJS model. Maps show Gini coefficients across various EU countries.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces TTV, establishes parallels with wealth inequality models exhibiting RJ condensation, details the use of Lorenz/Pareto curves and Gini coefficients, and applies these methods systematically to multiple historical election datasets (EU and French).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a thermodynamic theory of voting and show that it provides a good description of distribution of party votes in EU elections. The theory traces parallels between system energies of coupled nonlinear oscillators and party vote fractions. Such a classical system evolution is characterized by the conservation of total energy and probability norm that leads to the Rayleigh-Jeans (RJ) thermalization and condensation at low energy states. A similar thermalization also describes the wealth inequality in society. This feature belongs to the phenomena of constraint driven condensation known in statistical mechanics. We show that the RJ theory well depicts the Lorenz and Pareto curves obtained from the EU vote results. The theory also recovers the dispersion of votes between candidates of first round presidential elections in France.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a machine-learning pipeline to overcome the experimental bottleneck of measuring Majorana zero modes at ultra-low temperatures. By training neural networks on simulated paired high- and low-temperature conductance data, the authors demonstrate that key topological features can be reliably inferred from simpler, higher-temperature measurements. This offers a practical acceleration tool for quantum device development.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of advanced deep learning architectures like diffusion models and Vision Transformers to extract fundamental quantum signatures from noisy, experimentally accessible data is highly relevant for open quantum systems characterization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Characterizing Majorana zero modes (MZMs) in nanowires typically requires slow, resource-intensive ultra-low temperature measurements.

**Main result.** Machine learning models can successfully reconstruct low-temperature conductance and predict topological visibility using only easier-to-measure high-temperature data.

**Method.** The authors employed two machine learning approaches: a diffusion-inspired U-Net Transformer for conductance reconstruction, and a Video Vision Transformer (ViViT) for direct prediction of topological visibility.

**Model / system.** The study models semiconductor–superconductor hybrid nanowires hosting topological superconductors, governed by a Bogoliubov-de Gennes Hamiltonian. The analysis compares high-temperature simulations to target low-temperature MZM properties.

**Key observables.** Local and nonlocal conductances ($G_{ii}, G_{ij}$), and the Topological Visibility (TV).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ultra-low temperatures ($\approx 50 	ext{ mK}$), high-temperature measurements, disorder strength, superconducting gap $\Delta_0$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that ML models can learn the underlying physics to invert the thermal broadening effect inherent in high-temperature measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate network architectures (SWIN-Block, UNETR upsampling) and compare reconstruction/prediction results against direct measurement or inverse convolution methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the difficulty of low-T MZM detection. It then details using ML on paired high/low-T simulations to train two networks: one for conductance reconstruction (diffusion model) and one for TV prediction (ViViT). Results demonstrate high fidelity in both tasks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Identifying Majorana zero modes in semiconductor--superconductor nanowires requires ultra-low temperature transport measurements in dilution refrigerators, making device screening slow and resource-intensive. Here, we investigate whether high-temperature conductance data can be used to infer low-temperature Majorana nanowire properties before committing devices to dilution-refrigerator characterization. We generate paired high- and low-temperature conductance simulations for disordered Majorana nanowires and train neural networks to perform two related tasks. First, we use a Shifted Window U-Net Transformer diffusion-inspired architecture to reconstruct low-temperature conductance from thermally broadened high-temperature measurements, achieving high-fidelity recovery with $R^2 \approx {0.95}$ for local conductance and $R^2 \approx {0.91}$ for nonlocal conductance. Second, we train a Video Vision Transformer-based network to predict the low-temperature topological visibility directly from high-temperature conductance, obtaining $R^2 \approx {0.80}$. These results demonstrate that machine-learning models can recover and infer low-temperature Majorana features from experimentally easier high-temperature data, providing a practical route for rejecting poor devices early thus avoiding slow and resource-intensive dilution refrigeration for non-promising devices. This high-temperature screening approach could substantially accelerate the experimental feedback loop for Majorana nanowire device development.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper advances nonlinear thermodynamic computing by replacing simple mean readout with moment-resolved statistics ($\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}], \mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}^{\odot 2}], \mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}^{\odot 4}]$). They demonstrate that combining multiple, independently trained reservoirs using feature-level fusion significantly boosts classification accuracy on MNIST. This suggests that capturing higher-order fluctuations and leveraging reservoir heterogeneity are key design principles for these physical computing systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects concepts from non-equilibrium statistical mechanics (Langevin dynamics, finite-time observables) directly to machine learning architectures, providing a physical mechanism for feature extraction beyond simple mean measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to improve nonlinear thermodynamic computing by extending readout mechanisms from using only the mean expectation value to utilizing higher-order polynomial raw moments.

**Main result.** A heterogeneous multi-reservoir architecture employing feature-level fusion of moment-resolved readouts achieved state-of-the-art classification accuracy (96.95%) on MNIST, suggesting that joint optimization over multiple reservoirs is beneficial.

**Method.** The authors use Langevin dynamics to simulate non-equilibrium systems and construct a response vector from the first, second, and fourth raw polynomial moments of the system's state variables.

**Model / system.** The model is based on nonlinear thermodynamic computers driven by overdamped Langevin dynamics with quartic confinement. The dynamics are generally nonconservative, meaning they do not derive from a global scalar energy landscape.

**Key observables.** Raw polynomial moments: $\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}]$, $\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}^{\odot 2}]$, and $\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}^{\odot 4}]$; Test Accuracy on MNIST (e.g., 96.95%).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite observation time ($t_f$); Reservoir coupling weights ($\mathbf{W}$); Temperature/Fluctuation scale ($k_B T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The readout is based on finite-time Monte Carlo sampling of the stochastic dynamics, and the analysis assumes that feature-level fusion (concatenation) allows for joint optimization while maintaining an additive linear classifier structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures likely compare performance metrics like test accuracy across different reservoir combination strategies (single vs. fused) and show error overlap analyses using Jaccard coefficients to support the benefit of heterogeneity.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the limitations of mean-only readout, then proposing moment-resolved observables ($\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}], \mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}^{\odot 2}], \mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}^{\odot 4}]$), and finally demonstrating superior performance using a heterogeneous multi-reservoir architecture with feature-level fusion.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonlinear thermodynamic computers based on Langevin dynamics exploit thermal fluctuations as a physical substrate for computation. Recent work has shown that quartic-confined fluctuating degrees of freedom can act as thermodynamic neurons capable of nonlinear function approximation at finite observation times. Here we extend this paradigm from mean-only readout to moment-resolved readout. Instead of representing each driven reservoir solely by its first moment, we construct a response vector from the elementwise raw polynomial moments   \(\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}]\),   \(\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}^{\odot 2}]\), and   \(\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}^{\odot 4}]\).   These observables combine displacement and central-shape contributions and are naturally aligned with the linear, quadratic, and quartic terms of the local driven dynamics.   We further introduce a heterogeneous multi-reservoir architecture in which three reservoirs with distinct initialization and training histories form a joint \(2304\)-dimensional response representation. Under the fixed MNIST \(60000/10000\) reproduction protocol, feature-level fusion achieves the best observed accuracy of \(9695/10000=96.95\%\), compared with \(9682/10000=96.82\%\) for the strongest single-reservoir model and \(9684/10000=96.84\%\) for equal-weight logit averaging. An exact paired McNemar test does not establish a statistically significant improvement over the strongest single reservoir, but the ablation and wrong-set overlap results provide suggestive evidence of complementary classification errors. These results motivate higher-order polynomial-moment readout and reservoir heterogeneity as candidate design principles for finite-time Langevin computing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the fundamental problem of quantifying errors when approximating critical quantum ground states using tensor networks like MPS and PEPS. The authors introduce a novel, stable linear algebra technique to calculate necessary derivatives for optimization. This allows them to precisely determine the physical nature of finite-entanglement scaling perturbations in models like the Ising chain.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, stable computational tool for optimizing complex quantum states (PEPS/MPS), which is crucial for simulating strongly correlated materials or open quantum systems where exact diagonalization fails.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses determining the actual physical perturbations induced by approximating critical quantum systems using Matrix Product States (MPS) and developing a robust method to calculate derivatives of 2D tensor networks.

**Main result.** They show that MPS approximations induce relevant perturbations, which can be calculated precisely via implicit differentiation, providing insights into finite entanglement scaling beyond simple CFT predictions.

**Method.** The core methodology involves developing a sparse linear solver to compute forward and backward derivatives of 2D tensor networks implicitly, circumventing instabilities in traditional automatic differentiation techniques.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on critical quantum Hamiltonians modeled by MPS (1D) and two-dimensional lattice models like the Ising model represented by PEPS. The analysis is performed on square lattices using tensor network representations.

**Key observables.** Perturbation ($\delta$), Correlation Length ($\xi$), Overlap between induced perturbation and physical operators, and the Schatten 4-norm of the transfer matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond Dimension ($\chi$) is the key parameter controlling the approximation quality; finite size/entanglement scaling are central concepts.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is simplified by restricting to tensor networks on square lattices with real, rotationally/reflectionally invariant tensors for initial demonstrations.

**Figures summary.** TABLE I demonstrates that the overlap of the perturbation causing the largest change in correlation length exhibits perfect polynomial scaling with bond dimension $\chi$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the mathematical machinery (sparse linear solver, implicit differentiation) first, and then applies it to analyze finite-$\chi$ effects in critical 1D and 2D systems, comparing results against established CFT predictions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The concept of finite entanglement scaling forms one of the pillars on which the tensor network ecosystem is built. In this paper, we resolve the open problem of determining the actual perturbations induced by matrix product state approximations of critical systems, and we demonstrate that these can be quite different than the ones predicted by conformal field theory. To that aim, we develop a sparse linear solver to calculate the forward and backward derivatives of 2-dimensional tensor networks with respect to their defining parameters in an implicit way. This algorithm is of independent interest as it provides a primitive for the variational optimization of projected entangled pair states that circumvents the instabilities plaguing traditional automatic differentiation methods.

</details>

### [Quantum Markov Chains for an Asymmetric Mixed Ising-XY Model on a Cayley Tree](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14343v1)

**Authors:** Farrukh Mukhamedov  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.14343v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.14343_figures/2607.14343_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: The first levels of Γ2 + and the locally asymmetric mixed Ising–XY assignment of interactions.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2607.14343_figures/2607.14343_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>

</details>

<img src="2607.14343_figures/2607.14343_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the quantum dynamics of a mixed Ising-XY model situated on an infinite Cayley tree. By utilizing Quantum Markov Chain theory, the authors prove that the system admits a unique translation-invariant steady state solution. This establishes strong mathematical constraints on the possible physical states achievable by this complex interacting spin system.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools (QMCs) to characterize equilibrium states in complex quantum spin systems on infinite lattices, which is crucial for understanding critical phenomena and phase transitions in condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates Quantum Markov Chains (QMC) associated with a mixed Ising-XY model defined on an infinite Cayley tree structure.

**Main result.** The paper proves the existence and uniqueness of a translation-invariant QMC solution for all coupling constants, alongside demonstrating that the reduced dynamics possess no periodic points beyond period one.

**Method.** The analysis employs the compatibility criterion for tree-indexed QMCs, deriving boundary equations solved via transfer operators and analyzing the resulting finite-dimensional dynamical system.

**Model / system.** The model is a mixed Ising-XY Hamiltonian defined on the semi-infinite rooted Cayley tree of order two. The interactions are locally asymmetric: XY coupling acts along one branch, and Ising coupling acts along the other.

**Key observables.** Local two-site entanglement; the boundary law parameters (Bloch coefficients); existence/uniqueness of translation-invariant solutions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strengths ($J_I, J_{XY}$), inverse temperature ($eta$), and the structure of the tree ($\Gamma_2^+$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on working with the normalized trace convention and assuming the existence of a unique positive translation-invariant solution to proceed.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the locally asymmetric mixed Ising–XY assignment (XY on one edge, Ising on the other) on the Cayley Tree. Figure 2 shows the local three-site interaction block at a vertex.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the QMC framework using compatibility criteria, derives the boundary equation and transfer operator, analyzes the resulting reduced dynamical system for periodic points, proves uniqueness theorems for the translation-invariant solution, and concludes with entanglement calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a mixed quantum Ising-$XY$ model on the semi-infinite rooted Cayley tree of order two. For every vertex $u$, the edge $\langle u,(u,1)\rangle$ carries an $XY$ interaction and the edge $\langle u,(u,2)\rangle$ carries an Ising interaction. Using the compatibility criterion for tree-indexed quantum Markov chains and consistently working with the normalized trace, we derive the translation-invariant boundary equation and compute explicitly the associated local transfer operator, namely the one-step partial-trace map which propagates successor boundary data to the parent vertex. We prove that the boundary equation has a unique positive translation-invariant solution for all $J_I,J_{XY}\in\mathbb R$ and $β>0$. Hence the model admits a unique translation-invariant quantum Markov chain generated by a positive translation-invariant boundary condition. We also show that the reduced boundary-law dynamics, i.e. the induced finite-dimensional recursion for the boundary-law parameters, has no admissible periodic points of period greater than one and compute the local two-site entanglement on the natural three-site cluster of the tree.

</details>

**Authors:** Suraj Singh, Lakshya Nagpal, Vikas Chauhan, S. R. Hassan  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.14282v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

Comparison between the exact instantaneous spectral gap and the normalized worldline magnetization susceptibility measured in SQA for a representative n = 10 Sherrington–Kirkpatrick instance. The minimum of ∆(s) occurs at sED = 0.883, while the maximum of χm(s) occurs at sSQ = 0.920. Left: gap and normalized susceptibility over the full annealing path. The susceptibility identifies the minimum-gap region without using spectral information.</sub>

Right: instantaneous annealing velocity ds/dt on a logarithmic scale. The Roland–Cerf schedule produces an approximately four-orders-of-magnitude slowdown within a narrow interval surrounding the minimum gap, whereas the worldline-susceptibility schedule distributes the slowdown more smoothly over the broader critical region identified by χm(s).</sub>

The exact minimum gap lies at the terminal point, sED = 1, and the Roland–Cerf schedule remains strongly suppressed throughout the runtime interval. The worldline-susceptibility schedule, whose susceptibility maximum occurs at the interior point sSQ = 0.876, consistently produces the highest success probability.</sub>

For the n = 10 instance, the runtime is instead compressed into a narrow interval surrounding the interior minimum gap. Although both schedules satisfy exactly the same local-adiabatic condition, they produce two qualitatively different finite-time dynamical pathologies.</sub>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel scheduling technique for quantum annealing using the worldline magnetization susceptibility as a proxy for the spectral gap. This surrogate method allows for constructing an optimized anneal schedule without needing to solve the full Hamiltonian spectrum. The results prove that this observable-based approach is robust and can outperform theoretically ideal schedules when considering realistic finite-time limitations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant because it provides a practical, computationally tractable alternative to the theoretically optimal but inaccessible local-adiabatic schedule for quantum annealing simulations, directly impacting how open quantum systems are modeled.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a computationally feasible, optimal annealing schedule for quantum annealing that surpasses the theoretical but impractical local-adiabatic evolution.

**Main result.** A surrogate schedule based on worldline magnetization susceptibility consistently outperforms conventional linear annealing and, under realistic finite-time conditions, can surpass the exact spectral gap-based Roland–Cerf schedule.

**Method.** The authors propose using Simulated Quantum Annealing (SQA) to calculate the worldline magnetization susceptibility ($\chi_m$), which serves as a surrogate observable for the instantaneous spectral gap to construct the annealing time distribution.

**Model / system.** The system is quantum annealing, modeled by evolving a Hamiltonian $\hat{H}(s)$ from a driver to a problem Hamiltonian. The analysis uses Sherrington–Kirkpatrick spin-glass instances and extends to open systems using Lindblad master equations for decoherence testing.

**Key observables.** Worldline magnetization susceptibility ($\chi_m$), ground-state probability ($P_{GS}$), spectral gap ($\Delta(s)$), and the derived failure threshold ($\Delta^*$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Annealing time $T$, system size $n$ (up to 20), decoherence rate $\gamma$, and Trotter slice number $M$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that robust scheduling based on inexpensive equilibrium observables can outperform exact spectral gap strategies under realistic, finite-time conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate the comparative performance of schedules under decoherence (Fig. 10 vs Fig. 11), confirm the stability of failure mechanisms across system sizes (Fig. 14), and validate a critical failure threshold ($\Delta^*$) using held-out statistical tests (Fig. 13).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the computational bottleneck of exact scheduling, proposing $\chi_m$ as a surrogate observable via SQA, demonstrating its superior performance over RC in ideal cases, and finally rigorously validating this robustness against decoherence and finite-size effects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The performance of quantum annealing depends critically on how the available annealing time is distributed along the evolution. Although the Roland Cerf local adiabatic schedule is theoretically optimal, it requires complete knowledge of the instantaneous spectral gap, making it impractical for large optimization problems. We propose a computationally inexpensive surrogate schedule based on the worldline magnetization susceptibility measured during simulated quantum annealing. The susceptibility is obtained directly from equilibrium Monte Carlo sampling and identifies the critical region of the anneal without requiring spectral information. Using exact diagonalization of Sherrington Kirkpatrick spin glass instances as ground truth, we show that the resulting schedule consistently outperforms conventional linear annealing and, for a substantial fraction of instances, also surpasses the exact Roland Cerf schedule. We demonstrate that this unexpected behaviour originates from two finite time failure modes of exact local adiabatic scheduling a boundary gap trap, in which the minimum spectral gap occurs at the end of the anneal, and an oscillatory instability caused by excessively localized time allocation around an interior minimum gap. These results suggest that robust scheduling based on inexpensive equilibrium observables can outperform exact spectral gap based strategies under realistic finite time conditions. The complete methodology is implemented in the open source Qanneal framework.

</details>
