**Summary.** This paper presents a unified theoretical framework for analyzing time-stepping methods that approximate solutions to evolution equations by minimizing residuals on low-dimensional nonlinear manifolds. It rigorously separates and bounds the errors arising from both the time discretization scheme ($\zeta$-methods) and the residual minimization process itself. This is highly valuable for developing stable and accurate numerical solvers for quantum dynamics simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools to analyze the convergence of complex numerical schemes used in quantum dynamics, particularly those involving variational parameterizations common in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a unified error analysis for time-discrete parametric approximations of evolution equations posed in Hilbert spaces.

**Main result.** The global approximation error can be decomposed into separate residual and time discretization components, achieving convergence rates up to second order under various stability assumptions.

**Method.** The core method involves iteratively minimizing a residual norm (derived from either discretizing the equation then parameterizing, or parametrizing then discretizing the variational principle) at each time step.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to general evolution equations in Hilbert spaces, specifically illustrated using time-dependent Schrödinger and Fokker–Planck equations. The solution is approximated by a parametrized ansatz on a low-dimensional nonlinear manifold.

**Key observables.** Global approximation error, residual norms, and gradients of the cost function $F_k(	heta) = \|r_k(	heta, \cdot)\|^2_H$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time step size ($h$), total time ($T$), and the structure/conditioning of the parametrization map.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on Lipschitz, one-sided Lipschitz, or dissipativity assumptions for convergence guarantees; stability conditions involving parametrization map conditioning arise naturally in certain formulations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 10 shows the evolution of a single Gaussian wave packet under different regimes of the Fokker–Planck equation. Figure 11 illustrates the squared $L_2$-norm difference between exact and numerical solutions for this example, showing convergence with step size.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces an abstract framework (Section 2), derives explicit residual formulations based on two paradigms (Section 3), develops a unified error analysis for $\zeta$-methods (Section 4), and details closed-form formulas for Gaussian manifolds (Section 5).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study time-discrete parametric approximations of evolution equations in Hilbert spaces based on residual minimization. The solution is represented by a parametrized ansatz belonging to a low-dimensional nonlinear manifold, and time stepping is performed by minimizing suitably defined residuals at each step. Two natural residual formulations are considered: discretization followed by parametrization of the evolution equation, and discretization of the Dirac--Frenkel variational principle governing the parameter dynamics. A unified error analysis is developed for both approaches within the family of $ζ$-methods. The resulting bounds separate the effects of time discretization from those of residual minimization and yield first- and second-order convergence under Lipschitz, one-sided Lipschitz, and dissipativity assumptions. For the variational formulation, additional stability conditions involving the conditioning of the parametrization map arise naturally. The framework is applied to Gaussian approximation manifolds, for which residual norms and gradients admit explicit closed-form expressions when polynomial operators are involved. This enables efficient implementation without spatial discretization. Numerical experiments for time-dependent Schrödinger equations illustrate the theoretical convergence rates and the influence of residual accuracy on conservation properties.

</details>

### [Single-component twisted $\mathbb{Z}_3$ orthogonal metal in an $e/3$-anyon fluid](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15274v1)

**Authors:** Zhaoyu Han, Ashvin Vishwanath, Eslam Khalaf  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.15274v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **3/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5** · `correlated cavity matter` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.15274_figures/2607.15274_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. A possible phase diagram as a function of doping charge density δ and temperature T. Td and Tloc are, respec- tively, the quantum degeneracy and localization scales of the anyons. An alternative possibility, not shown, is that the Z3 orthogonal metal and the Fermi liquid (and correspondingly their zero temperature insulating phases) are separated by a first order transition.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a theoretical model for a twisted $\mathbb{Z}_3$ orthogonal metal formed by doping a Fractional Chern Insulator. The metallic phase is characterized by emergent $e/3$ fermions and exhibits unique transport signatures like fractional shot noise. Crucially, pairing this state allows the realization of superconducting phases whose topological properties are controllable via band structure analysis.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit prediction of fractional charge carriers ($e/3$) and the connection between orbital magnetism, fractionalization, and tunable chiral central charges are highly relevant for designing novel topological quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes an unconventional metallic phase, a twisted $\mathbb{Z}_3$ orthogonal metal, emerging when doping a Fractional Chern Insulator (FCI). This state serves as a parent for realizing anyon superconductivity with arbitrary chiral central charge.

**Main result.** The resulting metal exhibits fractionalized transport signatures ($e/3$ shot noise, $6\pi$ Josephson effect) and can pair into superconductors whose topological properties are dictated by the BdG band topology of the paired fermions.

**Method.** The analysis uses parton decomposition on a low-energy Lagrangian derived from the parent FCI state to separate physics into itinerant (f) and gapped (g) sectors, followed by detailed theoretical predictions for transport and pairing.

**Model / system.** The system models doping the $1/3$ Laughlin state on a lattice, characterized by three degenerate valleys due to fractional background flux. The effective low-energy theory is described by an interacting Lagrangian involving emergent charge-$e/3$ fermions coupled to a $\mathbb{Z}_3$ gauge field.

**Key observables.** Fractional charge ($e/3$), anomalous quantum oscillations (frequency factor of 9), $6\pi$ ac Josephson effect, and the chiral central charge ($c_{-}$) of the resulting superconductor.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping parameter ($\delta$), temperature regimes relative to localization scale ($T_{loc}$) and degeneracy scale ($T_d$), and the ratio of inter-valley to intra-valley repulsions ($r$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The stability of the single-pocket OM is argued based on net inter-valley repulsion, and the analysis assumes that the f-fermion sector governs low-energy charge dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows a $\delta$-$T$ phase diagram illustrating critical temperatures for different metallic and superconducting phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the parent state (FCI) and then uses parton decomposition to derive an effective Lagrangian. It analyzes transport properties, predicts fractionalized signatures, discusses competing metallic states (triple-pocket vs. single-pocket OM), and finally details how pairing leads to specific superconducting topological orders.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose an unconventional metallic phase emerging on doping the $1/3$ Fractional Chern insulator, which can serve as a parent state to anyon superconductivity with arbitrary chiral central charge. It is a twisted $\mathbb{Z}_3$ orthogonal metal: a state with vanishing electron quasiparticle weight but a single well-defined Fermi surface of emergent charge-$e/3$ fermions coupled to a Dijkgraaf-Witten twisted $\mathbb{Z}_3$ gauge field. It is manifestly valley-symmetric and valley-gapped. Pairing this fractionalized Fermi surface then removes the topological order and produces a family of superconductors whose chiral central charge is directly set by the BdG band topology of the paired $e/3$ fermions, while the angular momentum of the physical order parameter is constrained to be a multiple of three. This scenario provides a route to superconducting states beyond anyon-superconductivity constructions based on $2e/3$ anyons. The metallic phase itself carries distinctive ``fractional Fermiology'' signatures: $e/3$ shot noise, anomalous quantum oscillations, and a $6π$ ac Josephson effect when it mediates the Josephson coupling between superconductors. We construct explicit wavefunction ansatz realizing the phase, and argue that inter-valley repulsion between anyons stabilizes this phase compared to competing states. We show that the construction extends to higher Laughlin states but not to Jain states.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work uses DMRG on the Hofstadter-Hubbard model to examine an interface between a Quantum Hall insulator and a Chiral Spin Liquid. The primary finding is the existence of a gapless, spinless charged excitation localized at this boundary. The research also details how bulk excitations in both phases exhibit characteristic signatures of fractionalization or electron-hole binding.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of topological interfaces and fractionalized excitations is highly relevant to understanding emergent phenomena in strongly correlated electron systems, which often exhibit complex quantum ground states beyond simple band theory predictions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates exotic excitations at the interface separating two distinct topological insulating phases: an Integer Quantum Hall (IQH) phase and a Chiral Spin Liquid (CSL) phase.

**Main result.** The IQH–CSL interface hosts a unique, gapless spinless charged excitation. Furthermore, bulk analysis reveals fractionalization in the CSL phase and exciton formation in the IQH phase.

**Method.** Density Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) calculations were extensively used to study both topological response to fluxes and low-lying excited states across the interface.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using the triangular-lattice Hofstadter-Hubbard model, parameterized with spatially varying interaction strengths ($U$) to realize the IQH (TI) and CSL (TMI) regimes.

**Key observables.** Charge density deviation ($	ext{n} - 	ext{n}_{	ext{gs}}$), local magnetization ($S_z$), charge/spin pumping, and binding energies of spin-triplet excitons.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the half-filled ground state regime, comparing regimes with $U=8$ (IQH) and $U=14$ (CSL).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations are performed on finite-radius cylinders (YC3, YC5), and results rely on DMRG sweeps which serve as diagnostics for identifying characteristic fractionalized structures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate charge/spin density profiles under flux insertion, compare single-particle excitations in bulk IQH vs CSL phases, and track the energy evolution of two-particle states across different bond dimensions ($\chi$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the interface by first characterizing bulk excitations (fractionalization in CSL, excitons in IQH), then using pumping measurements to identify the gapless edge mode, and finally analyzing two-particle excited states to confirm spinon/chargon separation at the boundary.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the interface separating two topologically distinct insulating phases of matter using extensive density-matrix renormalization group calculations to study the triangular-lattice Hofstadter-Hubbard model with a spatially varying interaction strength, chosen to realize both integer quantum Hall and chiral spin liquid states in different spatial regions. We find that the integer quantum Hall-chiral spin liquid interface hosts a spinless charged excitation that is bound to the interface. This mode at the interface is identified through charge and spin pumping, and by direct calculations of low-lying excited states. We also characterize bulk excitations in both phases, finding evidence for fractionalization in the chiral spin liquid and for spin-triplet exciton formation in the integer quantum Hall phase.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an efficient backpropagation algorithm tailored for calculating gradients in quantum circuits simulated using Pauli propagation. By exploiting circuit reversibility, it achieves significant memory savings compared to standard automatic differentiation techniques. This enables accurate and scalable classical optimization of quantum models relevant to condensed matter physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial computational tool for optimizing quantum circuits applied to condensed matter models (like Ising/Heisenberg), enabling efficient parameter tuning that was previously limited by memory constraints in gradient estimation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing a memory-efficient backpropagation algorithm to calculate parameter gradients for quantum circuits simulated via Pauli propagation.

**Main result.** The proposed method achieves computational complexity comparable to standard sparse Pauli simulation while offering significant memory savings ($\mathcal{O}(n_{	ext{param}})$) over conventional reverse-mode automatic differentiation.

**Method.** It employs a specialized backpropagation technique that exploits the reversibility of quantum circuits, allowing gradient calculation by recomputing intermediate operators rather than storing them all.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on simulating quantum circuits applied to models like the transverse-field Ising model and the Heisenberg model, which are relevant in condensed matter physics.

**Key observables.** Parameter gradients ($\partial L / \partial 	heta$), observable expectation values, and operator-complexity measures such as the operator stabilizer Rényi entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of parameters ($n_{	ext{param}}$) in the circuit is crucial for determining computational scaling and memory savings.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on finite truncation thresholds ($\delta$) during Pauli propagation, which introduces controlled errors; it also assumes the cost function depends only on the final layer's observable state.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the forward (Heisenberg updates) and backward (gradient calculation) propagation workflow. Figure 2 compares norm error and operator infidelity between forward and backward passes, showing consistency with the truncation threshold $\delta$.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of gradient computation in Pauli simulation, details the forward pass using Heisenberg evolution rules for observables, presents the novel memory-efficient reverse-mode algorithm utilizing circuit reversibility, and validates it by applying it to state preparation and time-evolution compression tasks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a backpropagation algorithm for evaluating parameter gradients in quantum circuits using Pauli propagation simulation. The method has computational complexity comparable to that of standard sparse Pauli simulation techniques, while producing gradients whose accuracy is of the same order as the corresponding observable expectation values. By exploiting the reversibility of quantum circuits, the algorithm reduces the memory cost by a factor of $\mathcal{O}(n_\text{param})$ compared with conventional reverse-mode automatic differentiation, where $n_\text{param}$ denotes the number of parameters in the circuit. Compared with finite difference methods, the algorithm is $\mathcal{O}(n_\text{param})$ more efficient in function evaluations. These features enable efficient and accurate classical optimization of quantum circuits for applications such as state preparation and time-evolution compression, while also allowing operator-complexity measures such as the operator stabilizer Rényi entropy to be monitored and regularized during optimization. We demonstrate the method by optimizing low-energy state-preparation circuits for transverse-field Ising models in one, two, and three dimensions and for the three-dimensional Heisenberg model, and by compressing two-dimensional time-evolution circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper pioneers an evolution-level approach to quantum optimal control using Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs). Instead of optimizing pulses, it optimizes the entire controlled dynamical process on the Bloch sphere. This method allows researchers to design gates that are not only high-fidelity but also physically diagnosable and adaptable to complex hardware constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, modern computational tool for quantum control that moves beyond simple time-domain pulse shaping by enforcing physical laws directly into the optimization objective, which is highly relevant for designing robust quantum hardware protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how to optimize quantum gate design by treating the entire controlled evolution, rather than just the input pulse amplitudes, as the object of optimization.

**Main result.** Using Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) allows for a unified, differentiable framework that optimizes control fields, trajectories, and duration simultaneously, yielding physically readable controls.

**Method.** The authors employ PINNs to encode the Bloch equation dynamics as constraints within the loss function, enabling end-to-end optimization of the quantum process.

**Model / system.** The system is a single qubit whose evolution is modeled on the Bloch sphere and governed by a dissipative Bloch vector field ($\dot{\mathbf{r}} = \mathbf{F}[\mathbf{r}, \Omega, \Delta]$).

**Key observables.** Process fidelity ($	ext{F}_{	ext{proc}}$), average gate fidelity ($	ext{F}_{	ext{avg}}$), and the residual geometric error for path constraints.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipation rates ($\Gamma_1, \Gamma_2$), optimization weights ($\alpha_{	ext{dyn}}, eta_{	ext{gate}}$), and the trainable duration $T$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the continuous dynamics can be accurately represented by a differentiable function learned via PINNs, which unifies previously separated optimization steps.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare discrete pulse optimization methods against the proposed PINN generator approach for visualizing control field and trajectory learning.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitation of traditional pulse optimization, proposes the continuous PINN framework to model the full evolution, details the loss function incorporating dynamics and gate requirements, and validates this method on both standard rotation gates and geometrically constrained gates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum gate design is often represented as pulse optimization, although the physical object that implements a gate is the full controlled evolution generated by the pulse. Here we use physics-informed neural networks to represent single-qubit gate design at this evolution level: the control fields, the Bloch-state trajectories, and the total duration are learned together under the Bloch equation. This changes the optimized object from pulse amplitudes to a differentiable physical process whose structure can be inspected and refined. For rotation gates, the optimized evolutions recover the physical organization expected for bounded single-qubit control, with no prescribed pulse ansatz or duration scan. For a geometric gate, the representation identifies localized bottlenecks in maintaining the geometric condition and turns this diagnosis into feedback, reducing the residual path error while preserving high fidelity. Thus physics-informed learning is used not only to synthesize gates, but also to make optimized quantum controls physically readable, diagnosable, and locally refinable. This process-level view may be especially useful for adapting gates to hardware-specific, task-specific, and locally varying experimental constraints.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Subspace Iteration CTMRG (SI-CTMRG) to drastically improve the computational efficiency of calculating properties on infinite projected entangled-pair states (iPEPS). By replacing costly large-matrix Singular Value Decompositions with smaller, iterative updates, the method shifts the bottleneck to GPU-friendly tensor contractions. It successfully applies this technique to the triangular Heisenberg antiferromagnet, achieving state-of-the-art results much faster than previous methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter models, the core contribution is a highly efficient numerical technique for tensor network contractions (iPEPS/CTMRG), which has broad applicability in simulating quantum many-body systems beyond standard DMRG limits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to improve the computational efficiency of calculating observables on infinite projected entangled-pair states (iPEPS) by accelerating the Corner Transfer Matrix Renormalization Group (CTMRG) contraction step.

**Main result.** The proposed SI-CTMRG method shifts the dominant cost from computationally expensive SVDs to tensor contractions, yielding significant speedups (up to two orders of magnitude) and achieving state-of-the-art results for the triangular Heisenberg antiferromagnet on modern GPUs.

**Method.** It introduces Subspace Iteration CTMRG (SI-CTMRG), a QR-based projector construction that replaces large-matrix SVDs with smaller, iterative updates followed by a small SVD. This makes the method highly suitable for GPU acceleration and fixed-point automatic differentiation.

**Model / system.** The method is demonstrated on two-dimensional quantum lattice systems, specifically using the spin-1/2 nearest-neighbor Heisenberg antiferromagnet Hamiltonian on the triangular lattice, which exhibits 120-degree magnetic order.

**Key observables.** Energy per site ($e$), local magnetic moment ($m$), and correlation length ($\xi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($\chi$), oversampling parameter ($p$), and computational time/GPU resources (H100).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard CTMRG bottleneck is the cubic scaling of large-matrix SVDs, which SI-CTMRG circumvents by using subspace iteration updates.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare energy convergence and time per step between standard CTMRG and SI-CTMRG; Figure 3 shows linear extrapolation plots for ground state properties ($e$ vs $1/\xi^{*3}$) comparing results to other methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of standard CTMRG, details the novel SI-CTMRG algorithm using QR decomposition and subspace iteration, applies it to the triangular Heisenberg model, validates its efficiency via speedup comparisons, and finally presents extrapolated ground state properties consistent across different ansatz sizes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The corner transfer matrix renormalization group (CTMRG) is one of the standard contraction methods for infinite projected entangled-pair states (iPEPS), but its computational cost is dominated by repeated truncated singular value decompositions (SVDs). We introduce subspace-iteration CTMRG (SI-CTMRG), a QR-based projector construction that replaces each large-matrix SVD with an SVD of a much smaller matrix. The resulting algorithm shifts the dominant cost from decompositions to tensor contractions, making it highly suited to GPU acceleration and yielding speedups of up to two orders of magnitude over standard CTMRG. We demonstrate the efficiency and accuracy of the method for the triangular-lattice Heisenberg antiferromagnet, reaching state-of-the-art iPEPS results on a single H100 GPU in approximately 10 hours of computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores monopole Spin Density Wave (SDW) phases in magnetic Weyl semimetals by developing a theoretical framework based on monopole harmonic functions. The research demonstrates that different types of SDW ordering yield unique, measurable signatures in electronic band structures and surface spin polarizations. It unifies the understanding of topologically ordered states across various pairing channels.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between topological invariants (Berry curvature, quantum geometric tensor) and magnetically induced pairing states provides a powerful framework for understanding emergent phenomena in correlated materials relevant to spintronics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates monopole Spin Density Wave (SDW) states arising from particle-hole pairing in magnetic Weyl semimetals, extending the understanding of topologically ordered phases.

**Main result.** The SDW order parameter is described by monopole harmonic functions and realizes ideal quantum geometry in the weak-coupling limit. Helical and cycloidal orders produce distinct signatures observable via spin-ARPES.

**Method.** Theoretical derivation using mean-field Hamiltonians, projection onto low-energy Fermi surfaces, and analysis of topological invariants like the pairing quantum geometric tensor.

**Model / system.** The system is a magnetic Weyl semimetal modeled with nested Fermi surfaces. The Hamiltonian includes terms for the underlying band structure ($h_0$) and the SDW mean field ($H_M$).

**Key observables.** Band structures, Fermi arc distributions, surface spin polarization patterns, and the quantum geometric tensor.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weak-coupling limit; chirality of enclosed Weyl nodes; nesting vector Q (e.g., $Q=2\pi/3$ vs. same-chirality pairing).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes particle-hole pairing occurs between Fermi surfaces enclosing Weyl nodes of the same chirality for the primary focus, though comparisons are made with other nesting vectors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate bulk and surface band structures, showing distinct spin polarization patterns along Fermi arcs depending on whether the SDW order is helical or cycloidal. Comparisons are drawn between different pairing scenarios.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the concept of monopole SDW states in Weyl semimetals, deriving the necessary gap functions using monopole harmonics. It then analyzes specific magnetic orders (helical vs. cycloidal) by solving a lattice model Hamiltonian and calculating diagnostic observables like spin polarization and quantum geometry tensors. Finally, it compares these results to other nesting conditions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The interplay between topology and magnetism in Weyl semimetals has recently emerged as a fertile ground for novel quantum phases. While monopole harmonic order parameters have been established for superconductivity and charge density waves in these systems, their spin density wave counterparts remain unexplored. Here we introduce monopole spin density wave (SDW) states arising from particle-hole pairing between nested Fermi surfaces enclosing Weyl nodes of the same chirality. We demonstrate that the SDW order parameter inherits a nontrivial pairing Berry phase and is described by monopole harmonic functions that exhibit topologically protected nodal structures in the gap function. Through a concrete lattice model, we show that helical and cycloidal SDW orders produce distinct signatures in band structures, Fermi arc distributions, and surface spin polarization patterns, which can be directly resolved by spin- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy. Remarkably, we find that the quantum geometric tensor of the monopole pairing realizes ideal quantum geometry in the weak-coupling limit, where quantum distance fluctuations are entirely governed by Berry curvature. Our results not only unify the understanding of monopole ordered states across pairing channels but also provide experimental avenues for distinguishing competing magnetic orders in ReAlX (Re=rare earth elements, X=Si, Ge) materials and suggest potential applications in topological spintronics.

</details>

### [Analytical and Numerical Study of Quartic Nonlinear Electrodynamics in Holographic Fermion Systems](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15236v1)

**Authors:** Vitor Neves da Silva, Luiz Antonio Barreiro

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how quartic nonlinearities in the gauge field affect charged fermions propagating holographically. By solving the modified electrodynamics background, the authors calculate the fermionic spectral function and find that these nonlinear terms shift the Fermi momentum and enhance the excitation weight. This demonstrates a clear mechanism by which bulk nonlinearities propagate to observable boundary physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter/gravity duality, the explicit calculation of fermionic spectral functions and quasiparticle renormalization due to nonlinear bulk couplings provides a theoretical analogue for understanding how higher-order interactions modify low-energy excitations in strongly correlated systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how leading string-inspired quartic corrections modify holographic fermionic observables within a bottom-up AdS4/CFT3 model.

**Main result.** The quartic interaction increases the chemical potential and enhances the spectral weight of fermions, causing the Fermi momentum to shift without destroying the holographic Fermi surface.

**Method.** The authors analyze the nonlinear electrostatic background derived from the gauge sector using an exact first integral, then solve the resulting radial Dirac equation for charged probe fermions.

**Model / system.** The system involves charged probe Dirac fermions propagating in a fixed Reissner–Nordström-AdS geometry. The gauge field dynamics are governed by Maxwell electrodynamics supplemented by a quartic nonlinear term ($\alpha'^2 F^4$).

**Key observables.** Retarded Green's function, spectral function $A(\omega, k)$, chemical potential ($\mu$), and Fermi momentum ($k_F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Quartic coupling strength ($\alpha'$), black hole charge ($q$), fermion charge ($q_f$), and the ratio $T/\mu$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis operates within the probe approximation, meaning the fermions do not backreact on the geometry or gauge field. The treatment is restricted to the leading $\alpha'^2 F^4$ correction.

**Figures summary.** Figure 3 illustrates the momentum dependence of the spectral function for varying $\alpha'$, showing peak shifting and enhancement; Figure 4 shows the polynomial fits for $k_F$ and maximum spectral weight ($A_{max}$) versus $\alpha'$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first analyzes the nonlinear gauge sector to find an exact constitutive relation for the electric field. Subsequently, it uses this background to solve the radial Dirac equation for fermions, calculating observables like the Green's function and spectral function.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study how the leading string-inspired quartic correction $α'^2 F^4$ modifies holographic fermionic observables in a bottom-up $AdS_4/CFT_3$ model. The gauge sector is described by Maxwell electrodynamics supplemented by the quartic interaction, while the geometry is kept fixed in the probe approximation. We first analyze the nonlinear electrostatic background and derive an exact first integral of the gauge equation, the associated constitutive relation for the radial electric field, the critical coupling for globally regular solutions, and weak-coupling expansions for the electrostatic potential and chemical potential. We then use these backgrounds to compute the retarded Green's function and spectral function of charged probe fermions. The quartic interaction increases the chemical potential, lowers the effective ratio $T/μ$, shifts the Fermi momentum, and enhances the spectral weight without destroying the holographic Fermi surface. We further show that the fermionic observables inherit the even-power dependence on $α'$ implied by the analytic structure of the gauge sector. The resulting probe-limit analysis makes explicit how quartic bulk nonlinearities propagate from the bulk to boundary fermionic observables.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes fundamental circuit complexity lower bounds for preparing near-ground states of random quantum $p$-spin glasses. By analyzing the required entanglement structure, the authors prove that achieving low energy necessitates deep quantum circuits unless the interaction structure is highly constrained. This offers a probabilistic route to understanding state preparation difficulty in disordered quantum matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, complexity-theoretic lower bound on how efficiently quantum information can be encoded in disordered physical systems, which has implications for understanding quantum simulation and computation limits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central question is determining the circuit complexity (state preparation complexity) required to prepare quantum states near the ground state of random many-body Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** The authors prove that for quantum $p$-spin glasses, preparing near-ground states requires a minimum circuit depth of $\Omega(p \log n)$ in the growing average degree regime, and establishes a fixed-depth obstruction in the bounded average degree regime.

**Method.** The analysis uses techniques involving studying the 'residual process' relative to product states and applying concentration inequalities (like Gaussian supremum bounds) to derive lower bounds on circuit depth.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quantum $p$-spin glasses, defined by random Hamiltonians acting on $n$ qubits via Pauli strings. The model is analyzed in different regimes: mean-field, growing average degree, and bounded average degree.

**Key observables.** Circuit depth ($D$), normalized energy of the prepared state relative to the true ground state optimum, and the residual term quantifying deviation from product states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits ($n$), spin interaction order ($p$), and parameters defining the sparsity/average degree ($\kappa_n$ or $\zeta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results hold uniformly over circuits with an arbitrary number of ancilla qubits, and comparisons are drawn to related models like code-based Hamiltonians.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by contrasting ground state preparation complexity with product-state achievable energies. It then presents separate proofs for the growing average degree regime (yielding $\Omega(\log n)$ depth) and the bounded average degree regime (yielding fixed-depth obstructions). The context is set by comparing these findings to related problems like NLTS.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A central question in quantum information theory is the circuit complexity of states arising from standard many-body models. We study this question for quantum $p$-spin glasses, random Hamiltonians whose interactions act on $p$-tuples of qubits through Pauli strings. Anschuetz, Gamarnik, and Kiani (arXiv:2404.07231) showed that the optimum energy is separated from the best energy achievable by product states. This leaves open whether shallow circuits can close the gap, since even depth-one circuits can generate entanglement.   We show that the entanglement needed to close the product-state gap cannot be generated at shallow depth. When the average interaction degree grows with $n$, we prove that, for all sufficiently large fixed $p$, any circuit preparing an $n$-qubit state whose normalized energy is within a fixed positive constant of the optimum must have depth $Ω_p(\log n)$. In the bounded-average-degree regime, we prove a fixed-depth obstruction: for every fixed $D$, a sufficiently large degree prefactor rules out depth-$D$ preparation of near-ground states. Both results hold uniformly over circuits with an arbitrary number of ancilla qubits.   Our results give an obstruction in the spirit of the No Low-Energy Trivial States problem of Freedman and Hastings (arXiv:1301.1363), but for random quantum spin glasses rather than code-based Hamiltonians such as those of Anshu, Breuckmann, and Nirkhe (arXiv:2206.13228), whose ground states admit polynomial-size preparation circuits. This setting opens a probabilistic route to NLTS-like questions: we recast state-preparation lower bounds for random quantum Hamiltonians as uniform control of Gaussian processes indexed by shallow circuits.

</details>

**Authors:** Meng-Li Guo, Nannan Guan, Bo Li, Bin Hu, Shao-Ming Fei

**Summary.** This paper introduces imaginarity as a quantifiable resource distinct from total quantum coherence. Using geometric decomposition and operational protocols, the authors establish fundamental trade-off relations showing that coherence is bounded by the sum of its imaginary part (imaginarity) and a residual term. This provides crucial theoretical insights for managing resources in distributed quantum technologies.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep theoretical understanding of how different mathematical components of quantum state structure relate to physical capabilities, offering concrete resource management tools applicable to quantum communication and computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a rigorous framework to quantify imaginarity as a fundamental, quantifiable resource within quantum coherence.

**Main result.** It proves that total coherence is bounded by the sum of its purely imaginary component (imaginarity) and a residual component related to basis misalignment. Furthermore, it provides an operational protocol to convert imaginarity into usable coherence.

**Method.** The authors use geometric decomposition techniques based on metric distances (like trace distance) to partition quantum states and derive fundamental trade-off inequalities between different resource measures.

**Model / system.** The analysis is performed on general quantum states, extending from single qubits to bipartite systems. Dynamics are characterized under diagonal Hamiltonians, showing complementary oscillations of imaginarity and residual coherence.

**Key observables.** Quantum Coherence ($C(ho)$), Imaginarity ($M(ho)$), Residual Coherence ($CR(ho)$), and total correlations in bipartite settings.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relationship $C(ho) \le M(ho) + CR(ho)$ is the central constraint, with specific bounds derived for qubit states using trace distance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on defining resource measures via metric distances and assumes physical admissibility of operations for conversion protocols.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometric relationship $C \le M + CR$ and provide a schematic circuit diagram detailing an operational protocol to convert imaginarity in one subsystem to coherence in another.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining resource measures geometrically, establishing fundamental trade-off theorems for single and bipartite systems, providing explicit conversion protocols (operational proofs), and finally characterizing the dynamics under specific Hamiltonians.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish a rigorous framework that identifies imaginarity as a fundamental resource inherent in quantum coherence. By means of a geometric decomposition, we partition coherence into distinct imaginarity and residual components, thereby revealing a universal hierarchical relationship among these resources. For bipartite systems, this decomposition provides explicit bounds on the extent to which nonlocal correlations and imaginarity limit local coherence generation. Furthermore, we devise an explicit operational protocol that converts imaginarity into usable coherence, demonstrating the direct interconvertibility of these resources under physically admissible operations. The dynamical evolution under diagonal Hamiltonians is fully characterized, showing that while total coherence is conserved, imaginarity and residual coherence exhibit complementary oscillations. Our results provide a rigorous geometric and operational characterization of imaginarity as a fundamental constituent of quantum coherence, offering concrete insights for resource management in distributed quantum technologies. The geometric framework and theoretical bounds established herein are fully general, while the explicit conversion protocol and dynamical analysis serve as a compelling proof-of-principle demonstration in qubit systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an advanced quantum protocol for lock-in detection by utilizing successive adiabatic evolution to generate ideal triangular modulation functions. This approach fundamentally overcomes spectral leakage issues present in current methods, enabling the robust and accurate measurement of weak alternating signals. The technique is demonstrated using NV centers in diamond and is presented as a general framework applicable to various controllable quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a sophisticated theoretical framework for quantum sensing that directly addresses fundamental limitations (spectral leakage) in measurement protocols. The reliance on adiabatic passage connects concepts from quantum control theory to real-world high-sensitivity measurements, which is highly relevant to AMO physics research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is achieving accurate quantum lock-in detection for weak signals buried in noise, overcoming limitations imposed by spectral leakage inherent in existing pulse-based control protocols.

**Main result.** The authors propose and demonstrate a general protocol using successive adiabatic evolution that implements triangular modulation, fundamentally solving spectral leakage and providing a robust sensing method.

**Method.** The technique relies on adiabatically controlling the time evolution of a quantum probe through carefully designed sequences of Hamiltonian parameters to extract signal components via accumulated relative phases.

**Model / system.** The model uses a driven two-level quantum probe described by a Hamiltonian incorporating control and signal terms. Practical implementations are demonstrated using nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in diamond, but the protocol is generalizable to other controllable two-level systems.

**Key observables.** The key observables measured are relative accumulated phases ($\alpha$ and $eta$) derived from successive quantum evolution steps, which are proportional to integrals involving the unknown signal $M(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the control field strengths ($\Omega_R(t), \Delta(t)$) and the modulation function shape (achieving triangular profiles). The method is robust against experimental imperfections.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption involves treating the signal coupling term ($H_{	ext{sig}}(t)$) as a weak perturbation during the adiabatic evolution process. It also assumes the applicability of the rotating-wave approximation.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes detail mathematical derivations involving propagators $U(T, 0)$ calculated through sequential application of time-evolution operators for different measurement components ($I_{	ext{sig}}$ and $Q_{	ext{sig}}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of current quantum sensing methods. It then introduces the general protocol based on successive adiabatic evolution to achieve triangular modulation. The methodology is detailed by deriving the necessary propagators for measuring in-phase and quadrature components, followed by a discussion of its practical realization using NV centers.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In recent years, quantum lock-in detection has emerged as a promising technique to accurately detect weak signals submerged in background noise. However, the signal-to-noise ratio of existing protocols is severely limited by spectral leakage resulting from control operations implemented in pulse form. Here, we propose a general protocol for realizing quantum lock-in detection by employing successive quantum adiabatic evolution. In our protocol, the signal modulation is achieved by adiabatically controlling the time evolution of the quantum probe, which enables the implementation of triangular modulation functions. The realization of triangular-wave modulation fundamentally solves the problem of spectral leakage and facilitates the extraction of the complete characteristics of the target signals. We present a practical implementation scheme of adiabatic quantum lock-in detection based on nitrogen-vacancy centers in diamond, and demonstrate that the proposed protocol possesses strong resilience against experimental imperfections. Our results establish adiabatic quantum lock-in detection as a robust and experimentally accessible approach to detection of weak alternating signals in noisy environments, thus promoting the advance of real-world quantum sensing technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally confirms the presence of sizable bond-dependent Kitaev interactions in NiI2, a triangular antiferromagnet. By combining neutron scattering and magnetization data with advanced spin-wave theory, the authors establish that these crucial interactions are mediated by the ligand ions' strong SOC rather than the magnetic metal ion itself. This discovery significantly expands the predicted material landscape for realizing quantum spin liquid physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding that ligand SOC can mediate Kitaev physics provides a new theoretical pathway for designing quantum magnets, which is highly relevant for understanding emergent quantum phenomena in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how bond-dependent anisotropic interactions, such as Kitaev terms, can be realized in magnetic materials where the primary magnetic ions have weak spin-orbit coupling.

**Main result.** The authors provide compelling experimental evidence for a ligand-driven Kitaev mechanism in NiI2, showing that strong SOC on the non-magnetic ligand ions can induce significant bond anisotropy. This broadens the scope of potential Kitaev materials beyond those with intrinsically strong SOC on the magnetic ion.

**Method.** A combination of inelastic neutron scattering (INS), magnetization measurements, and first-principles calculations are used to constrain an effective spin Hamiltonian analyzed via linear spin-wave theory (LSWT).

**Model / system.** The system is NiI2, a van der Waals magnet with S=1 spins arranged on a triangular lattice. The model incorporates Heisenberg exchange ($J$), Kitaev ($K$), and off-diagonal ($\Gamma$) interactions.

**Key observables.** Inelastic neutron scattering (INS) magnon dispersion curves (showing gaps and kinks), magnetic transition temperatures, and the canting angle of the magnetic ground state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Optimal fitted parameters include $J_1 \approx -5.86$ meV, $K \approx 3.33$ meV, $\Gamma \approx 0.37$ meV, and a resulting canting angle near $53^\circ$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that conventional Heisenberg interactions alone are insufficient to explain the observed magnetic anisotropy and ground state.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic magnetic structure, Kitaev geometry, magnetization data ($\chi(T)$ vs $H$), and the calculated/measured magnon dispersion curves showing characteristic gaps and kinks.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by analyzing multiple experimental observables (magnetization, INS) to constrain an effective spin Hamiltonian. LSWT is then used to fit the resulting parameters ($J_1, K, \Gamma$, etc.) against the measured spectral features, confirming the necessity of ligand-driven anisotropic terms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The bond-dependent anisotropic Kitaev interactions are the key for the Kitaev model, which has attracted intense interest for its potential to host quantum-spin-liquid states and fractional excitations. However, experimental realizations of such interactions remain scarce. Here, we investigate the magnetic excitations of NiI$_2$, a van der Waals magnet with spin $S=1$. By combining inelastic neutron scattering, magnetization measurements, magnetic structure analysis, first-principles calculations, and linear-spin-wave simulations, we identify a minimal model that features substantial Kitaev and off-diagonal $Γ$ interactions, which together stabilize the canted magnetic ground state and open a gap in the spin-wave spectrum. Notably, these interactions arise from strong spin-orbit coupling on the ligand ions, despite the quenched orbital moment of the magnetic Ni$^{2+}$ ions. Our results provide compelling experimental evidence for the ligand-driven Kitaev mechanism. This demonstrates a concrete pathway to generating strong bond-dependent anisotropy in systems where the magnetic ions themselves have weak spin-orbit coupling, thereby substantially broadening the range of potential Kitaev materials.

</details>

### [Adaptive Entanglement Management in Quantum Multi-Core Architectures](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14592v1)

**Authors:** Rajeswari Suance P S, Anubhab Dutta, Ruchika Gupta, John Jose

**Summary.** This paper analyzes entanglement management strategies for multi-core quantum processors communicating via a Quantum Network-on-Chip. It compares reactive, proactive, and an adaptive approach (ACGP) for generating necessary entangled pairs. The results show that ACGP significantly minimizes communication latency while maintaining high fidelity through background purification.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on optimizing communication overhead through adaptive resource scheduling is highly relevant to scaling quantum computation architectures and managing decoherence effects in large-scale quantum networks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Efficient entanglement generation is critical for performance in multi-core quantum processors because inter-core communication relies on entanglement-assisted quantum teleportation.

**Main result.** The proposed Adaptive Continuous Pre-Generation (ACGP) strategy significantly reduces average teleportation latency compared to reactive or uniformly proactive methods. Furthermore, entanglement purification can restore fidelity lost during pre-generation with minimal performance penalty.

**Method.** A comparative study evaluating three entanglement management paradigms—On-Demand Generation (ODG), Continuous Pre-Generation (CGP), and ACGP—using simulation on mesh-based architectures.

**Model / system.** The system models multi-core quantum processors interconnected via a Quantum Network-on-Chip (NoC). Inter-core communication is simulated using entanglement-assisted quantum teleportation across various mesh topologies ($2	imes1$ to $8	imes8$).

**Key observables.** Average teleportation latency (ms) and Entanglement Fidelity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mesh size ($m 	imes n$), coherence time, and the degree of adaptation in entanglement generation probability.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes all-to-all qubit connectivity within each core for simplification; pre-generation introduces fidelity degradation due to storage time.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare average teleportation latency across ODG, CGP, and ACGP for various topologies (Fig. 3), and show entanglement fidelity comparisons (Fig. 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for multi-core quantum systems $ightarrow$ identifies entanglement generation as the bottleneck $ightarrow$ compares three management protocols (ODG, CGP, ACGP) using simulation on benchmark circuits $ightarrow$ demonstrates ACGP's superior performance in latency while addressing fidelity loss via purification.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Scalable quantum computing architectures increasingly rely on multi-core designs, where qubits are distributed across multiple processing cores interconnected through a quantum Network-on-Chip (NoC). In such systems, inter-core communication is typically realized through entanglement-assisted quantum teleportation, making efficient entanglement generation critical for performance. In this paper, we perform a comparative study of three entanglement management paradigms for multi-core quantum processors: reactive on-demand generation (ODG), proactive continuous pre-generation (CGP), and an adaptive continuous pre-generation approach (ACGP). While ODG generates entanglement only when required, CGP reduces average teleportation latency by pre-generating EPR pairs in the background. To improve upon this, we propose ACGP which dynamically adjusts entanglement generation probabilities based on observed inter-core communication patterns. We evaluate these approaches using an extended SeQUeNCe simulator on mesh-based multi-core architectures on real benchmark circuits. Results show that ACGP significantly reduces average teleportation latency compared to ODG and CGP. Although pre-generation introduces fidelity degradation due to storage time, entanglement purification effectively restores fidelity with minimal impact on latency. These results demonstrate that adaptive entanglement managements can substantially improve communication efficiency in scalable quantum multi-core systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents an experimental demonstration of a metropolitan-scale quantum network using a q-ROADM to dynamically distribute entanglement among multiple users. It proves the feasibility of flexible, service-oriented networking by supporting full-mesh topologies and complex multi-protocol tasks like secure user onboarding (SIAT). This work advances the practical realization of scalable quantum internet infrastructure over existing optical fiber.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the physical implementation challenges of quantum networking by proposing a reconfigurable hardware solution (q-ROADM), which is highly relevant for researchers working on photonic quantum information infrastructure and network protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge addressed is achieving scalable, dynamic entanglement distribution necessary for multi-user and multi-protocol quantum networks.

**Main result.** A quantum reconfigurable optical add-drop multiplexer (q-ROADM) architecture was demonstrated to enable stable, service-oriented entanglement distribution over metropolitan fiber, supporting full-mesh connectivity and complex protocols like SIAT.

**Method.** The authors combine hardware demonstration using a q-ROADM with theoretical analysis of key rates and time complexity for establishing authenticated links.

**Model / system.** The system is an entanglement-based quantum communication network built upon deployed metropolitan fiber, utilizing a q-ROADM to dynamically allocate polarization-entangled photon pairs from a broadband source to multiple users (up to six).

**Key observables.** Secret Key Rate (SKR), Quantum Bit Error Rate (QBER), and total time required for establishing authenticated links (SIAT time).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Source heralding efficiency ($\approx 15\%$ favourable, $\approx 3\%$ challenging), detector jitter ($< 100 	ext{ ps}$ favourable), and security parameters ($\epsilon_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the full link-dependent ratio for authentication can be approximated by the time-tagging contribution, and performance is compared between ideal (favourable) and realistic (challenging) operating conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the 6-user network architecture using q-ROADM components. Key plots show the stable SKR of all links over $>150$ hours in full-mesh mode, and time analyses compare total SIAT time for different security levels ($\epsilon_c$) and operational conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for dynamic distribution using a q-ROADM. It then details experimental demonstrations of stable multi-user connectivity (full/partial mesh) and compares key generation rates. Finally, it analyzes the time complexity required to establish secure links using the SIAT protocol across varying link conditions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dynamic entanglement distribution is a key requirement for scalable, multi-user and multi-protocol quantum networks. We demonstrate a metropolitan-scale entanglement-based quantum communication network enabled by a quantum reconfigurable optical add-drop multiplexer (q-ROADM), which dynamically distributes polarisation-entangled photon pairs from a broadband source to six users over deployed campus and metropolitan fibre. The network supports programmable full-mesh, partial-mesh and sliced sub-network configurations, enabling flexible allocation of entanglement resources according to link condition and service requirement. We demonstrate stable six-user full-mesh operation over more than 150 hours, compare full-mesh and time-shared partial-mesh strategies under different source and detector conditions, and realise quantum network slicing with optional/additional interconnection links. We also show that the same infrastructure can support different quantum protocols by showcasing Secure Inaugural Authentication-Transfer (SIAT) combined with Network flooding over multiple paths to improve the security of onboarding a new user. These results demonstrate a q-ROADM-enabled entanglement distribution architecture as a novel route towards reconfigurable, service-oriented quantum networking over optical fibre infrastructure.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the numerical instability and high cost associated with calculating gradients during PEPS optimization. By employing implicit differentiation techniques on the underlying contraction environment, the authors create a more stable and computationally efficient method for gradient computation. This advancement significantly simplifies implementing advanced variational quantum algorithms for many-body physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful mathematical tool (implicit differentiation) that directly addresses computational bottlenecks in simulating complex quantum many-body systems using tensor networks, which is crucial for modern condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is efficiently and stably calculating the energy gradient for variational optimization of Projected Entangled-Pair States (PEPS).

**Main result.** The authors propose using implicit differentiation techniques, reformulating the gradient calculation via a characteristic equation to reduce computational cost and eliminate numerical instabilities.

**Method.** They replace direct automatic differentiation through complex contraction subroutines with an algebraic approach based on solving a single characteristic equation derived from the environment contraction.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on variational optimization of PEPS, which is used to approximate ground states for quantum many-body systems on lattices like the infinite square lattice.

**Key observables.** The energy gradient $\partial E/\partial p$, which dictates the optimization path.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The local variational degrees of freedom ($p$) and the environment variables ($x$) defining the PEPS ansatz.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the core contraction step can be accurately characterized by a single algebraic equation $F(y, p) = 0$.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in detail; notes focus on mathematical equations and index structures for tensor contractions (e.g., Equations F2-F8).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the bottleneck of AD in PEPS optimization to introducing implicit differentiation theory, applying it by reformulating the gradient calculation around a characteristic equation, and finally demonstrating its practical simplification.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The current leading approach to the variational optimization of projected entangled-pair states (PEPS) is based on automatic differentiation, which allows for a convenient evaluation of the energy gradient with respect to the local variational degrees of freedom. However, evaluating the energy gradient not only remains a major computational bottleneck of the optimization procedure, but also suffers from frequent numerical instabilities. In this work, we adopt recent advances in implicit differentiation techniques to address these challenges in PEPS optimization. By reformulating the core step of the gradient computation in terms of a single characteristic equation for the contraction environment, we reduce the cost of the gradient computation and improve its scaling with the problem size. By choosing a suitable parametrization of this characteristic equation based on the intrinsic symmetries of the contraction environment, we can directly remove instabilities from the global gradient computation that would otherwise arise from the derivatives of subroutines of the contraction algorithm. Finally, we demonstrate how this approach drastically simplifies the practical implementation of stable gradient-based PEPS optimization.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a comprehensive symmetry-based theory to uncover the microscopic origins of the Spin Hall Effect in ferromagnets. By treating spin-orbit coupling as a perturbation, the authors systematically derive distinct T-even and T-odd contributions to the spin Hall conductivity. The findings clarify how magnetic order dictates the magnitude and nature of these spin current sources for spintronic applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the rigorous use of symmetry group theory to decompose transport coefficients based on fundamental interactions (like SOC) provides a powerful theoretical template applicable across many areas of quantum physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to elucidate the microscopic origins of the Spin Hall Effect (SHE) in collinear ferromagnets, specifically detailing how magnetic order activates distinct spin Hall responses.

**Main result.** It identifies multiple SHE mechanisms—including magnetization-independent and dependent T-even contributions, and a leading T-odd Magnetic Spin Hall Effect (MSHE)—whose origins are determined by the order of SOC perturbation.

**Method.** The study employs symmetry analysis based on magnetic point-group symmetries and treats Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC) as a systematic perturbation to expand the Spin Hall Conductivity (SHC).

**Model / system.** The focus is on collinear ferromagnets, where SOC breaks spin-group symmetry. The theory is validated using first-principles calculations on materials like fcc ferromagnets.

**Key observables.** Spin Hall Conductivity ($\sigma_{ij}^k$), decomposed into Time-Reversal-Even (T-even) and Time-Reversal-Odd (T-odd) components, including the MSHE and Planar Spin Hall Effect (PSHE).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The strength of SOC ($\xi$) is treated as a small parameter for perturbation expansion; magnetization direction ($\mathbf{m}$) dependence is crucial.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes collinear ferromagnetism and develops the theory by treating SOC perturbatively up to second order in the expansion.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spin current generation using an electric field, schematic interactions with magnetization $\mathbf{m}$, and show the calculated dependence of SHCs on SOC scaling factors and magnetization angle ($	heta$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a theoretical framework by applying symmetry constraints to expand the SHC in powers of SOC. It systematically identifies contributions at first order (T-even/T-odd) and second order (PSHE), validating these findings with first-principles calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic materials support both time-reversal-even (T-even) and time-reversal-odd (T-odd) spin Hall currents, yet their underlying microscopic origins remain elusive. Here, we elucidate the spin Hall effect (SHE) in collinear ferromagnets by treating spin-orbit coupling (SOC) as a perturbation that breaks spin-group symmetry, thereby revealing how magnetic order activates distinct spin Hall response. To first order in SOC, we identify two dominant T-even SHE mechanisms: a magnetization-independent conventional contribution and a magnetization-dependent channel associated with anomalous Hall charge transport. At the same order, the leading T-odd magnetic spin Hall effect (MSHE) originates from the exchange interaction between the conventional spin current and the local magnetization. At second order in SOC, we further uncover a distinct T-odd planar spin Hall mechanism. Our spin-symmetry analysis is corroborated by first-principles calculations, which reveal a pronounced anisotropic magnetic spin Hall effect whose magnitude can be comparable to the T-even spin Hall conductivity (SHC) when the magnetic moment is tilted away from the principal crystallographic axes. These findings clarify the microscopic origins of the SHC in collinear ferromagnets and pave the way for ferromagnet-based spin current sources with versatile properties in spintronic applications.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents an experimental demonstration showing how to integrate high-rate continuous-variable QKD with Tb/s classical communication in a shared optical link. By utilizing anti-resonant hollow-core fiber, the system effectively suppresses noise accumulation that typically limits quantum transmission. This approach establishes a promising pathway for realizing secure, high-capacity quantum internet infrastructure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes the boundaries of practical quantum networking by solving the critical engineering problem of integrating weak quantum signals with powerful classical channels over long distances using novel fiber platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is enabling high-rate continuous-variable quantum key distribution (CV-QKD) to operate reliably alongside massive classical optical data streams within a shared fiber infrastructure.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated that using an anti-resonant hollow-core fiber combined with residual-carrier assistance allows for achieving simultaneous rates of 153.22 Mb/s quantum key rate and 7.2 Tb/s classical data rate over 24.3 km.

**Method.** The system employs dual-polarization, high-baudrate modulation (15 Gbaud for QKD) and utilizes advanced digital signal processing (DSP) techniques at the receiver end for phase recovery and equalization.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is an anti-resonant hollow-core fiber (AR-HCF), which confines light primarily in air to minimize nonlinear interactions with silica glass. The quantum channel uses CV-QKD, while the classical channel supports multiple wavelength-division multiplexed channels.

**Key observables.** Secret Key Rate (SKR) ($	ext{Mb/s}$), aggregate data rate ($	ext{Tb/s}$), and sustained positive SKR under high classical launch power.

**Important parameters / regimes.** 24.3 km link length, 15 Gbaud modulation, up to $15 	ext{ dBm}$ classical launch power, and projected extension to a $100	ext{-km-equivalent}$ condition.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The performance relies on the physical advantage of air-core propagation to suppress noise sources like Four-Wave Mixing (FWM) and Spontaneous Raman Scattering (SpRS).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the AR-HCF structure and low attenuation, compare nonlinear noise spectra between SMF and HCFs, and show simulated SKR vs. distance curves demonstrating superior performance in HCF.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the coexistence challenge, then detailing the physical solution (AR-HCF) to mitigate noise, followed by implementing advanced signal processing techniques (DSP/equalization) for robust quantum reception, and finally quantifying the high achievable rates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum key distribution (QKD) can provide secret keys with security rooted in quantum mechanics, but operation alongside high-capacity classical traffic remains limited by the excess-noise budget of weak quantum states in conventional solid-core fiber. Here, we combine ultralow-loss anti-resonant hollow-core fiber with residual-carrier-assisted discrete-modulation continuous-variable QKD (DM-CV-QKD) to address both propagation-induced coexistence noise and low-SNR phase recovery. Over a 24.3-km hollow-core link with 3.3-dB end-to-end loss, a dual-polarization 15-Gbaud DM-CV-QKD channel achieves an average asymptotic secret-key rate (SKR) of 153.22 Mb/s and a finite-size SKR of 149.99 Mb/s, while 39 coherent wavelength-division-multiplexed channels deliver an aggregate data rate of 7.6 Tb/s and a net data rate of 7.2 Tb/s. The system can even sustain a positive SKR under a high classical launch power of up to 15 dBm, without an optical bandpass filter (BPF). Finite-size analysis against collective attacks further yields a projected positive secret-key rate at a 100-km-equivalent condition. These results show that an anti-resonant hollow-core fiber, combined with carrier-assisted phase recovery, can greatly extend the operating regime of shared-fiber quantum-secured coherent links, pointing to a promising approach for integrating high-rate CV-QKD with high-capacity optical networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a novel representation-theoretic framework to derive exact state-independent uncertainty relations for multipartite spin 1/2 systems. By analyzing the total spin variance using Clebsch–Gordan decomposition, the authors establish universal algebraic bounds up to five qubits. These results provide the first unified approach to quantifying fundamental measurement limits in large qubit ensembles.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental algebraic tools (representation theory) to quantify limits on simultaneous measurements in multi-qubit quantum systems, which is central to understanding quantum metrology and entanglement.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing state-independent uncertainty relations (SIURs) for multipartite spin 1/2 systems, specifically focusing on variance-based formulations beyond the bipartite level.

**Main result.** The authors derive exact state-independent variance bounds up to quintipartite systems, establishing a structural dichotomy where odd $n$ enforce strictly positive universal bounds while even $n$ allow for vanishing variance in certain sectors.

**Method.** They employ a representation-theoretic framework utilizing Clebsch–Gordan decomposition and extremal analysis of the total spin variance ($\Delta^2(\mathfrak{su}_2)$).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as multipartite collective spin-$1/2$ systems, analyzed using operators forming representations of $\mathfrak{su}(2)$ on an $n$-qubit Hilbert space.

**Key observables.** Total variance ($\Delta^2(\mathfrak{su}_2)$) and the Casimir operator expectation value are key diagnostics for bounding uncertainty.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($n$), which dictates the complexity of the representation decomposition, is a crucial parameter.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extending known techniques from one- and bi-partite systems to higher multipartite settings via algebraic structure theorems.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the bipartite structure for $(1/2)\otimes(1/2)$, showing variance bounds related to irreducible components $V(0)$ and $V(1)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing bounds for single $su_2$ systems, then analyzing direct sum representations via Clebsch–Gordan decomposition, culminating in the derivation of universal bounds up to quintipartite systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Uncertainty relations quantify fundamental limits on simultaneous measurement of quantum observables. While conventional formulations are state-dependent, state-independent uncertainty relations (SIURs) impose universal bounds determined solely by the algebraic structure of the operators, with applications across metrology, quantum cryptography, and entanglement detection. Despite extensive study, exact analytical variance-based SIURs have so far been established primarily for one- and bi-partite systems, while entropic SIURs have been extended to multipartite and memory-assisted settings through information-theoretic constructions. In contrast, exact variance-based SIURs beyond the bipartite level have remained analytically unresolved.} Here we develop a representation-theoretic framework for multipartite SIURs in collective spin-$\tfrac{1}{2}$ systems. Using the Clebsch--Gordan decomposition and extremal analysis of total spin variance, we derive exact state-independent bounds up to quintipartite systems. A clear structural dichotomy emerges: odd $n$ systems exhibit strictly positive universal bounds (e.g., $Δ^2(\mathfrak{su}_2)\!\ge\!4/11$ for $n=3$), whereas even $n$ admit vanishing variance on trivial sectors but retain positive reduced-space bounds (e.g., $Δ^2(\mathfrak{su}_2)\!\ge\!1/8$ for $n=4$). These results establish the first unified, algebraic framework for multipartite variance-based SIURs in qubit ensembles.

</details>
