**Summary.** The paper generalizes the Kochen-Specker theorem by constructing finite tests to rule out noncontextual models where outcome probabilities are drawn from any arbitrary finite set O. Using sheaf theory and logical gadgets, it proves that quantum mechanics exhibits 'strong $O$-valued contextuality,' demonstrating that such discrete alternatives cannot consistently describe quantum measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep mathematical constraints on the structure of quantum reality, showing that even if one restricts possible outcomes to discrete values beyond $\{0,1\}$, contextuality remains a necessary feature.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To construct finite measurement configurations that rule out noncontextual empirical models whose outcome probabilities are drawn from an arbitrary finite subset O of [0,1], thereby generalizing the Kochen-Specker theorem.

**Main result.** The paper proves a generalized KS theorem showing that quantum theory evades global sections of all finite-valued presheaves defined over such $O$-valued assignments. This rules out many-valued logics as viable ontological models for quantum mechanics.

**Method.** The authors employ sheaf theory, propositional logic (Compactness Theorem), and construct specific 'gadgets' involving vector sets in $\mathbb{C}^3$ to derive contradictions from assumed context-independent probability assignments.

**Model / system.** The focus is on quantum measurements represented by projectors $P \in \mathcal{P}(H)$ on a Hilbert space, testing noncontextual models where outcome probabilities are restricted to an arbitrary finite set $O$.

**Key observables.** Contextuality itself, quantified by the failure of global assignments $\lambda_O: V 	o O$ to satisfy structural constraints derived from measurement compatibility.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the allowed value set $O$, particularly when it includes 0 and 1, and the dimension of the Hilbert space ($d \ge 3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that any alternative theory must assign probabilities from a finite set $O$ to all measurements, allowing for the application of logical compactness arguments.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate specific 'gadget' graphs (e.g., nested Clifton graph) used in the proof structure to enforce constraints on assignments, leading to contradictions when assuming context-independent values.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from Gleason's theorem and KS theorem by first introducing $O$-valued valuations. It then develops constructive tests using Hardy-type measurements (Chunk 2), culminating in a formal proof using sheaf theory and propositional logic gadgets (Chunks 3 & 4) to establish the generalized KS theorem for finite $O$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gleason's theorem identifies the Born rule via non-contextuality over an infinite continuous lattice of projectors, while its corollary the Kochen-Specker (KS) theorem rules out the specific class of deterministic ($\{0,1\}$-valued) noncontextual models using finite sets of projectors. Gleason's theorem also indicates the existence of KS-type finite vector constructions to rule out other discrete nondeterministic alternatives to quantum theory beyond the $\{0, 1\}$ case. Here, we construct finite measurement configurations to rule out noncontextual empirical models with outcome probabilities drawn from an arbitrary finite subset $O \subset [0,1]$. We do this by two means: (i) constructing a family of experimentally feasible state-dependent Hardy-type tests, and (ii) proving a generalized KS theorem for a broad class of $O$ that includes prior results as special cases. In the sheaf-theoretic framework of Abramsky and Brandenburger, a hierarchy of probabilistic-possibilistic-strong contextuality has been established quantifying contextuality as a resource. We extend this framework by introducing strong $O$-valued contextuality, showing that quantum theory evades global sections of all finite-valued presheaves. We also discuss the implications of the result on finite many-valued logics as viable ontological models for quantum theory and for contextuality-based (semi)-device-independent protocols.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a unified Uncertainty Quantification framework designed to benchmark noisy quantum computers across diverse computational tasks. By integrating Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs) and Green's function reconstruction via QSVT, it moves beyond simple hardware metrics. The resulting common benchmark quantifies the reliability of backends to achieve useful task-level results.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantitative method to benchmark quantum hardware beyond simple gate fidelity, directly assessing if the noise profile allows for meaningful physical conclusions in complex applications like many-body physics or chemistry.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a unified Uncertainty Quantification (UQ) framework for application-level benchmarking of noisy quantum backends, bridging Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs) and non-variational methods like QSVT.

**Main result.** The framework provides a common benchmark that measures how reliably different quantum backends can achieve useful task-level behavior across diverse workloads, identifying robust parameter regions and failure modes.

**Method.** It combines Bayesian optimization with posterior distribution refinement to quantify uncertainty for both VQA objective functions and Green's function reconstructions derived via QSVT.

**Model / system.** The work evaluates performance on NISQ quantum hardware backends using two primary workload classes: general variational tasks (chemistry, optimization, PDEs) and spectral reconstruction from time-propagators (Green's functions).

**Key observables.** Backend rankings across workloads, robust parameter regions geometry, residual gaps to ideal behavior, mean regret trajectories, and quality vs. speed Pareto frontiers.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The workload parameters ($\vec{\lambda}$) defining the task observable $\mathcal{O}_w$, the optimization parameters ($oldsymbol{	heta}$), and the noise characteristics of the quantum backend.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on IBM fake backend noise models instead of live QPUs, and assumes that a single statistical interface can unify both variational and non-variational workloads for consistent comparison.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate shared UQ workflows connecting VQA and QSVT branches; they show cross-backend sensitivity summaries (Morris/SHAP), density maps of robust parameter regions, and application-level performance comparisons (Pareto views).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the unified UQ framework by first defining the two workload classes (VQA and QSVT) and then detailing the combined methodology. It presents results comparing backend performance across multiple domains using sensitivity analysis and optimization convergence metrics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present an uncertainty quantification (UQ) framework for application level benchmarking and characterization of noisy quantum backends. The framework compares two workload classes under one statistical pipeline: noisy intermediate scale quantum (NISQ) variational quantum algorithms (VQAs) and Quantum Singular Value Transformation (QSVT) based Green's function reconstruction. For the VQA branch, we evaluate ten benchmark families spanning chemistry, optimization, simulation, compiling, linear solving, partial differential equations, metrology, error correction, tomography, and channel fidelity estimation. For the QSVT branch, we reconstruct orbital resolved Green's functions and spectral peaks from a block encoded real time propagator. The workflow combines Bayesian optimization, posterior distribution refinement, sensitivity analysis, robust parameter density estimation, backend ranking, noise correlation, and resource estimation analysis. Instead of reporting only one best parameter vector, the framework identifies robust parameter regions, residual gaps to ideal behavior, backend specific failure modes, and calibration sensitive uncertainty. The result is a common benchmark for variational and non-variational workloads that measures how reliably each backend reaches useful task level behavior.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies a unified Renormalization Group framework to study self-similar diffusion processes. It demonstrates that whether an initial condition's memory survives the asymptotic limit depends critically on whether the underlying physical model conserves mass. This provides a refined understanding of universality beyond simple scale invariance.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical PDEs, the concept of scale invariance breaking down due to retained initial conditions mirrors complex phenomena in open quantum systems or strongly correlated materials where boundary effects or finite size are crucial.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how information from initial conditions persists or is lost during self-similar evolution governed by nonlinear diffusion equations.

**Main result.** For non-conservative systems, the memory of the initial length scale survives the Renormalization Group (RG) flow; conversely, conservative systems exhibit stronger universality where this memory is lost asymptotically.

**Method.** A unified Renormalization Group (RG) framework is employed to analyze scaling dynamics by treating asymptotic solutions as functions of all relevant length scales.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on self-similar diffusion processes, specifically the Density-Dependent Diffusion (DDD) model and its modified version, which are nonlinear partial differential equations.

**Key observables.** Memory retention of initial length scales ($l$ or $X_0$), scaling exponents ($eta$, $q$), and the functional dependence on multiple length scales in the asymptotic solution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Diffusion parameters ($\kappa$), anomalous dimension ($q$), and initial scale factors ($m_0, l$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that self-similar solutions can be characterized by RG Fixed Points (FPs) derived from scale transformations.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the unified RG framework for scaling dynamics, applies it to the DDD model showing memory loss in the conservative case, and then analyzes the modified DDD model where non-conservation forces the retention of initial condition memory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Universality is usually associated with asymptotic behavior becoming independent of details of the initial state. Using a unified renormalization-group (RG) framework for self-similar dynamics, we show that retaining relevant length scales leads naturally to distinct classes of memory-retaining fixed points. A modified density-dependent diffusion model reveals a general scaling structure in which initial-condition information remains asymptotically relevant, with the Barenblatt equation emerging as a special case. These results provide a new perspective on universality and anomalous scaling.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel end-to-end quantum algorithm designed to simulate weakly nonlinear plasma physics, which is classically demanding. It overcomes key quantum bottlenecks by employing advanced techniques like Lyapunov transforms and hierarchical block-encoding for dense matrices. The resulting algorithm promises exponential memory savings and superquadratic time speedups over existing classical spectral methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on plasma physics, the core technical contributions—developing block-encoding for nonlinear dynamics and achieving superquadratic speedups in quantum simulation of complex PDEs—are highly relevant to simulating general non-equilibrium many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing an end-to-end quantum algorithm capable of simulating weakly nonlinear plasma physics with a superquadratic speedup over classical methods.

**Main result.** The proposed quantum algorithm achieves rigorous convergence guarantees, yielding exponential memory savings and superquadratic time improvements relative to standard spectral solvers for the kinetic energy estimation.

**Method.** The approach combines three novel ingredients: using a Lyapunov transform for an exponentially converging Carleman linear embedding, developing a hierarchical block-encoding protocol for dense matrices, and introducing a specialized subroutine for information extraction from the full history state.

**Model / system.** The system is a 3D electron–ion plasma modeled by the Vlasov-Poisson system. The dynamics are simplified via Fourier–Hermite truncation to a high-dimensional quadratic ordinary differential equation, analyzed in a controlled perturbative regime.

**Key observables.** Spacetime-averaged kinetic energy moment ($\langle K angle$), and the nonlinearity strength ($\varphi_{\max}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonlinearity strength $\varphi(x, t)$, Fourier cutoff ($N_F$), Hermite cutoff ($N_H$), simulation time $T$, and error tolerance $\epsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analytical convergence guarantees are strictly limited to nonlinearities significantly weaker than those typically encountered in practice ($\varphi_{\max} \ll 1$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the convergence regimes based on nonlinearity strength ($\varphi_{\max}$), showing transitions between analytically certified Carleman-convergence and breakdown limits for different initial data types (Fourier vs. Gaussian).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model, addresses quantum bottlenecks (nonlinear embedding, dense encoding), presents the combined algorithmic framework with complexity analysis, and concludes by deriving bounds on key parameters like $\varphi_{\max}$ and estimating observables like $\langle K angle$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonlinear kinetic plasma simulation is high-dimensional and classically demanding, while quantum algorithms face different bottlenecks: embedding nonlinear dynamics into a linear computation, loading dense field-interaction data, and efficiently extracting information. We present an end-to-end quantum algorithm, with rigorous convergence guarantees, for a weakly nonlinear kinetic plasma model. The system describes a 3D electron-ion plasma with adiabatic electrons, kinetic ions, Debye screening, and Krook relaxation. After Fourier-Hermite truncation, the dynamics reduces to a high-dimensional quadratic ordinary differential equation. To tackle quantum bottlenecks we combine three key ingredients. First, we use a plasma free energy to identify a Lyapunov transform under which a Carleman linear embedding converges exponentially in the truncation order within a certified weakly nonlinear regime. Second, we develop a hierarchical block-encoding protocol for dense matrices, exploiting the spatial decay of the field to avoid polynomial overhead from sparse access encodings. Third, we introduce a subroutine for information extraction that exploits nonlinear components encoded in the full Carleman history state to improve the estimation of linear observables. We construct a quantum algorithm to estimate the spacetime-averaged kinetic energy using $\widetilde{O}\!\left( N_F N_H^{1/2} \operatorname{polylog}\!\left(\frac{T}ε\right)\frac{1}ε\right)$ gates and $\widetilde{O}\!\left(\log\!\left(N_F N_H^{1/2}T\right)\log\!\left(\frac{1}ε\right)\right)$ qubits, where $N_F$ and $N_H$ are the Fourier and Hermite cutoffs. Relative to a Fourier-Hermite spectral solver, this yields exponential memory savings and superquadratic improvements in time. Together, these results establish a controlled nonlinear plasma benchmark for quantum simulation.

</details>

**Summary.** This review details Liquid Transport Growth (LTG), an advanced crystal growth technique superior to conventional methods for synthesizing complex quantum materials. LTG achieves this by spatially separating the dissolution and precipitation of material using a molten flux under a temperature gradient. The work highlights how this method allows precise control over stoichiometry and defect chemistry, paving the way for predictive synthesis protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on materials synthesis, the core concept of controlling non-equilibrium phase boundaries via imposed gradients relates to open quantum systems dynamics and driven processes in condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing advanced, controllable techniques for synthesizing high-quality single crystals of diverse quantum materials.

**Main result.** Liquid Transport Growth (LTG) offers significant advantages over conventional flux methods by spatially separating dissolution and crystallization zones, enabling the growth of temperature-sensitive compounds with higher yields.

**Method.** The authors review LTG, a horizontal flux growth technique that uses a molten flux under an imposed temperature gradient to control crystal precipitation.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the crystal growth process itself, modeled by controlling solute transport between a hot dissolution end and a cooler crystallization end. The materials studied include various quantum compounds like $	ext{UTe}_2$, $	ext{MoTe}_2$, and $	ext{LuNb}_6	ext{Sn}_6$.

**Key observables.** Crystal yield, stoichiometry, defect concentration, physical properties (superconductivity, electronic structure).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature gradient ($\Delta T$), crystallization temperature (ideally constant), solute transport rate, and initial charge/flux ratio.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that separating dissolution and crystallization zones allows for independent control over growth conditions, overcoming equilibrium solubility constraints.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically compares CVT, LTG, and conventional flux growth. Figure 2 details the LTG setup and results for $	ext{Fe}_3	ext{Sn}_2$, while Figure 3 does the same for $	ext{CrTe}_3$.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces LTG as an improvement over conventional methods, details its mechanism by separating dissolution/precipitation zones, provides case studies ($	ext{Fe}_3	ext{Sn}_2$, $	ext{CrTe}_3$), and concludes by outlining future work to make the technique predictive rather than empirical.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Liquid transport growth (LTG) is a horizontal flux growth technique that is closely analogous to chemical vapor transport, with the key distinction that a molten flux rather than a vapor serves as the transport agent. Unlike conventional flux growth, LTG spatially separates charge dissolution and crystal precipitation and couples them through continuous solute transport under a deliberately imposed temperature gradient. This enables crystal growth to begin before the charge is completely dissolved, removes the equilibrium solubility constraint on the starting charge/flux ratio, and allows large yields of single crystals to be obtained from a single growth. Recent studies have further shown that by spatially separating dissolution and crystallization and maintaining crystallization at a nearly constant temperature, LTG is particularly effective for two classes of materials: compounds that crystallize only within a narrow temperature and/or composition window, and compounds whose stoichiometry, defect concentration, and thus physical properties are sensitive to the crystallization temperature. In this review, we discuss representative examples including Fe$_3$Sn$_2$, CrTe$_3$, YFe$_2$Ge$_2$, UTe$_2$, CeRh$_2$As$_2$, MoTe$_2$, WTe$_2$, and LuNb$_6$Sn$_6$ to illustrate the unique capabilities of LTG for producing high quality single crystals of diverse quantum materials. We also summarize practical considerations for LTG experimental design, including furnace selection, growth time, melt stability, and ampoule geometry, and discuss future opportunities for transforming LTG from an empirical growth method into a more predictive crystal growth technique.

</details>

**Summary.** This review article thoroughly examines Persson's theory for modeling contact mechanics between rough surfaces. It details how the stochastic nature of surface roughness is incorporated by deriving a differential equation that governs the evolution of the contact pressure probability density function with increasing magnification scale. The work aims to provide researchers with a comprehensive understanding of this state-of-the-art multi-scale theoretical framework.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical mechanics, the rigorous use of stochastic processes (stochastic differential equations) to model system evolution based on scale separation ($\zeta$) shares mathematical parallels with analyzing open quantum systems or complex many-body dynamics where coarse-graining is necessary.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary problem is modeling tribological phenomena arising from rough surface contact by integrating the multi-scale nature of surface texture into contact mechanics theory.

**Main result.** The paper details Persson's theory, which develops a differential equation describing how the probability density function (PDF) of contact pressure evolves deterministically with increasing magnification ($\zeta$).

**Method.** The methodology involves characterizing rough topography using stochastic processes and solving partial differential equations to track the evolution of the contact pressure PDF.

**Model / system.** The system is the contact between two uncorrelated rough surfaces. The model centers on Persson's theory, treating the variation as a Markov process whose statistics are governed by diffusion-like equations.

**Key observables.** Interfacial gap, real area of contact, interfacial contact stiffness, and the magnification-dependent Probability Density Function (PDF) of contact pressure $P(p, \zeta)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnification $\zeta = \lambda_l/\lambda_s$, upper cut-off wavelength ($\lambda_l$), lower cut-off wavelength ($\lambda_s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The roughness is assumed to be random, bandwidth-limited, self-affine, and isotropic. Key assumptions include that the pressure variation follows a Markov process.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Power Spectral Density (PSD) of rough topography across various length scales and map the historical development of multi-scale contact models leading up to Persson's theory.

**Paper structure.** The review first introduces the background of Persson's theory, thoroughly discusses its application to purely normal elastic contact by solving the PDF evolution via PDEs, examines subsequent applications in other tribological fields, and concludes with recommendations for future research directions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The stochastic process model of rough surface contact, widely known as Persson's theory of contact, serves as a representative multi-scale model that has been extensively applied across various fields of tribology. In this chapter, we briefly introduce the background of the development of Persson's theory of contact. We thoroughly discuss Persson's theory for purely normal elastic contact, with a special focus on solving the probability density of the contact pressure and the interfacial gap using partial differential equations. Subsequent applications of these fundamental results in addressing more complex interfacial properties in other fields of tribology are also examined. Finally, several recommendations regarding future studies of Persson's theory are proposed. This review article is expected to assist researchers in quickly familiarizing themselves with the current state of the art of Persson's theory and to attract more attention from tribologists and solid mechanicians, thereby contributing to the development and application of Persson's theory of contact.

</details>

**Summary.** The research proposes using machine learning to sense the plaquette phase in a three-level atomic system by analyzing how this unknown phase perturbs STIRAP population transfer efficiencies. By training an MLP on simulated data, the authors demonstrate that these accessible quantum control measurements encode enough information to accurately reconstruct the underlying gauge-invariant phase.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, modern example of leveraging machine learning in quantum physics—specifically turning a difficult-to-measure physical parameter (a gauge phase) into a measurable diagnostic signal via controlled dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the quantum sensing of the plaquette phase ($\phi$) within a three-level $\Delta$ system, which is a gauge-invariant quantity affecting system dynamics.

**Main result.** A Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) trained on simulated STIRAP population transfer efficiencies can accurately reconstruct the unknown plaquette phase with high fidelity.

**Method.** The methodology involves framing the problem as an inverse mapping, using supervised machine learning to predict $\phi$ from multiple measured quantum control observables.

**Model / system.** The system is a three-level $\Delta$-type atomic configuration described by a Hamiltonian that includes the unknown plaquette phase. The dynamics are probed using Stimulated Raman Adiabatic Passage (STIRAP) protocols.

**Key observables.** Stimulated Raman Adiabatic Passage (STIRAP) population transfer efficiencies ($\eta$), which depend non-trivially on the plaquette phase $\phi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The plaquette phase $\phi$, system coupling strengths ($\Omega_0, \Omega_p, \Omega_s$), and detuning parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that the STIRAP efficiency measurements provide sufficient information to uniquely determine the unknown gauge-invariant phase $\phi$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the three-level system setup; one shows the dependence of STIRAP efficiency on $\phi$, and later figures demonstrate the convergence and test performance of the MLP model in reconstructing the true phase.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the physical model and the concept of the plaquette phase. It then details how this phase breaks Coherent Population Trapping (CPT) and influences STIRAP dynamics. The core is the development and training of an MLP using simulated data derived from these dynamic measurements to achieve phase estimation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a machine-learning-empowered approach to the quantum sensing of the plaquette phase, a gauge-invariant quantity arising in three-level $Δ$ systems. This phase profoundly affects the system dynamics, breaking coherent population trapping and inducing a non-trivial phase dependence of the dynamics. We demonstrate that a multi-layer perceptron (MLP), trained in a supervised-learning framework, can accurately estimate the plaquette phase from STImulated Raman Adiabatic Passage (STIRAP) population transfer efficiencies measured under different driving conditions, which provide experimentally accessible observables. Our results highlight how the combination of coherent control and machine learning (ML) enables effective phase identification in closed-loop quantum systems, opening new perspectives for quantum technologies, specifically quantum sensing applications including synthetic gauge fields.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the computational bottleneck of moment optimization in the NPA hierarchy by treating moment selection as a combinatorial search problem. The authors develop and compare advanced machine learning and sampling techniques (RBM, BO) that efficiently select optimal subsets of moments. This establishes a scalable framework for obtaining tighter, certified bounds on quantum correlations and energy gaps.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial computational tool for rigorously bounding physical quantities in quantum systems where exact calculation is intractable due to high dimensionality or non-commutativity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is optimizing moment selection within the Navascués-Pironio-Acín (NPA) hierarchy to efficiently bound solutions to noncommutative polynomial optimization problems.

**Main result.** The developed framework allows for scalable moment selection, significantly improving certified bounds on quantum correlations and ground-state energies by orders of magnitude compared to greedy or full brute-force methods.

**Method.** The authors reframe moment selection as a combinatorial subset selection problem and compare three optimization techniques: Parallel Tempering (PT), RBM-based Reinforcement Learning, and Bayesian Optimization (BO).

**Model / system.** Applications include bounding Bell inequalities (e.g., $I_{3322}$) and certifying ground-state energies for many-body Hamiltonians like the one-dimensional Heisenberg spin chain.

**Key observables.** Bound quality (gap $\Delta C$), synergy diagnostic ($\Delta(S_k)$), and convergence behavior across different Bell inequalities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Computational budget (fixed resource constraint) and moment selection level ($n$ in the NPA hierarchy).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The NPA relaxation sequence provides an outer approximation to the true quantum expectation value, but its computational cost grows combinatorially with the number of moments.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare optimization strategies (PT) for energy and observable gap certification on the Heisenberg chain, showing that warm-starting from local solutions significantly improves bounds for long-range correlations.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the NPA framework, identifies moment selection as a combinatorial challenge, introduces synergy diagnostics, and then develops/compares advanced optimization algorithms (PT, RBM, BO) using quantum information benchmarks like Bell inequalities and spin chains.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Navascués-Pironio-Acín (NPA) hierarchy provides a convergent sequence of semidefinite programming (SDP) relaxations for noncommutative polynomial optimisation, ubiquitous in quantum physics. However, its practical applicability is limited by the combinatorial growth in operator moments required at each level. Since not all moments contribute equally to bound tightness, selecting moments within a fixed computational budget is a relevant problem.   We reframe moment selection as combinatorial subset selection and show it is governed by strong higher-order synergistic interactions among moments, quantified through a marginal synergy diagnostic adapted from complex systems theory. We develop and compare three optimisation methods: Parallel Tempering (PT), an RBM-based reinforcement learning policy, and Bayesian Optimisation (BO). On the $I_{3322}$ Bell inequality benchmark, all three substantially outperform greedy approaches at costs around two orders of magnitude below brute force, with the RBM achieving the closest approach to optimal throughout the hard transition regime.   We apply the framework to the 174 Bell inequalities in the $(4,4,2,2)$ scenario, finding heterogeneous convergence behaviour across inequalities, and to the one-dimensional Heisenberg spin chain, demonstrating that physically motivated monomial bases are internally compressible and are not globally optimal in general. A budget-aware search over a broader pool improves certified bounds on long-range correlations by nearly two orders of magnitude. These results establish a scalable framework for moment selection in noncommutative polynomial optimisation, with broad applications across quantum physics and quantum information.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes a quantum remote implementation protocol that operates on two-qubit hyperstates by simultaneously leveraging both polarization and spatial degrees of freedom. The scheme uses cross-Kerr nonlinear interactions and homodyne measurements, demonstrating enhanced performance over previous methods. The analysis rigorously accounts for realistic noise sources like dissipation, confirming the feasibility of hybrid quantum communication.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, resource-efficient blueprint for hybrid quantum operation implementation in photonic systems, directly relevant to developing distributed quantum information processing networks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of remotely implementing an arbitrary hybrid quantum operator on a single-photon two-qubit hyperstate.

**Main result.** A novel protocol is proposed that successfully utilizes both polarization and spatial degrees of freedom simultaneously, significantly enhancing performance compared to schemes using only one DOF.

**Method.** The method involves entangling the unknown state with a shared hyperentangled resource via cross-Kerr nonlinear interactions and subsequent homodyne measurements on a probe coherent state.

**Model / system.** The system uses two-qubit hyperentangled states encoded across polarization (H/V) and spatial modes ($x0/x1$). The protocol is realized using linear optical elements and cross-Kerr nonlinear interactions, making it compatible with photonic quantum communication.

**Key observables.** Success probability ($P_{	ext{succ}}$), which is analyzed in the presence of measurement errors and dissipation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cross-Kerr phase shift ($	heta$), coherent state amplitude ($\alpha$), and environmental dissipation rate ($\gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis explicitly models and accounts for two sources of error: finite distinguishability of coherent states during homodyne detection, and coherent state dissipation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the protocol components (Fig. 1), summarize local Pauli operations based on measurement outcomes (Table II), and show 3D landscapes demonstrating how $P_{	ext{succ}}$ is affected by $\alpha$, dissipation ($D$), and $	heta$ (Fig. 2, Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper details the protocol steps: initial state transformation using cross-Kerr interaction, measurement on an auxiliary coherent state to generate entanglement, conditional local operations, application of the desired operator $U$, and final recovery via Pauli corrections.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum remote control, also known as quantum remote implementation of an operator (QRIO), enables the remote manipulation of an arbitrary quantum state by implementing a desired quantum operation at a distant location. Significant progress has recently been made in developing QRIO protocols and their variants. Most existing schemes employ hyperentangled states where entanglement is shared across multiple degrees of freedom (DOFs). However, these protocols typically exploit only one degree of freedom at a time. In this work, we propose a QRIO protocol that simultaneously utilizes the polarization and spatial DOFs of a two-qubit hyperentangled state to remotely implement an arbitrary hybrid operator on an unknown single-photon two-qubit hyperstate. The shared hyperentangled resource is realized using the polarization and spatial modes of photons, while the protocol is constructed using linear optical elements and cross-Kerr nonlinear interactions to facilitate effective photon-photon coupling. Furthermore, the effects of measurement errors arising from finite coherent state distinguishability and coherent state dissipation are analyzed and the corresponding success probability of the protocol is evaluated. The results demonstrate that an appropriate choice of the cross-Kerr phase shift and coherent state amplitude significantly enhances the protocol performance, making the proposed scheme a promising candidate for hybrid quantum communication and distributed quantum information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses de Haas-van Alphen oscillations and thermal measurements on the kagome metal LuNb6Sn6 to map its Fermi surface topology. The research reveals two distinct electronic pockets whose behavior is significantly altered by a first-order Charge Density Wave transition. By combining quantum oscillation data with DFT calculations, the authors establish that CDW reconstruction dictates the material's ground state electronic structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism/transport, the interplay between symmetry breaking (CDW formation) and the resulting changes in electronic band structure and topological features (Berry phase evidence) is highly relevant to understanding emergent quantum phases in strongly correlated systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate the Fermi surface topology and electronic structure of the kagome metal LuNb6Sn6, particularly in relation to its Charge Density Wave (CDW) transition.

**Main result.** The study confirms two major quantum oscillation frequencies ($F_\alpha$ and $F_eta$) originating from ellipsoidal Fermi pockets, while also showing that CDW-induced Fermi surface reconstruction is crucial for understanding the ground state electronic structure.

**Method.** A combination of experimental measurements (dHvA oscillations via torque magnetometry, heat capacity) and theoretical calculations (DFT band structure analysis) was employed.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the kagome compound LuNb6Sn6. The investigation focuses on its electronic properties near the Fermi level, particularly how these are modified by a temperature-dependent CDW transition.

**Key observables.** dHvA frequencies ($F_\alpha \approx 20	ext{ T}, F_eta \approx 200	ext{ T}$), CDW transition temperatures ($	ext{T}_{	ext{CDW}} = 85	ext{ K}$), and the angular dependence of quantum oscillation frequencies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperature (2 K), magnetic fields up to 14 T, and electronic parameters derived from Lifshitz-Kosevich analysis ($m^*$, $T_D$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the observed quantum oscillations are governed by well-defined Fermi surface pockets whose topology can be mapped using standard solid-state physics models (e.g., ellipsoidal FS).

**Figures summary.** Figures show thermal anomalies confirming a first-order CDW transition, dHvA oscillations in torque vs. H, FFT spectra identifying two main frequencies, and plots showing the angle dependence of these frequencies consistent with ellipsoidal pockets.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by characterizing the material's phase transitions (CDW) using thermal measurements, followed by detailed quantum oscillation measurements to map the Fermi surface topology, and finally comparing experimental findings against first-principles electronic structure calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report a detailed de Haas-van Alphen (dHvA) study of the recently discovered kagome metal LuNb6Sn6 using torque magnetometry, magnetization, and heat-capacity measurements. Temperature-dependent torque and heat-capacity data reveal a charge density wave (CDW) transition at T_CDW = 85 K. The thermal hysteresis observed in both measurements establishes the first-order nature of the transition. Quantum oscillation measurements identify two major dHvA frequencies: F_alpha ~ 20 T and F_beta ~ 200 T, and their angular dependence is consistent with ellipsoidal Fermi surface (FS) pockets. Landau fan diagram analysis reveals evidence for a nontrivial Berry phase associated with the F_alpha pocket, indicating possible nontrivial electronic topology in LuNb6Sn6. Analysis of the temperature and magnetic field dependence of the oscillations using the Lifshitz-Kosevich formula yields electronic parameters that indicate anisotropic quantum transport properties. First-principles calculations provide further insight into the electronic structure, revealing Dirac-like band crossings, a flat band, and multiple van Hove singularities near the Fermi level. Our calculations based on the pristine phase cannot fully reproduce the experimentally observed quantum oscillation frequencies, suggesting that CDW-induced FS reconstruction plays a crucial role in the ground-state electronic structure of LuNb6Sn6. These results provide new insight into the FS topology and electronic structure of LuNb6Sn6, enriching our understanding of the electronic properties of kagome materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces 'ridgetronics,' a novel platform for electrical control over charge transport in 2D altermagnets. It achieves this by exploiting Ridge-Spin-Layer Coupling (RSLC), which locks electronic states into continuous, dispersionless lines in momentum space. The resulting material properties allow for highly directional, spin-polarized currents and electric field-tunable Hall effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state magnetism, the concept of controlling electronic states via symmetry constraints (Spin Layer Group Theory) and achieving highly directional, tunable current flow echoes principles used in designing quantum devices where coupling between multiple degrees of freedom is key.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to extend valleytronics by developing 'ridgetronics,' which controls directional current flow using continuous lines of electronic states in momentum space.

**Main result.** Ridge-Spin-Layer Coupling (RSLC) is established in 2D altermagnets, allowing electric field control over spin-filtered transport and inducing an Electric Hall Effect.

**Method.** The work uses symmetry group theory to derive conditions for ridge states and employs tight-binding models combined with band structure calculations.

**Model / system.** The system is based on 2D altermagnets on a square lattice, featuring continuous lines of dispersionless electronic states (ridges) coupled to spin polarization and atomic sublayers. The Hamiltonian describes this coupling using specific orbital hopping terms.

**Key observables.** Direction-discriminating currents, layer-selective switching via electric field ($E_z$), spin-polarized conductivities ($\sigma_{xx}, \sigma_{yy}$), and the Electric Hall Response ($\sigma_{xy}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strength (RSLC) is crucial; the system must be altermagnetic with specific symmetry operations $\{C_2||O_L\}$ connecting orthogonal ridges.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a 2D square lattice structure and relies on the existence of specific spin-layer group symmetries to support the coupling mechanism.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transition from discrete valleys to continuous ridges, show band structures derived from tight-binding models for candidate materials like Mg2Mo2(PO5)2, and demonstrate layer-dependent Q1D spin transport and EHE effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the concept of ridgetronics via RSLC in altermagnets. It then uses symmetry analysis to identify suitable candidate materials ($	ext{Mg}_2	ext{Mo}_2(	ext{PO}_5)_2$, etc.). Finally, it details how electric fields control spin-polarized transport and induce novel electrical responses.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Extending valleytronics from discrete points to continuous lines in momentum space transforms dispersionless bands into a controllable degree of freedom. Here we introduce ridge--spin--layer coupling (RSLC) in two-dimensional (2D) altermagnets, where a one-dimensional continuous line of dispersionless electronic states (a ridge) in momentum space locks to both spin polarization and atomic sublayer. This ridge-induced quenching of kinetic energy mimics flat-band physics, yet crucially, RSLC grants external control, allowing for layer-selective switching of ridge orientation in reciprocal space, spin-filtered transport in real space, and a distinct electric Hall response. Guided by collinear spin layer group symmetry, we identify three 2D candidate materials, namely Mg$_2$Mo$_2$(PO$_5$)$_2$, Ca(FeP)$_2$, and Mg$_2$V$_2$(SO$_5$)$_2$, each featuring a crossed-ridge structure with two ridges, one per spin channel and sublayer. Our work establishes ridgetronics as a controllable platform for direction-discriminating currents, bridging dispersionless bands with multifunctional device operation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the resource bottleneck of preparing complex joint states for quantum option pricing by introducing a structure-aware state preparation framework. It uses Tensor-Train ranks to design shallow variational circuits that efficiently encode asset correlations, achieving linear scaling in circuit depth while maintaining high accuracy in estimating basket payoffs.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on finance, the core technical contribution—using tensor decomposition structure (TT ranks) to efficiently design shallow variational quantum circuits for encoding complex high-dimensional probability distributions—is highly relevant to general quantum simulation tasks in condensed matter physics and quantum chemistry.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming the exponential circuit depth required for exact state preparation of high-dimensional joint probability distributions in Quantum Amplitude Estimation (QAE) for multi-asset derivative pricing.

**Main result.** The proposed structure-aware framework reduces the state preparation depth from exponential to linear scaling by utilizing Tensor-Train ranks and targeting the basket pushforward distribution directly.

**Method.** It employs a variational quantum circuit guided by Tensor-Train (TT) rank information, which is used to design shallow circuits that encode asset marginals locally and model cross-asset dependence via a compact latent block trained on the Basket-CDF objective.

**Model / system.** The system models multi-asset financial derivatives (basket options) whose joint probability distribution $p(x)$ must be encoded into an amplitude-encoded quantum state $|\psi_pangle$ for QAE.

**Key observables.** Circuit depth, low-percent basket-pricing errors, and the fidelity of reproducing empirical payoff curves/Basket-CDF values.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of assets ($d$), the TT ranks ($r_k$) which dictate entanglement structure, and the option strike price ($K$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that basket payoffs depend only on the distribution of the basket projection (pushforward) rather than the full joint state. It also treats amplitude estimation as a downstream subroutine.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the general circuit architecture combining TT-informed marginal loaders and a latent dependence block for state preparation; Figure E3 shows the successful reproduction of empirical payoff curves and the basket CDF using historical scenario data.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the bottleneck (state prep depth), proposes the structure-aware framework utilizing TT ranks to guide ansatz design, details the two-part circuit (marginal loaders + latent block trained via Basket-CDF objective), validates this approach with numerical experiments showing linear scaling, and concludes with end-to-end integration studies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Basket option pricing often relies on Monte Carlo estimation, for which quantum amplitude estimation (QAE) provides a quadratic speed-up. However, the practical benefit of QAE can be limited by the depth of the state-preparation circuit. We propose a structure-aware quantum state-preparation framework for QAE-based basket option pricing. The framework uses tensor-train (TT) rank information to design shallow variational state-preparation circuits. In the independent regime, TT ranks remove unnecessary entangling links from a hardware-efficient ansatz. In correlated basket settings, we instead prepare asset-wise marginals locally and train a compact latent block to match the basket cumulative distribution function. The Basket-CDF objective targets the basket pushforward distribution rather than the full joint state, directly aligning state preparation with basket-dependent payoffs. Numerical experiments show that the proposed circuits replace the exponential state-preparation depth scaling of exact amplitude loading with linear scaling, while maintaining low-percent basket-pricing errors. Additional sampling-based training experiments and an end-to-end QAE integration study support compatibility with sample-estimated training and standard QAE-based pricing workflows.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.14263v1>

**Summary.** This paper presents AutoHF, a flexible solver that calculates Hartree-Fock solutions by directly minimizing the variational energy functional. By leveraging automatic differentiation, it bypasses the need to write specialized code for every unique many-body Hamiltonian. This makes advanced electronic structure calculations significantly more general and easier to implement across diverse physical models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter theory, the reliance on automatic differentiation and gradient-based optimization techniques makes this methodology highly relevant for developing general quantum simulation tools applicable in AMO physics or open quantum systems where complex energy functionals need efficient minimization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is solving the interacting many-electron problem, which scales exponentially with system size.

**Main result.** The authors introduce AutoHF, a general framework that allows direct variational minimization of the energy functional for Hartree-Fock solutions across various Hamiltonians without requiring bespoke code development.

**Method.** Instead of traditional Self-Consistent Field (SCF) iterations, the method minimizes the variational energy by optimizing parameters defining the single Slater determinant using automatic differentiation and modern optimization tools.

**Model / system.** The theory applies to interacting N-fermion systems described by Hamiltonians containing both non-interacting (T) and quartic interaction terms (e.g., Hubbard U, density/spin interactions). The state is approximated by a single Slater determinant $|\Psiangle$, supporting RHF, UHF, and GHF ansätze.

**Key observables.** Variational energy ($\langle H angle$), the 1-body reduced density matrix ($ho$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system Hamiltonian $\hat{H}$, electron number ($N_{	ext{elec}}$), and variational parameters ($\alpha$) defining the orbital coefficients.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the interacting ground state can be accurately approximated by a single Slater determinant (the Hartree-Fock approximation), neglecting true electron correlation effects.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but notes mention successful convergence tests on molecular systems ($	ext{N}_2$, 3d TM Oxides) and lattice models (Hubbard cylinder).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the general framework by contrasting direct variational optimization with traditional SCF methods. It details the implementation using automatic differentiation to minimize the energy functional for various HF ansätze, demonstrating its applicability across molecular and solid-state Hamiltonians.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present autohf, a general, easy-to-use mean-field solver for quantum many-fermion Hamiltonians. It allows the user to bypass the process of deciphering the mean-field form for each many-body Hamiltonian $H$ and thus avoid setting up a tailored program for each $H$. Rather, autohf finds the optimal Slater determinant $|Ψ\rangle$, written in terms of orbital coefficients and subject to symmetry constraints, by directly minimizing the variational energy $\langle H \rangle$. By embracing this variational approach, autohf makes use of the growing power of automatic differentiation and optimization tools developed by the machine learning community.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the rich phase diagram of rhombohedral graphene, demonstrating that strong electron interactions drive the system into a Wigner Crystal (WC) state at low carrier densities. Under magnetic fields, this WC phase competes with superconducting and unconventional Quantum Hall states, establishing the WC's central role in governing these emergent correlated electronic orders.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides experimental evidence of strongly correlated insulating states (WC) coexisting with topological/superconducting phases in a tunable 2D material platform, which is highly relevant for understanding emergent quantum phenomena.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand the complex, emergent electronic phases arising from strong electron-electron interactions in rhombohedral graphene.

**Main result.** The highly insulating state at low carrier density is identified as a Wigner Crystal (WC) phase, which coexists and competes with superconducting (fSC) and reentrant quantum Hall (RIQH) states under magnetic fields.

**Method.** Transport measurements, including $n-D$ maps of resistance ($	ext{R}_{xx}, 	ext{R}_{xy}$), differential conductance ($V-I$), and Landau fan diagrams, are used across varying magnetic fields and temperatures.

**Model / system.** The physical system is rhombohedral multilayer graphene (specifically pentalayer graphene, R5G) encapsulated in hBN, allowing independent tuning of carrier density ($n$) and displacement field ($D$).

**Key observables.** Longitudinal resistance ($	ext{R}_{xx}$), Hall resistance ($	ext{R}_{xy}$), nonlinear $V-I$ characteristics, and quantized Hall plateaus.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low carrier densities, out-of-plane magnetic fields ($B$), and the interplay between Coulomb interaction energy and kinetic energy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping distinct phase boundaries (WC/mWC, cSC1, cSC2) observed through characteristic transport signatures like threshold behavior or sign reversals.

**Figures summary.** Figures map out $n-D$ phase diagrams showing regions corresponding to WC, $	ext{fSC}$, and $	ext{RIQH}$ states; Landau fan diagrams confirm the origin of these quantized/structured states from WC boundaries.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically maps the phase diagram by varying carrier density ($n$) and displacement field ($D$), analyzing transport signatures (resistance, $V-I$ curves) at fixed magnetic fields to delineate competing orders like Wigner Crystals and superconductivity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rhombohedral graphene systems provide a unique platform where strong electronic interactions and nontrivial band topology coexist at low carrier densities and high displacement fields, giving rise to a rich landscape of emergent electronic phases. Here, we report that the highly insulating state on the low-density side of chiral superconductivity in rhombohedral pentalayer graphene (R5G) corresponds to a Wigner crystal (WC) phase. In addition, a hole-doped metallic Wigner crystal (h-mWC) phase emerges near the WC boundary. Under an out-of-plane magnetic field, the system hosts competing magnetic-field-stabilized superconductivity (fSC) and unconventional reentrant quantum Hall (RIQH) states. These emergent phases are closely connected to the underlying WC and mWC states and evolve continuously across phase boundaries. Our results establish that WC phase plays an important role in the phase diagram of rhombohedral multilayer graphene and highlight its connection to a rich landscape of emergent phases.

</details>

### [Perturbation Analysis of Maximal Quantum Leakage](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14469v1)

**Authors:** Zijia Zhao, Shuixin Xiao, Farhad Farokhi  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.14469v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5**

<img src="2607.14469_figures/2607.14469_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously investigates the robustness of Maximal Quantum Leakage (MQL), a measure of worst-case information leakage, to small perturbations in the quantum encoding. The authors establish a tight continuity bound using the trace distance metric, demonstrating that MQL varies quadratically with the perturbation size. This work provides essential theoretical tools for quantifying noise resilience in quantum data processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** Understanding the stability of information-theoretic quantities like MQL under physical noise or imperfect implementation is crucial for designing robust quantum communication protocols and secure quantum computation schemes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes how sensitive the Maximal Quantum Leakage (MQL) measure is to small perturbations in the quantum states used to encode classical data.

**Main result.** A Lipschitz-type continuity bound for MQL with respect to the trace distance is established, showing that the leakage variation scales quadratically ($\epsilon^2$) with the perturbation size $\epsilon$ in the worst case.

**Method.** The authors derive bounds by analyzing the difference between ideal and perturbed quantum states using the trace distance, fidelity, and relative entropy metrics.

**Model / system.** The model involves an information leakage setting where a classical variable $X$ is encoded into an ensemble of quantum states $\{p_X(x), ho_x\}_{x\in X}$ on a Hilbert space $H$. Perturbations create a new ensemble $\{\dots, ho'_x\}_{x\in X}$.

**Key observables.** Maximal Quantum Leakage ($2Q_{ho}$), trace distance ($\|\cdot\|_1$), Fidelity ($F$), and Quantum Relative Entropy ($D$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Perturbation radius $\epsilon$, size of the random variable domain $|X|$, and Hilbert space dimension $d$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on establishing continuity bounds, though the derived fidelity and relative-entropy bounds are shown to be potentially loose compared to the trace distance bound.

**Figures summary.** Figures 1 and 2 compare the empirical maximum deviation of $2MQL$ versus $\epsilon$ for both fidelity-based and relative-entropy-based perturbations against their respective theoretical upper bounds, showing a gap.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces MQL and its robustness issue. It then derives the primary continuity bound using the trace distance, followed by deriving secondary bounds based on fidelity and relative entropy, concluding with numerical illustrations of these results.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Maximal quantum leakage (MQL) is a worst-case information leakage measure that quantifies an adversary's inference advantage gained from accessing quantum encoding of classical data with arbitrary measurements. While MQL admits an exact characterization for a given ensemble of quantum states, its robustness to implementation imperfections has not been systematically studied. In this paper, we analyze the sensitivity of maximal quantum leakage under perturbations of the quantum encoding. We establish a continuity bound in terms of the trace distance between ideal and perturbed quantum states, and show, via an example, that this bound is attainable. We further derive fidelity-based and relative-entropy-based sufficient conditions for bounding the variation of maximal quantum leakage, and illustrate numerically that these conditions can be loose.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops and analyzes a sophisticated, multi-stage training protocol using Transformer backflow Neural Quantum States (NQS) to simulate the 2D Hubbard model. It systematically investigates how hyperparameters—such as network width and update constraints—affect the convergence and accuracy of the variational energy calculation. The findings establish practical best practices for applying deep learning models to challenging strongly correlated quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly advances the computational tools used to tackle strongly correlated electron systems by providing rigorous guidelines for optimizing advanced machine learning representations (Transformers) in quantum many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the optimization dynamics of Transformer backflow Neural Quantum States (NQS) perform when applied to calculate ground states for the two-dimensional Hubbard model.

**Main result.** The research identifies practical optimization trends, showing that a balance between ansatz expressivity (Transformer width/channels), update stability (update constraint), and sampling quality (MC batch size) is crucial for convergence.

**Method.** A multi-stage training workflow involving NNB initialization, supervised Transformer pre-training, and main energy minimization using the Moment-Adaptive ReConfiguration Heuristic (MARCH) within Variational Monte Carlo (VMC).

**Model / system.** The primary system is the two-dimensional Hubbard model on a square lattice. The Hamiltonian includes hopping terms ($t, t'$) and an on-site repulsion term ($U$). Studies cover doped and half-filled regimes, using both Periodic and Open Boundary Conditions.

**Key observables.** Variational energy per site ($	ext{E}$), convergence behavior of the VMC optimization, and local energy $E_{loc}(n)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Update-norm threshold ($c_0$), Transformer width ($d$), number of determinant channels ($K$), Monte Carlo batch size ($B$), interaction strength ($U$), and doping level.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of a multi-stage training protocol (NNB $ightarrow$ Pre-training $ightarrow$ MARCH) is necessary to stabilize the optimization of the highly expressive Transformer architecture.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the optimization behavior across various Hubbard model settings (half-filled vs. doped, PBC vs. OBC) and show how increasing Transformer width leads to lower late-time variational energies on larger lattices.

**Paper structure.** The paper details a multi-stage training protocol: 1) NNB initialization for lightweight orbital generation; 2) Supervised pre-training of the full Transformer architecture using NNB outputs as targets; and 3) Main energy optimization via MARCH within VMC, analyzing parameter dependencies at each stage.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Building on the multi-determinant Transformer backflow neural quantum state (NQS) ansatz and the associated multi-stage training workflow for the doped two-dimensional Hubbard model, we investigate how the optimization dynamics of the NQS depend on several key optimization and architectural hyperparameters. The workflow consists of neural-network backflow (NNB) initialization, supervised Transformer pre-training, and main energy optimization using the Moment-Adaptive ReConfiguration Heuristic (MARCH) within variational Monte Carlo. Using the doped $4\times4$ periodic Hubbard model at $U=8$ as a baseline, we examine how the update-norm threshold, Transformer width, number of determinant channels, and Monte Carlo batch size affect convergence. We find that a moderate update constraint improves the efficiency of MARCH optimization, larger Transformer width and more determinant channels improve the expressive capacity of the ansatz, and larger Monte Carlo batches reduce sampling noise in the update direction. We further test the same workflow at half filling, weaker interaction strength, open boundary conditions, and on a larger $8\times8$ doped lattice. These results identify practical optimization trends for Transformer backflow NQSs and highlight the balance between ansatz expressivity, MARCH update stability, and Monte Carlo sampling quality.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes and analyzes the use of trivalent lattice surgery as an efficient alternative to conventional four-valent methods for implementing surface code QEC. By reducing resource overheads in planar architectures, it paves a scalable path toward realizing logical quantum processors using hardware with limited qubit connectivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware constraints (connectivity) in realizing fault-tolerant quantum computation, which is a major bottleneck for building large-scale quantum processors based on superconducting circuits or similar planar platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing low-overhead quantum error-correction (QEC) schemes for planar architectures with limited qubit connectivity.

**Main result.** A trivalent lattice-surgery protocol significantly reduces required resources compared to standard four-valent methods, potentially improving logical fidelity by up to 25% in near-term superconducting implementations.

**Method.** The authors compare the resource scaling and logical performance of two stabilizer measurement schemes—trivalent versus four-valent—within the framework of lattice surgery for surface codes.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on implementing the surface code, particularly using a rotated geometry suitable for planar qubit layouts like those realized with fluxonium qubits. The comparison is between circuits requiring 3-neighbor (trivalent) versus 4-neighbor (four-valent) interactions for stabilizer measurements.

**Key observables.** Logical fidelity ($\approx 25\%$ improvement potential), required physical resources ($O(d)$ reduction in qubits/gates), and the logical error rate ($\epsilon_L$) under various noise models.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($d=3$ benchmarked), qubit connectivity (trivalent vs. four-valent), and noise parameters including gate errors ($p$), measurement errors ($q$), and idling noise ($I$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to embed plaquettes correctly for fault tolerance, and later sections compare performance using leading-order quadratic expansions of $\epsilon_L$ based on specific noise sources.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate distance $d=3$ surface code patches, comparing measurement circuits (four-valent vs. trivalent), and showing comparisons of logical error rates ($p_L$) under different noise assumptions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the need for low-overhead QEC on planar lattices, detailing the structural advantages of trivalent lattice surgery over four-valent methods in terms of resource scaling, and concluding with quantitative benchmarking of logical fidelity using realistic noise models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Low-overhead quantum error-correction schemes are essential for enabling quantum computation on registers containing multiple logical qubits. For planar architectures with limited nearest-neighbor qubit connectivity, the surface code has emerged as the leading paradigm. Recent theoretical and experimental work has shown that a physical-qubit connectivity of degree three is sufficient to implement fault-tolerant quantum error correction. In this work, we study lattice surgery in the context of such trivalent architectures and introduce scalable circuit constructions to implement it. Compared with the four-valent measurement scheme, the trivalent lattice-surgery protocol reduces the required resources by $\mathcal{O}(d)$ qubits out of a total qubit count of $\mathcal{O}(d^2)$ and by $\mathcal{O}(d)$ two-qubit gates out of a total two-qubit gate count of $\mathcal{O}(d^3)$. We benchmark the logical fidelity of both lattice-surgery schemes in terms of experimentally realistic simulations targeting an implementation with a fluxonium qubit based architecture and find a potential improvement of up to $\approx25\%$ for distance-three. These results open a way for scalable planar trivalent qubit architectures to host a surface-code-based logical quantum processor.

</details>

**Authors:** Anubhav Chaturvedi  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.14569v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** The paper rigorously proves that the standard NPA hierarchy fails to provide an exact characterization of quantum correlations in simple Bell tests. By analyzing asymptotic limits and using polynomial sum-of-squares techniques, the authors show a persistent gap between the best finite approximation and the true quantum maximum. This establishes a fundamental limitation on the power of fixed-level semidefinite programming relaxations for certifying quantum nonlocality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental theoretical limits on how well classical or semi-classical relaxation techniques (like those used in semidefinite programming) can capture true quantum correlations, which is crucial for understanding resource theories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining if any finite level of the Navascués–Pironio–Acín (NPA) hierarchy can exactly characterize the complete quantum set in the simplest bipartite Bell scenario.

**Main result.** The authors prove that no fixed, finite NPA relaxation level can perfectly describe the true quantum correlations, demonstrating a strict inclusion $\mathcal{Q} \subsetneq 	ext{NS}$.

**Method.** The proof uses asymptotic analysis by comparing the quantum maximum value ($\omega_Q$) with the bound derived from fixed-level moment constraints ($\omega_L$), showing their difference diverges.

**Model / system.** The system is a bipartite Bell experiment involving two binary measurements per party. The analysis focuses on the doubly tilted CHSH functional, $h_\alpha$, which quantifies correlations between measurement outcomes.

**Key observables.** Quantum maximum value ($\omega_Q(\alpha)$), fixed-level NPA bound ($\omega_L(\alpha)$), and the Motzkin polynomial ($M(X, Y)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The tilt parameter $\alpha$ (or $T=1-\alpha$), which is taken to approach 1 in the asymptotic analysis.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes standard NPA constraints based on moment positivity and linear identities; it specifically excludes state- or operator-optimality conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem using the CHSH functional, develops the asymptotic comparison between $\omega_Q$ and $\omega_L$, uses polynomial sum-of-squares arguments involving the Motzkin polynomial to show a contradiction, and concludes with the strict inequality $\mathcal{Q} \subsetneq 	ext{NS}$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Navascués--Pironio--Acín (NPA) hierarchy gives the standard semidefinite outer approximations to quantum behaviors. Whether \emph{any} finite level can already equal the quantum set has remained open even in the bipartite scenario with two binary measurements per party. We demonstrate that \emph{no finite level} is exact. For the symmetric doubly tilted CHSH functional $h_α=A_0B_0+A_0B_1+A_1B_0-A_1B_1+α(A_0+B_0)$, set $T=1-α$. Its quantum maximum satisfies $[ω_{\rm Q}(1-T)-(4-2T)]/T^3\to4/3$, whereas every fixed NPA level satisfies $[ω_L(1-T)-ω_{\rm Q}(1-T)]/T^3\to+\infty$. Under the corresponding boundary rescaling, an explicit expectation of the positive operator $ω_{\rm Q}(1-t^2)I-H_t$ converges to the Motzkin polynomial. A bounded fixed-level error would therefore make the Motzkin polynomial plus a nonnegative constant a sum of squares, which is impossible. Consequently, every standard NPA relaxation based on a fixed finite list of words in the measurement projectors strictly contains the complete quantum set, and its nonquantum behaviors accumulate at a local deterministic behavior. Thus, the finite-level exactness of CHSH and all one-sided tilted CHSH maxima does not extend to an exact finite-level description of the complete quantum set in the minimal scenario.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides rigorous analytical results for the distribution of eccentricities in Random Regular Graphs. By deriving closed-form expressions for key statistical measures like the mean and variance, it offers a detailed understanding of network structure based on graph theory principles. The methods developed using extreme value statistics are valuable benchmarks for analyzing global properties in large complex systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on graph theory, the mathematical techniques used—analyzing extreme value statistics (eccentricity) in large complex systems—are highly relevant to characterizing rare events or global properties in quantum many-body systems or disordered lattices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is deriving a closed-form analytical expression for the distribution of eccentricities (DoE) in random regular graphs (RRGs). This provides a detailed view of the large-scale structure beyond just characteristic length scales.

**Main result.** A closed-form tail distribution $P(E > \ell)$ is derived, along with asymptotic expressions for the mean eccentricity $\langle E angle$ and variance $	ext{Var}(E)$. The mode is found to be close to the rounded value of the mean eccentricity.

**Method.** The analysis employs Extreme Value Theory (EVT) and utilizes tail-sum formulas combined with approximations like the Euler-Maclaurin formula to analyze moments ($\langle E angle, \langle E^2 angle$).

**Model / system.** The model is a Random Regular Graph (RRG) consisting of $N$ nodes, where every node has a fixed degree $c$. Eccentricity quantifies the maximum shortest path distance from any node to all others.

**Key observables.** Distribution of eccentricities ($P(E > \ell)$), Mean eccentricity ($\langle E angle$), Mode of DoE ($E_{	ext{mode}}$), and Variance ($	ext{Var}(E)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of nodes $N$, constant degree $c$ (with $c \ge 3$). The analysis is focused on the large $N$ limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivations rely on approximations derived from the tail distribution of shortest path lengths and assume that correlations between distances can be managed or neglected in the asymptotic limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare analytical predictions ($	imes$, $+$) against simulation results ($\circ$) for the mode ($E_{	ext{mode}}$) and variance ($	ext{Var}(E)$) as a function of network size $N$ for fixed degree $c=5$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the tail distribution using EVT, then deriving closed-form expressions for higher moments like $\langle E^2 angle$, and finally analyzing the resulting variance and mode behavior as functions of network size.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive a closed-form analytical expression for the distribution of eccentricities (DoE) in random regular graphs (RRGs) that consist of $N$ nodes of degree $c$. The DoE is given by the tail distribution $P(E > \ell) \simeq 1 - \exp \left[ - \exp \left( - \frac{ e^{b \ell} - μ}β \right) \right]$, where the distance $\ell$ takes integer values, $b = \ln (c-1)$ is the shape parameter, $β= \frac{c-2}{c} N$ is the scale parameter and $μ= \frac{c-2}{c} N \ln N$ is the location parameter. By providing the full distribution rather than a single characteristic length scale, we present a detailed view of the large-scale structure. In spite of the fact that the degrees of all the nodes are the same, their eccentricities exhibit non-trivial variations. We derive a closed-form expression for the mean eccentricity, which is given by $\langle E \rangle \simeq \frac{\ln N}{\ln (c-1)} + \frac{\ln \ln N}{\ln (c-1)} - \frac{ \ln c - \ln (c-2) }{ \ln (c-1) } + \frac{1}{2}$. We calculate the mode of the DoE, which exhibits a staircase profile as a function of the network size. Interestingly, the mode is given by $E_{\rm mode} ={\rm Round} \left( \langle E \rangle \right)$, where ${\rm Round}( x )$ is the nearest integer to $x$. We also calculate the variance ${\rm Var}(E)$ and show that it exhibits oscillations as a function of the network size $N$. The results presented in this paper may serve as benchmarks for algorithmic approaches to eccentricity calculations in large sparse networks. The eccentricities are important in practical applications such as broadcasting and global dissemination, where the network performance is determined by the longest delay times.

</details>
