**Summary.** This paper applies star-triangle duality analysis to permutation models on triangular and honeycomb lattices. By treating the fundamental interaction unit as a star-triangle block, the authors estimate critical bond dimensions for both geometries. The results suggest that the lattice geometry significantly influences the system's phase behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on statistical mechanics, the use of permutation models and duality techniques is highly relevant to understanding complex interactions in disordered systems, which share mathematical structures with quantum many-body problems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates star-triangle duality estimates for permutation models defined on triangular and honeycomb lattices.

**Main result.** The analysis yields critical bond dimension estimates: approximately 2.635 for the honeycomb lattice and 1.476 for the triangular lattice, suggesting different phases of matter based on geometry.

**Method.** The core method combines duality analysis with the star-triangle transformation, treating the fundamental unit as a star-triangle block rather than a bare bond.

**Model / system.** The system is a ferromagnetic permutation model defined on 2D lattices (triangular and honeycomb). The site variable is a permutation $\sigma_i \in S_Q$, and the partition function depends on edge weights involving these permutations.

**Key observables.** Critical bond dimension ($D^c$), principal factors ($H, T$) for the lattice blocks, and duality relations between original and dual principal factors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($D$), replica parameter ($Q$), and the structure of the underlying lattices (triangular vs. honeycomb).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on using a finite-basis unit (star-triangle block) for duality, and physical estimates are derived in the replica limit ($Q 	o 0$).

**Figures summary.** Not specified; notes refer to equations and tables detailing finite-$Q$ results comparing honeycomb and triangular lattice dimensions.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by defining principal factors for both lattices, applying the star-triangle duality condition at a finite basis level, deriving replica limit equations, and finally calculating the critical estimates for both geometries.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a duality analysis in conjunction with the star-triangle transformation for symmetric-group permutation models on the triangular and honeycomb lattices. The calculation is motivated by the permutation-model description of random tensor networks and by earlier duality analyses of replicated spin glasses. The essential point is that the finite-basis unit is not a bare bond but a star-triangle block. Our analysis estimates the critical bond dimension for the honeycomb lattice to be 2.634929344884, and the associated single-bond duality relation yields the triangular-lattice estimate 1.475661534848.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a theoretical model for bilayer nickelates, treating the system as having two coupled electronic sectors: an itinerant $d_{x^2-y^2}$ band and localized $d_{z^2}$ moments. By combining RKKY interactions mediated by the former with superexchange among the latter, the authors predict robust antiferromagnetic order near Q = (pi/2, pi/2), offering a unified explanation for magnetism in these unconventional superconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit separation of electronic degrees of freedom into itinerant quasiparticles mediating long-range RKKY forces and localized moments governed by superexchange provides a powerful theoretical framework applicable to other correlated Mott insulators where orbital physics is crucial.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to elucidate the nature of density wave order and understand the microscopic physics driving high-temperature superconductivity in bilayer Ruddlesden-Popper Nickelates.

**Main result.** The model predicts an antiferromagnetic ground state with an ordering wavevector near Q = (pi/2, pi/2), driven by the interplay between RKKY and superexchange interactions. This magnetic order is consistent with experimental findings regarding spin dynamics in these materials.

**Method.** The authors use a slave-spin mean-field approach on a two-orbital Hubbard model to derive an effective spin Hamiltonian incorporating both RKKY (from itinerant quasiparticles) and superexchange (from local moments).

**Model / system.** The system is the bilayer nickelate La3Ni2O7, modeled by a two-orbital Hubbard Hamiltonian. The low-energy physics separates into an itinerant $d_{x^2-y^2}$ sector mediating RKKY interactions and localized $d_{z^2}$ moments contributing superexchange.

**Key observables.** Antiferromagnetic ordering wavevector Q, spin-wave spectrum softening at Q ~ (pi/2, pi/2), static magnetic susceptibility $\chi(q)$, and orbital-dependent quasiparticle spectral weights Z_alpha(U).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hubbard interaction strength U, which controls the degree of orbital selectivity; temperature T relative to N's ordering temperatures.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on projecting susceptibility vertices onto the $d_{x^2-y^2}$ orbital and assuming that correlation effects can be incorporated into an effective quasiparticle tight-binding Hamiltonian via slave-spin mean-field theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams, static spin susceptibilities $\chi(q)$ showing peaks near (0.5pi, 0.5pi), the dependence of the ordering wave vector Q on U, and the calculated spin-wave spectrum softening at the predicted magnetic ordering point.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical context using experimental data to motivate a theoretical model. It then details the slave-spin mean-field calculation to derive an effective Hamiltonian that separates itinerant (RKKY) and localized (superexchange) interactions, culminating in predictions for the magnetic ground state.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The recent discovery of high-temperature superconductivity in the bilayer nickelate La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$ has led to extensive interest in the correlation physics of its normal state. Given that the superconducitivity develops near a density wave order in the phase diagram, it is important to elucidate the nature of this order. Based on the accumulated experimental evidence for a bad metal state in proximity to an orbital-selective Mott phase, here we describe magnetic correlations of the system in a conceptually new way -- in terms of effective local moments experiencing a combination of RKKY and superexchange interactions. This gives rise to a magnetic order with a wavevector that is close to $\mathbf{Q}=(π/2,π/2)$ and, at the same time, yields a clear understanding of the associated spin dynamics. Our results are consistent with the rapidly emerging experiments about the magnetic correlations in the density wave order of the bilayer nickelate. Implications for unconventional superconductivity in this and related multiorbital systems are discussed.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel hybrid Material Point Method (hybrid-MPM) framework to accurately simulate the bending elasticity of very flexible structures like tubes and tape springs. The methodology successfully couples cross-sectional and lengthwise deformations, allowing for robust prediction of mechanical response under complex contact conditions. Validation against physical experiments confirms its utility for modeling soft robots and deployable systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical mechanics, the development of advanced numerical techniques like hybrid-MPM for large deformation and contact mechanics is highly relevant to simulating complex geometries encountered in quantum device modeling or soft matter systems where continuum assumptions break down.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to develop a robust computational framework to simulate the complex, large-deformation bending elasticity of highly flexible slender structures.

**Main result.** The developed hybrid-MPM framework shows excellent agreement when validating numerical simulations against desktop experiments for both tubes and tape springs.

**Method.** A hybrid Material Point Method (hybrid-MPM) is employed, integrating Lagrangian finite element and Eulerian finite difference frameworks to handle complex contact mechanics during large deformation analysis.

**Model / system.** The physical systems studied are slender structures like tubes and tape springs, whose bending behavior exhibits coupling between cross-sectional deformation (e.g., ovalization) and lengthwise bending, particularly near instability points like the Brazier instability.

**Key observables.** Bending moment (M), applied force (F), displacement ($\Delta$), curvature ($\kappa$), and the resulting force-displacement curve.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Young's modulus ($E$), Poisson ratio ($
u$), structure dimensions ($R, h, L$), and internal pressure ($p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Theoretical derivations assume linear constitutive laws and inextensible centerlines; computationally, the framework is validated against experimental measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares experimental snapshots of bending tests on tubes and tapes with corresponding simulation results. Figure 4 compares force-displacement curves for tape springs across experiments, simulations, and theory.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the theoretical moment-curvature relations via energy minimization, extends this to include internal pressure effects, implements a computational framework using hybrid-MPM, and validates this approach by comparing simulation results against physical three-point bending tests on fabricated samples.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Slender structures are highly flexible, spanning several orders of magnitude in length scale. Their deformation depends on the slenderness of their cross sections, highlighting that the elasticity and geometry of structures are intrinsically coupled. The deformation of the cross-section becomes significant, particularly when tubes and pipes are subjected to bending, known as the Brazier instability. Although the bending performance of slender structures is quantified experimentally using a canonical three-point bending test, their numerical counterparts remain under-explored because complex contact mechanics must be implemented in simulations. In this study, we develop a computational framework to simulate experimental three-point bending tests using a hybrid material point method (hybrid-MPM) approach, which integrates Lagrangian finite element and Eulerian finite difference frameworks. We adapt our framework to elastic tubes and tape springs as canonical examples that exhibit characteristic bending deformation in which the cross-sectional and lengthwise bending are coupled. The predictions of numerical simulations are validated against desktop experiments and classical theory. The excellent agreement between the simulation and the experiments implies that the hybrid-MPM framework provides a robust computational framework for predicting the large deformation of structures involving complex contact, such as soft robots and deployable structures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how magnetic vector potentials perturb periodic Grover walks on finite graphs. By treating the potential as a small perturbation, the authors derive an effective continuous-time Hamiltonian $H$. The key finding is that the graph's spectral structure dictates the robustness of periodicity, providing quantitative measures for this stability.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects fundamental concepts of quantum walks and graph theory to the stability of quantum dynamics under external fields, which is highly relevant for understanding decoherence or engineered robustness in quantum computation architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how magnetic vector potential perturbations affect the periodicity of Grover walks executed on finite quantum graphs.

**Main result.** The perturbed dynamics can be shown to converge asymptotically to a continuous-time quantum walk generated by a Hermitian matrix $H$, whose spectral structure characterizes the robustness of the original periodicity.

**Method.** The analysis employs spectral theory, treating the vector potential as a small perturbation ($eta 	o 0$) and deriving an effective Hamiltonian $H$ from the discrete-time evolution operator.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum graph representing Grover walks on finite graphs ($P_n, C_n, K_{n,n}$). The dynamics are governed by a time evolution operator modified by a magnetic vector potential $eta$.

**Key observables.** Periodicity ($	au$), Robustness of Periodicity ($R_p = \dim \ker H / |A|$), and the spectrum of the effective Hamiltonian $H$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The perturbation strength $eta$, which is assumed to be small; graph structure (e.g., presence of non-simple eigenvalues).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the underlying graph possesses at least one non-simple eigenvalue to derive the characterizing Hermitian matrix $H$. The convergence relies on the small-$eta$ limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate specific entries and structures of the derived Hamiltonian matrix $H$ for cycle graphs ($C_n$) and complete bipartite graphs ($K_{k,k}$), detailing dependencies on edge configurations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the perturbed walk dynamics, deriving the effective continuous-time Hamiltonian $H$, analyzing its spectral properties based on graph structure (e.g., path vs. cycle), and quantifying periodicity robustness using dimension counts ($\dim S_{	ext{sim}}, \dim T_{	ext{per}}, \dim L^{\perp}$).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the effect of magnetic vector potentials on periodic Grover walks on finite graphs. The magnetic vector potential is introduced through the framework of quantum graphs, which induces the Grover walk as a special case. We regard the vector potential as a perturbation of a periodic Grover walk and investigate the robustness of its periodicity. Our analysis reveals that the response to such perturbations depends on the spectral structure of the underlying graph. In particular, when the graph possesses at least one non-simple eigenvalue, we derive a Hermitian matrix that characterizes the robustness of its periodicity. As a consequence, we show that the perturbed dynamics is asymptotically described by a continuous-time quantum walk generated by this Hermitian matrix.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates superconductivity in ThFeAsN by calculating superconducting properties using advanced first-principles methods. The authors determined that spin fluctuation interactions are the dominant pairing mechanism, leading to an unconventional $d_{xy}$-wave order parameter and a calculated critical temperature consistent with experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the methodology of using first-principles calculations to map out competing many-body interactions ($	ext{EPC}$ vs. $	ext{SF}$) to determine the ground state pairing symmetry is highly relevant to understanding complex quantum phases in strongly correlated systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Understanding the superconducting (SC) pairing mechanism, the origin of high critical temperature ($T_c$), and the symmetry of the SC order parameter in Fe-based superconductors.

**Main result.** The calculations reveal that ThFeAsN is an unconventional superconductor with a $d_{xy}$-wave order parameter, driven primarily by spin fluctuation (SF) mediated interactions rather than electron-phonon coupling.

**Method.** Ab initio superconducting density functional theory (SCDFT) was employed to calculate pairing interactions and the resulting SC gap structure across multiple Fermi surface sheets.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on $	ext{ThFeAsN}$, an Fe-based high $T_c$ superconductor, analyzing its electronic structure using DFT methods incorporating electron-phonon coupling (EPC), screened Coulomb repulsion ($\mu_{ee}$), and spin fluctuation interactions ($\mu_{SF}$).

**Key observables.** Calculated critical temperature ($T_c = 22.4 	ext{ K}$), $d_{xy}$-wave SC order parameter symmetry, quasiparticle density of states (QPDOS), and ultrasonic attenuation coefficient.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $T_c$ comparison with experiment ($\sim 29 	ext{ K}$); relative strengths of pairing interactions ($\mu_{SF}^{nk} > \mu_{ee}^{nk} > \lambda_{nk}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations rely on solving the SC gap equation while treating EPC, Coulomb repulsion, and SF interaction effects separately to determine the dominant pairing mechanism.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure, momentum-dependent SC gap ($\Delta_{\mathbf{k}}$) across five Fermi surface sheets showing $d_{xy}$-wave character, and comparisons of calculated $	ext{QPDOS}$ and ultrasonic attenuation coefficients.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares different pairing mechanisms (EPC only vs. EPC + e-e repulsion vs. SF interaction) to determine the dominant attractive force, leading to the final conclusion that SF mediation dictates the unconventional $d_{xy}$-wave superconductivity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconducting (SC) pairing mechanism, origin of high $T_c$ and symmetry of SC order parameter in Fe-based superconductors are among the important unsolved problems in condensed matter and materials physics. We study the SC properties of ThFeAsN, a Fe-based high $T_c$ superconductor, by {\it ab initio} superconducting density functional theory calculations with electron-phonon coupling, screened static and dynamic electron-electron Coulomb repulsion and spin fluctuation (SF) mediated pair-interaction fully taken into account. Our calculations reveal that ThFeAsN is a SF-mediated multiband superconductor with the calculated $T_c$ of 22.4 K and the $d_{xy}$-wave SC order parameter with different signs on different Fermi surface sheets, in consistent with experiments. We also present distinct SC properties such as quasiparticle density of states and ultrasonic attenuation coefficient which can be immediately verified by experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper derives a closed-form expression for the two-particle reduced density matrix ($ho_2$) of spinless fermions in a Tomonaga-Luttinger liquid. By using constructive bosonization, the authors reveal a second correlation exponent $\lambda$ that governs off-diagonal coherence beyond what single-particle measurements can capture. The result successfully connects universal Luttinger liquid scaling theory with quantitative predictions for finite microscopic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an exact analytical tool (the 2-RDM) for quantifying complex many-body correlations in 1D systems, connecting universal field theory results to measurable quantities in finite microscopic models, which is highly relevant for understanding quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is deriving an explicit, analytically controlled expression for the equal-time two-particle reduced density matrix ($ho_2$) in strongly correlated 1D systems.

**Main result.** The authors derive a closed form for $ho_2$ showing two characteristic exponents: one familiar exponent $\gamma_2$ and a new exponent $\lambda$, which controls off-diagonal correlations between opposite chiralities.

**Method.** The calculation employs constructive bosonization techniques combined with the introduction of an explicit ultraviolet cutoff to manage divergences inherent in calculating higher-order correlation functions.

**Model / system.** The system studied is spinless fermions modeled as a Tomonaga-Luttinger liquid (TLL) on a ring, which is analyzed using continuum field theory methods derived from the fermionic Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Two-particle reduced density matrix ($ho_2$), pair distribution function ($g_2$), static structure factor, and specific correlation signatures like $2k_F$ charge-density-wave correlations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Luttinger parameter $K$, the interaction strength parameters (J/V ratio), and the derived exponents $\gamma_2 = (K+K^{-1}-2)/2$ and $\lambda = (K^{-1}-K)/2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on bosonization, assuming the system is in the Luttinger liquid regime, and requires fixing the cutoff based on results from the one-particle density matrix.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 graphically illustrates $ho_2$ for a 1D N-body system. Figure 2 visualizes $ho_2$ on a spherical surface in $(r, r', \Delta R)$ space, particularly highlighting correlation structures for attractive interactions.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically builds the theory by first introducing the general formalism of reduced density matrices ($ho_n$), then applying constructive bosonization to derive the TLL result, followed by detailed analysis of the resulting correlations, and finally validating the analytic result against numerical DMRG calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-particle coherence is the first level of the reduced-density-matrix hierarchy that contains correlations inaccessible to single-particle observables, yet analytic two-particle density matrices are rare even in one dimension. We derive a closed, finite-size expression for the equal-time two-particle reduced density matrix of spinless fermions in a Tomonaga-Luttinger liquid using constructive bosonization with an explicit ultraviolet cutoff. In addition to the familiar Luttinger parameter $K$-dependent exponent $γ^2=(K+K^{-1}-2)/2$ which governs the spatial decay of matrix elements, the result exposes a second exponent, $λ=(K^{-1}-K)/2$, which encodes correlations between opposite chiralities and controls the off-diagonal structure. The diagonal limit of the two-particle reduced density matrix yields density correlations and the static structure factor, while its coherences resolve algebraic $2k_F$ charge-density-wave correlations for repulsion and odd-parity p-wave pairing correlations for attraction. After fixing the cutoff from the one-particle density matrix, the analytic result quantitatively reproduces density matrix renormalization group calculations of the interacting J-V chain within the Luttinger liquid regime. The result connects universal Luttinger liquid scaling with observables in finite microscopic systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the persistent Fermion-Doubling problem in chiral QFT discretizations by analyzing the Dirac Quantum Cellular Automaton (QCA). It proves that while FD exists, the QCA offers a superior approximation to the continuum limit in ultrarelativistic regimes compared to standard LGTs. The authors provide explicit calculations for the Green's functions of both the original and fixed models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides rigorous theoretical benchmarks for lattice discretizations of fundamental quantum field theories, which are crucial for developing and interpreting results from quantum simulation platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper definitively proves that the Dirac Quantum Cellular Automaton (QCA) exhibits Fermion Doubling (FD) in its chiral discretization, and compares its approximation quality to continuous-time Lattice Gauge Theories (LGTs). It also computes the Green's function for a fixed version of this model.

**Main result.** The QCA is shown to be superior to continuous-time LGTs for describing ultrarelativistic regimes, while the latter performs better in non-relativistic ones. The resulting two-point correlation functions are found to possess an 'astonishing simplicity'.

**Method.** The analysis involves rigorous mathematical proofs, calculating one-time-step two-point correlation functions (Green's functions) using both direct and Fourier space methods, and performing systematic comparisons between lattice approximations and the continuum Dirac equation.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the $(1+1)$D Dirac Quantum Cellular Automaton (QCA), which serves as a discrete spacetime discretization for QFT like QED. It is compared against continuous-time LGTs and an advanced, FD-fixed variant called Flavored Dirac QCA (FQCA).

**Key observables.** The one-time-step two-point correlation function (Green's function, GF) of the Dirac QCA and FQCA; comparison metrics for approximating the continuum Dirac equation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Energy regimes (ultrarelativistic vs. non-relativistic); lattice spacing ($\epsilon$); momentum ($p$) and energy ($E$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the single-particle sector (free models) when studying and fixing FD, although comparisons are made to experimental limitations in quantum simulation.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare ratios of matrix elements for different lattice models across momentum space, illustrating performance differences between regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first proving the existence and nature of FD in the Dirac QCA. It then quantitatively compares the QCA to continuous-time LGTs regarding their approximation quality in specific energy regimes. Finally, it computes the GF for the specialized FQCA model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give the definitive proof that the Dirac Quantum Cellular Automaton (QCA) used for both quantum simulation and algorithmic foundations of Quantum Field Theory (QFT), and especially of Quantum Electrodynamics (QED), as put forward in References https://doi.org/10.1007/s11128-019-2555-4 and https://doi.org/10.22331/q-2023-11-08-1179, does exhibit Fermion Doubling (FD), albeit thrice as less severe as discrete-time standard Lattice Gauge Theories (LGTs) -- as shown in Reference arXiv:2505.07900 -- , which are naive regarding the spacetime discretization of differential operators acting on fermionic fields. The proof is done for the $(1 + 1)$D Dirac-QCA model. We show that the (one-time-step) two-point correlation function, also called Green's function (GF), of the Dirac QCA, is of astonishing simplicity, which is in contrast with the GF of the Dirac equation. We also compare, both qualitatively and quantitatively, this Dirac QCA to the continuous-time-LGT spatial discretization of Dirac fermions regarding how well these two lattice models approximate their naive continuum limit -- which is nothing but the Dirac equation -- even when far away from that limit, a situation which must be considered because of experimental limitations in quantum simulation -- : the Dirac QCA is better for ultrarelativistic regimes, whereas continuous-time LGT is better for non-relativistic regimes. In a second part of this work, we compute the GF of the FD-fixed model put forward in the last cited reference, called Flavored Dirac QCA (FQCA) -- which staggers an extra, artificial flavor \emph{only}, on a diamond spacetime lattice, and does not stagger chirality as staggered fermions in usual LGT. The structure of this FQCA two-point correlation function is of extreme simplicity, and can be expressed in a very simple manner in terms of the four chiral components of the FD-suffering, original-model GF.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Highway, a novel and scalable algorithm for Overlapping Community Detection (OCD) that improves efficiency by utilizing the sparse backbone of an input network. By combining modularity and Jaccard scores to build this backbone, Highway accurately infers overlapping community structures. The method demonstrates superior performance across multiple metrics when tested against numerous benchmark networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on network science rather than quantum physics directly, the underlying mathematical framework of community detection shares conceptual parallels with finding emergent structures or ground states in complex physical systems, which can sometimes be modeled using graph theory techniques relevant to condensed matter analysis.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is Overlapping Community Detection (OCD) in complex networks, where nodes can belong to multiple clusters.

**Main result.** The proposed Highway algorithm achieves state-of-the-art performance on 728 benchmark networks, ranking first in overlapping normalized mutual information with a significant improvement over existing methods.

**Method.** Highway is a scalable OCD algorithm that leverages the sparse backbone of the input network to perform efficient community inference through anchor initialization and neighbor propagation.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is general network data represented by a graph G=(V, E). The method constructs a sparse backbone H using a hybrid edge scoring function combining modularity and Jaccard overlap scores.

**Key observables.** Membership matrix (alpha), Pattern confidence score (gamma(P)), Calibration strength (lambda_v), Fuzzy Rand Index (FRI), overlapping modularity (Q_ov), Dice index, F*, and Overlapping Normalized Mutual Information (ONMI).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The backbone construction uses a hybrid weight $\omega$ for the edge scoring function. The final membership update involves tuning parameters like $eta$, $\eta$, and $	au$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that primary community signals propagate effectively through a limited set of structurally informative edges (the sparse backbone).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the Highway algorithm in stages: Backbone Construction, Anchor-based Signal Initialization, Neighbor-only Propagation, and final Anchor-Preserving Pattern Calibration. It concludes with extensive comparative results on benchmark networks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Community structures are common in real networks, and extracting them provides valuable insight in applications ranging from drug discovery to market segmentation. Overlapping community detection (OCD) is the task of clustering networked data in which nodes may belong to multiple clusters. Existing OCD algorithms often struggle to achieve a suitable balance between detection quality and scalability. We, therefore, propose Highway, a scalable OCD algorithm that exploits the sparse backbone of the input network to perform efficient community inference. We used 728 Lancichinetti-Fortunato-Radicchi benchmark networks to compare Highway and its ablated version against 10 existing OCD algorithms. Our results, based on five performance measures, demonstrate a competitive performance for Highway. It ranks first in overlapping normalized mutual information with a 6.9% improvement over the strongest baseline. It also ranks second in all the other four performance measures. These comparative results suggest that Highway coupled with its backbone procedure offers a suitable accuracy-efficiency trade-off. The Highway algorithm is open-source and available as part of the CDlib library.

</details>

**Summary.** This work precisely disentangles the roles of in-plane and out-of-plane lattice strains in $	ext{CsV}_3	ext{Sb}_5$ by combining independent mechanical strain controls. It reveals that $c$-axis compression is the dominant tuning mechanism, strongly enhancing superconductivity while suppressing CDW order. Crucially, it isolates symmetry-specific coupling coefficients, showing a clear link between the CDW order parameter and out-of-plane lattice distortions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to decouple and quantify anisotropic coupling mechanisms (like $m_{13}$) in strongly correlated systems under external tuning fields provides general principles for understanding strain effects on quantum phases, which can be relevant to designing artificial lattice potentials in AMO or condensed matter simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to independently determine the roles of in-plane ($	ext{A}_{1g,1}$) and out-of-plane ($	ext{A}_{1g,2}$) symmetric strain channels governing the interplay between Charge-Density-Wave (CDW) order and superconductivity in $	ext{CsV}_3	ext{Sb}_5$.

**Main result.** Out-of-plane lattice control via $c$-axis compression dominates the phase competition, causing a massive linear enhancement of $T_c$ while suppressing $T_{	ext{CDW}}$. Furthermore, the out-of-plane cross-coupling coefficient ($m_{13}$) exhibits an order-parameter-like onset across the CDW transition.

**Method.** The researchers combined in-plane uniaxial strain with direct $c$-axis compression using specialized mechanical setups to measure resistivity components and extract symmetry-resolved elastoresistivity coefficients.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the kagome metal $	ext{CsV}_3	ext{Sb}_5$, which exhibits intertwined CDW and superconducting states. The analysis models the strain dependence of $T_c$ and $T_{	ext{CDW}}$ as linear functions of applied uniaxial strains ($\epsilon_{xx}$ and $\epsilon_{zz}$).

**Key observables.** Superconducting transition temperature ($T_c$), Charge-Density-Wave transition temperature ($T_{	ext{CDW}}$), and symmetry-resolved elastoresistivity coefficients ($m_{11}+m_{12}$ and $m_{13}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $c$-axis compression efficiency significantly exceeds in-plane strain efficiency; the CDW order parameter coupling exponent $eta \approx 0.5$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that by independently controlling both in-plane and out-of-plane strains, the intrinsic mixing of contributions found in conventional piezo experiments can be resolved.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure and strain symmetries; data show linear $\Delta T_c$ vs. strain dependence for both directions, and temperature plots reveal order-parameter-like onsets for extracted pure elastoresistivity coefficients below $T_{	ext{CDW}}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical problem of mixed strain responses in $	ext{CsV}_3	ext{Sb}_5$. It then details the combined experimental methodology using independent uniaxial strain control. The core results demonstrate that out-of-plane lattice control is dominant, followed by a detailed symmetry decomposition of the elastoresistivity coefficients.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The kagome metal CsV$_{3}$Sb$_{5}$ hosts an intriguing interplay between charge-density-wave (CDW) order and superconductivity that is highly sensitive to lattice distortions. However, determining the specific roles of the in-plane ($A_{1g,1}$) and out-of-plane ($A_{1g,2}$) symmetric strain channels has been hindered by their intrinsic mixing in conventional piezo-based experiments. Here, we combine in-plane uniaxial strain with direct $c$-axis compression to independently access and disentangle these symmetry-resolved responses in CsV$_{3}$Sb$_{5}$. We reveal that $c$-axis compression drives a massive, linear enhancement of the superconducting transition temperature ($T_c$) alongside a suppression of $T_{\rm CDW}$. The tuning efficiency of this out-of-plane deformation acts with an opposite sign and far exceeds that of in-plane strain, demonstrating that $c$-axis lattice control dictates the phase competition. Furthermore, by isolating the pure elastoresistivity coefficients, we find that the out-of-plane cross-coupling coefficient ($m_{13}$) is comparable in magnitude but opposite in sign to the in-plane response ($m_{11}+m_{12}$). Unlike the sharply peaked in-plane response, $m_{13}$ exhibits a distinct, order-parameter-like onset across the CDW transition. Our results establish that out-of-plane lattice control plays a dominant role in tuning the intertwined states in CsV$_{3}$Sb$_{5}$ and provide a general pathway for resolving strain-coupled electronic responses in layered quantum materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the Hopf ansatz, a novel binary-tree circuit parameterization designed to map and optimize arbitrary pure quantum states on the unit sphere. It successfully provides analytic expressions for the metric tensor and demonstrates how to prepare necessary tangent states directly within the circuit structure. This turns state preparation into a fully navigable framework for advanced quantum optimization algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, geometrically rigorous tool for variational quantum algorithms by transforming state optimization from an abstract manifold problem into a concrete, efficiently measurable circuit decomposition.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a systematic method for optimizing arbitrary pure quantum states on the unit sphere in Hilbert space, requiring more than just knowing if a target state is reachable.

**Main result.** The Hopf ansatz provides a unified, navigable framework that yields coordinates, local distance metrics, and preparable gradient directions for any pure-state optimization problem.

**Method.** The authors introduce the Hopf ansatz, a binary-tree circuit structure parameterized by angles. This structure allows for the explicit calculation of the state's metric tensor components and the preparation of necessary tangent states to compute gradients efficiently.

**Model / system.** The system is an arbitrary pure quantum state $|\psiangle$ in a Hilbert space, which is constrained to the unit sphere ($S^{N-1}$ or $S^{2N-1}$). The optimization process is framed as navigating this geometric manifold using variational quantum algorithms (VQE).

**Key observables.** Local distance information derived from the diagonal induced metric; gradient components calculated via measurable expectation values involving tangent states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parameters are the angles ($	heta$) defining the state preparation circuit, which govern both probability splitting (internal angles) and complex phases (leaf phases).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the existence of a fixed binary-tree gate skeleton and assumes operation within the 'regular set' where certain subtree weights are non-zero.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes, but the structure implies diagrams illustrating the binary tree circuit for state preparation and subsequent gradient measurement circuits.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the geometric challenge of pure-state optimization to introducing the Hopf ansatz as a solution. It then rigorously derives the diagonal metric tensor components (real and complex cases) and proves that the necessary derivative states can be synthesized using local trigonometric shifts within the same circuit structure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Optimizing arbitrary quantum state vectors is like navigating the unit sphere in Hilbert space: beyond target reachability, optimization asks for coordinates, local distance information, and measurable directions. We introduce the Hopf ansatz, a binary-tree circuit for arbitrary normalized real and complex state vectors. Internal angles steer probability between subtrees, leaf phases carry the complex degrees of freedom, and the same tree gives state preparation and an explicit inverse map from amplitudes to physical angles. Together these structures act as a compass for the search: the inverse map gives coordinates, the diagonal induced metric gives local distance information, and nonzero coordinate tangents become preparable normalized states.   For Hamiltonian objectives, and for objectives with the same local transition-moment form, each gradient component is a known scale factor times a transition moment between the current state and a tangent state. A branch-state construction expresses these moments through expectation-value measurements, while the tree organizes the compiled gradient settings by magnitude layer and leaf-indexed phase family. Thus the number of distinct compiled gradient-access circuit families grows only logarithmically with Hilbert-space dimension, while the measurement budget required for a chosen precision remains a separate statistical cost. In deterministic real-state benchmarks with exact costs, exact gradients, and known global optima, metric-aware Hopf optimizers reach numerical-precision median gaps, with the clearest baseline gains in smaller VQE mean final gaps and stronger concentration of metrology-inspired traces at numerical precision. The Hopf ansatz turns universal state preparation into a navigable framework for arbitrary pure-state optimization.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a three-point continuous-variable quantum MacWilliams identity for bosonic error-correcting codes. It uses advanced techniques involving characteristic functions and SDPs to derive bounds on code dimension. The key finding is that this sophisticated three-point bound collapses back to the established two-point bound for important classes of codes, providing deep structural insight into the limitations of current QEC theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep mathematical constraints on quantum error correction codes beyond simple stabilizer assumptions, linking algebraic structures (MacWilliams identities) directly to fundamental physical limitations imposed by positivity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors construct a three-point continuous-variable (CV) quantum MacWilliams identity to derive tighter semidefinite-programming (SDP) bounds on the dimension of CV quantum error-correcting codes.

**Main result.** They prove that for GKP lattice codes, the three-point bound does not improve upon the existing two-point linear-programming optimum; furthermore, general bosonic code analysis shows a collapse to the two-point bound due to structural constraints imposed by the code projector.

**Method.** The work involves constructing and analyzing complex integral kernels (the three-point identity) using characteristic functions derived from displacement operators, followed by rigorous SDP bounding techniques and positive-definiteness proofs in exact arithmetic.

**Model / system.** The system studied is bosonic quantum error-correcting codes, particularly those based on the Gottesman–Kitaev–Preskill (GKP) construction within an infinite-dimensional harmonic oscillator Hilbert space. The analysis focuses on weight distributions over the configuration space of Gram matrices.

**Key observables.** Code dimension ($K$), protection distance ($d$), and the minimum eigenvalue ($\lambda_{\min}$) of positive combination matrices $P(\mu)$ used as SDP certificates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The lattice structure (GKP), the number of modes ($N$), and the truncation level ($M$) are key parameters governing the bounds and analysis complexity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on an envelope-cut approximation ($\sigma > 0$) for physical realization, and certain structural assumptions about the form of the weight distributions ($A_3$ and $B_3$).

**Figures summary.** Figures/tables summarize calculated values for admissibility distances and ratios comparing different bounding techniques (e.g., $ho_N = K_{ne}/K_{Lev}$), confirming theoretical bounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the construction of the three-point identity kernel, through deriving its relationship to known two-point identities via BCH expansion, and culminates in proving the non-improvement of this bound for specific code types using SDP techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct the three-point continuous-variable (CV) quantum MacWilliams identity, extending the two-point framework of Burchards, and give its closed-form integral kernel. Its configuration space carries a symplectic invariant with no classical counterpart, which encodes the GKP quantization condition and a three-point sign phase. Using the identity, we derive the semidefinite-programming bounds it supports on the dimension of CV quantum error-correcting codes, and we prove, in two collapse theorems, that the three-point apparatus does not improve on the two-point bound. For GKP lattice codes the three-point optimum equals the Burchards two-point linear-programming optimum identically. This is an exact determination of the lattice three-point optimum, so the $E_8$ and Leech magic functions saturate it rather than beat it. For general bosonic codes a completely-positive reformulation bypasses the positivity obstruction that rules out the natural factored-form constructions; the phase-sign condition together with Choi positivity then force the three-point term to vanish. We certify this collapse for radial Choi forms on the first eight Laguerre levels at one mode, and leave the full trace-class cone open. Both collapses have a single cause with no classical analogue, the code projector: it orients the bound correctly but also removes the full positivity that powers the classical three-point improvement.

</details>

### [Casimir effect for a massive scalar field confined between parallel plates with a spatially varying effective mass](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15070v1)

**Authors:** R. L. Araújo Xavier, M. H. B. Chaves, E. R. Bezerra de Mello, Herondy Mota  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.15070v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

The solid black curve corresponds to the massless case, m0 = 0. The horizontal dotted line indicates the value unity, for which the additional contribution Eren c vanishes.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper calculates the renormalized vacuum energy (Casimir effect) for a massive scalar field confined between plates with a spatially varying effective mass. Using zeta-function regularization, the authors show the total energy splits into two parts: one standard Landau-like contribution and an additional term from the mass variation. The results confirm that while both terms are suppressed in strong coupling, the system's physics is largely governed by the standard boundary effect unless near a singular limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an exactly solvable framework for analyzing Casimir effects in inhomogeneous relativistic media, which is relevant for understanding boundary conditions and vacuum energy renormalization in complex quantum field theories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the Casimir effect for a massive scalar field confined between parallel plates when the effective mass term varies spatially.

**Main result.** The renormalized vacuum energy separates into two contributions: a Landau-like sector and an additional term from mass variation. For large coupling, both are exponentially suppressed, showing the Landau-like part dominates except near singular limits.

**Method.** The authors solve the Klein-Gordon equation using separation of variables, derive the normal mode spectrum, and calculate the vacuum energy via generalized zeta-function regularization and renormalization.

**Model / system.** The system is a massive real scalar field confined between two parallel plates (separated by L), governed by a Klein-Gordon equation incorporating a position-dependent effective mass term ($m_{	ext{eff}}^2 = m^2 + \alpha^2ho^2$).

**Key observables.** Renormalized vacuum energy per unit area ($E_{	ext{ren}}^0$), the Landau-like contribution ($E_{	ext{ren}}^L$), and the additional mass-induced contribution ($E_{	ext{ren}}^c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Plate separation $L$, coupling constant $\alpha$ (related to magnetic field strength), scalar mass $m$, and the dimensionless parameter $\alpha_0 = \alpha L^2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on generalized zeta-function regularization for renormalization, and the results are noted to be singular in the limit where the additional term's validity breaks down ($\alpha 	o 0$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 shows both renormalized energy contributions ($E_{	ext{ren}}^L$ and $E_{	ext{ren}}^c$) exhibit exponential suppression for large $\alpha_0$. Figure 3 illustrates the ratio of total to Landau-like energy, showing it approaches unity for large $\alpha_0$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the solvable model by solving the Klein-Gordon equation to find the quantized spectrum. It then calculates the vacuum energy using zeta-regularization, separating the result into two components based on the mass variation. Finally, it performs asymptotic analysis in different coupling regimes ($\alpha_0 \gg 1$ vs $\alpha_0 \ll 1$) to analyze the physical implications.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the Casimir effect for a massive real scalar field confined between two perfectly reflecting parallel plates in the presence of a position-dependent effective mass, a mechanism for coupling a scalar background to a scalar field. Exact normal modes are obtained by solving the corresponding Klein-Gordon equation, leading to a transverse energy spectrum that exhibits a characteristic Landau-like structure despite the absence of an external magnetic field. Upon quantization of the field, the vacuum energy is evaluated by means of generalized zeta-function regularization together with an appropriate renormalization procedure. The renormalized vacuum energy naturally separates into a Landau-like contribution and an additional term induced by the spatial dependence of the effective mass. We show analytically and numerically that both contributions are exponentially suppressed in the strong-coupling regime. In the opposite limit, the Landau-like contribution smoothly reproduces the standard vacuum energy for a massive scalar field confined between parallel plates, whereas the additional contribution becomes singular owing to the restricted domain of validity of the exact spectrum. Except in the vicinity of this singular limit, the vacuum energy is shown to be dominated by the Landau-like sector. Our results establish a direct connection between position-dependent effective masses and boundary-induced quantum vacuum phenomena, providing a new exactly solvable framework for the investigation of Casimir effects in spatially inhomogeneous relativistic systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the Hamilton extension as a method to systematically generate and recover logical Kochen-Specker contextuality in quantum systems. By analyzing how this construction transforms vector sets, the authors establish that six vectors constitute the smallest set capable of generating a contradiction with noncontextual hidden variables. This offers a new structural route for understanding fundamental limitations on quantum theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a systematic, constructive framework to engineer quantum nonclassicality using algebraic structures, which is highly relevant for theoretical understanding of contextuality beyond standard Bell tests.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates how logical Kochen-Specker (KS) contextuality, a hallmark of quantum mechanics, can emerge from mathematically structured vector sets rather than just being observed in specific configurations.

**Main result.** A constructive procedure called the Hamilton extension is shown to generate logical KS contradictions when applied to six or more parent vectors, establishing six as the minimum size required for this emergence.

**Method.** The authors employ the Hamilton extension, a construction based on quaternion algebra, to augment vector sets and analyze the resulting orthogonality graphs using graph coloring arguments related to KS colorings.

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum measurements represented by vector sets in $\mathbb{R}^4$ or $\mathbb{C}^d$, analyzed through their associated orthogonality graphs. The core mathematical tool is the Hamilton extension, $H[V]$.

**Key observables.** Logical Kochen-Specker contextuality (non-colorability of the derived graph structure) and the existence/structure of emergent measurement contexts.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The minimum parent set cardinality required to generate contradiction is six vectors. The analysis distinguishes between five-vector sets (always colorable) and six-vector sets (can be uncolorable).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof for the threshold relies on exhaustive case analysis of emergent contexts generated by five Hamilton-distinct projectors, showing they are always KS-colorable.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate apex augmentation effects and the structure of Hamiltonian extensions ($H[V]$), detailing how parent vectors generate 'Hamilton contexts' and additional edges create 'emergent contexts.'

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the Hamilton extension, then demonstrating its ability to recover contextuality lost via apex augmentation. It establishes a sharp threshold theorem regarding the minimum size of the parent set required for contradiction (six vectors). Finally, it provides detailed proofs showing that five-vector sets are always colorable while specific six-vector sets yield contradictions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Logical Kochen-Specker (KS) contextuality is widely regarded as an intrinsic property of specially constructed measurement configurations. We show instead that it can emerge from KS-colorable vector sets through a constructive procedure we call the Hamilton extension. Defined for four-dimensional vector sets, the Hamilton extension associates each real vector with a measurement context while inducing additional measurement contexts among Hamilton-extended children of distinct parent vectors. These emergent contexts fundamentally alter the compatibility structure, transforming KS-colorable configurations into KS-uncolorable ones and recovering logical contextuality lost under apex-vertex augmentation. We establish a sharp and optimal threshold -- the Hamilton extension of every five-vector parent set remains KS-colorable, whereas suitably chosen six-vector parent sets already generate logical KS contradictions. Thus, six vectors constitute the smallest parent set capable of generating KS contradiction through this mechanism. Our results reveal a new structural route to contextuality, provide a systematic framework for constructing compact KS sets, and have implications for contextuality-based quantum information protocols and graph-theoretic approaches to nonclassicality.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the notorious Fermion sign problem in PIMC by reframing the calculation as a statistical inference task. It develops sophisticated machine learning models, such as LSTMs, trained on permutation families to learn the underlying sign structure. This approach allows for stable and accurate energy extraction even in strongly correlated regimes where traditional Monte Carlo methods fail.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced computational tools for simulating interacting quantum many-body systems where sign problems are prohibitive, offering insights into the limitations of current numerical methods in condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming the Fermion sign problem encountered when calculating fermionic observables using path-integral Monte Carlo (PIMC) simulations.

**Main result.** The proposed statistical inference framework successfully extracts stable energy estimates in regimes where direct ratio estimation fails due to severe sign fluctuations, demonstrating robustness across benchmark systems.

**Method.** The authors recast the calculation as a statistical inference problem, training machine learning models (like LSTM networks) on permutation families derived from Monte Carlo samples, guided by physical priors.

**Model / system.** The work applies to fermionic systems simulated via PIMC, focusing on modeling the sign structure associated with exchange permutations within conjugacy classes ('permutation-families').

**Key observables.** Fermionic energy ($\langle \hat{E} angle_F$), average sign ($\langle \sigma angle_B$), and cycle-count vectors $\mathbf{C}$ characterizing permutation families.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System parameters include particle number ($N$), density regimes (low/high), temperature proxies, and simulation steps (e.g., $10^6$ to $300 	imes 10^6$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The methodology relies on imposing physical understanding as inductive priors within a Bayesian framework, treating the problem as an inference task rather than a direct ratio calculation.

**Figures summary.** Tables present numerical results for energy and sign estimates across varying simulation steps and system parameters ($N=7$ vs $N=33$), comparing different model fits (Linear, LSTM) against raw estimators.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the failure of direct ratio estimation to developing a statistical inference framework based on permutation families. It details specific modeling techniques (MaxEnt, MAP, LSTM) and validates them through applications to electron gas models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Starting from a \emph{probabilistic numerics} approach to the Fermion sign problem in path integral Monte Carlo, we recast the arithmetic calculation of a Fermionic observable as a statistical inference problem. We develop approaches that learn the behaviour of Fermion exchange cycles binned by the conjugacy class of the permutation group (which we term `permutation family'). This extends the work of DuBois, Brown and Alder\cite{dubois2017overcoming} to inhomogeneous and more complex systems. Monte Carlo samples are used to train models for both the probability of a permutation family and the energy of this set of exchange permutations. The overall Fermionic energy is then directly inferred from these models, without using a direct ratio estimator on the Monte Carlo samples.   By imposing physical understanding as inductive priors, we produce accurate and useful fits that remain robust even in regimes with severe sign problems. We generalise the linear (ideal-gas style) models of DuBois et al. with Bayesian priors that enforce the intuitive models of Feynman\cite{Feynman1953A} at their asymptotic limits. These linear models serve as the baseline for a Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) neural network, which is tasked with learning only the residual many-body \emph{correlations} on top of the physical model. We develop active important sampling methods driven by these models, which direct the Monte Carlo chains toward undersampled permutation regions, to efficiently reduce the variance in the observable.   We apply this framework to small experiments on benchmark systems: the spin-polarised uniform electron gas, and electrons in a 2D harmonic confining potential. In both cases we demonstrate that this inference-based framework can extract stable energies in regimes where direct Monte Carlo sampling fails due to the sign problem.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces SevenNet-Polar, an equivariant graph neural network designed for fast and accurate multi-task prediction of key material properties, including the Born effective charge (BEC). By training on diverse materials under extreme conditions, it achieves high accuracy while maintaining computational efficiency for simulating large systems with applied electric fields. This makes advanced, charge-aware molecular dynamics simulations significantly more accessible.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics potentials, the use of advanced equivariant GNNs to model complex tensor properties like BEC bridges ML techniques with fundamental quantum mechanical response functions relevant for understanding material polarization in external fields.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing computationally efficient methods for accurately predicting the Born effective charge (BEC) tensor, which is essential for simulating materials under external electric fields.

**Main result.** The proposed SevenNet-Polar framework achieves state-of-the-art accuracy across multiple properties (Energy, Forces, Stress, BEC), enabling large-scale simulations up to 1.5 million atoms on supercomputers.

**Method.** The authors developed an equivariant graph neural network architecture, SevenNet-Polar, which is trained in a multi-task setting to predict several material properties simultaneously.

**Model / system.** The model is applied to crystalline materials like $	ext{ZrO}_2$, $	ext{Li}_3	ext{PO}_4$, and perovskites. It handles complex structural environments such as high temperatures, defects, Nudged Elastic Band (NEB) trajectories, and grain boundaries.

**Key observables.** Energy (meV/atom), Forces ($	ext{meV}/^\circ	ext{\AA}$), Stress ($	ext{GPa}$), and the Born Effective Charge (BEC) tensor components ($e$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The model's performance is analyzed using power-law scaling exponents ($\alpha$) related to training data size, and hyperparameters like maximum spherical harmonic order ($l_{\max}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that the network can generalize robustly to structural environments not present in the training set. The loss function requires careful tuning due to differing variance scales between properties.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate power-law scaling of errors for BEC components versus training size, and show predicted tensor values along NEB trajectories or grain boundaries compared to DFT results.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the SevenNet-Polar framework, details its multi-task learning capabilities (Energy, Force, Stress, BEC), presents quantitative performance benchmarks across various materials and conditions, analyzes data scaling laws, and discusses computational scalability via FlashTP integration.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Accurate prediction of the Born effective charge (BEC) tensor is crucial for modeling materials under electric fields but remains computationally expensive. To bridge this gap, we present SevenNet-Polar, an equivariant graph neural network framework based on the SevenNet architecture for fast and accurate BEC predictions. Our BEC-only predictors can achieve an RMSE as low as 0.0043 e on ZrO$_2$, Li$_3$PO$_4$, and perovskites, despite the presence of high-temperature (up to 2,000 K) and defect-laden training data. Our all-in-one multitask models for predicting energy, forces, stress, and BEC in ZrO$_2$ and Li$_3$PO$_4$ achieve high accuracy with an RMSE of 1.0 meV/atom for energy, 12 meV/angstrom for forces, 0.05 GPa for stress, and 0.0029 e for BEC. BEC accuracy is not degraded by multitask training. Scaling analysis reveals distinct exponents for diagonal and off-diagonal BEC components, both of which exhibit less favorable scaling than energy, force and stress errors. SevenNet-Polar generalizes robustly when tested on scenarios containing structural environments absent from the training set, such as along nudged elastic band (NEB) trajectories or grain boundaries in ZrO$_2$. Accelerated by FlashTP, SevenNet-Polar enables simulations containing up to 1.5 million atoms on multi-GPU supercomputers and up to approximately 15,000 atoms on a single consumer-grade GPU. This makes charge-aware molecular dynamics simulations under electric fields more accessible.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a new worst-case quantum algorithm for solving the Optimal Polynomial Intersection problem. It significantly improves upon prior theoretical bounds by achieving high satisfaction rates ($s=1$) in regimes previously unreachable by DQI, particularly when analyzing MDS codes. The work advances the understanding of quantum computational limits for structured algebraic problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the techniques involving quantum algorithms to solve hard combinatorial/coding theory problems (like list decoding or polynomial intersection) are foundational to understanding the limits of quantum computation for structured data processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) problem, which seeks to find a low-degree polynomial satisfying membership constraints on many inputs.

**Main result.** The authors present a novel quantum algorithm that solves OPI in the worst-case regime beyond what is achievable by Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI).

**Method.** The core methodology involves applying a novel application of a Brascamp--Lieb-type inequality within the framework of Maximum Distance Separable (MDS) codes.

**Model / system.** The problem is framed abstractly over finite fields ($\mathbb{F}_q$) and relies on quantum oracle access to membership information for prescribed subsets $\{S_i\}$.

**Key observables.** Satisfaction rate ($s$), the ratio parameter $R=k/n$, and the existence threshold for a solution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The density $ho = |S_i|/q$ is crucial, with specific focus on the balanced case $ho=1/2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** General algorithmic results are restricted to MDS codes whose dual admits an efficient list decoder; initial guarantees were based on random choices of sets.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares achievable satisfaction rates ($s$) for DQI, average-case bounds, and the new worst-case algorithm in the balanced case. Figure 2 and Figure 3 compare saturation/satisfaction bounds across different regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the OPI problem definition to comparing algorithmic bounds against prior work (DQI, average-case). It then develops the novel quantum algorithm using advanced inequalities in the MDS setting, culminating in improved worst-case and existential bounds for OPI and MaxLINSAT.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) problem asks us to find a low-degree polynomial over a finite field whose values lie in prescribed subsets on as many given inputs as possible. Decoded quantum interferometry (DQI) gives a quantum algorithm for OPI in parameter regimes beyond those achieved by the best known classical heuristics. Follow-up works improve the parameter regimes, but their analyses are limited to average-case settings. Recently, Sun and Wootters showed that, even in the worst case, OPI has a solution in a larger parameter regime than the one covered by DQI. However, they left open whether one can design a quantum algorithm that solves OPI in the worst-case beyond the DQI regime.   We give such a quantum algorithm. As a byproduct, we also improve the existential bound of Sun and Wootters in certain parameter regimes. In particular, when each subset contains roughly half of the field elements, our algorithm finds a solution with satisfaction rate $s=1$ whenever the rate satisfies $R>0.75$. This matches the previous average-case bound, whereas DQI cannot achieve $s=1$ unless $R=1$. Our existential bound guarantees the existence of a solution when $R> 0.7158$, improving over the previous threshold $R>0.7495$. More generally, our existential results extend to the Max-LINSAT problem with respect to arbitrary maximum distance separable (MDS) codes. The corresponding algorithmic results apply only to MDS codes whose dual admits an efficient list decoder. Our results are obtained through a novel application of a Brascamp--Lieb-type inequality in the MDS setting, which may have further applications.

</details>

**Summary.** The study investigates the Boson Peak and elastic heterogeneity in C-S-H using THz spectroscopy and calorimetry. By analyzing how these vibrational signatures change with composition, the authors identify a structural crossover mechanism governing material properties like creep. This dual-probe approach offers a quantitative link between nanoscale dynamics and concrete durability.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an experimental link between low-frequency vibrational anomalies (like the BP) and macroscopic material properties (creep/thermal transport) in amorphous solids, which is relevant to understanding energy dissipation mechanisms in complex disordered systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to characterize the Boson Peak (BP) and medium-range elastic heterogeneity in Calcium Silicate Hydrate (C-S-H), linking nanoscale vibrational dynamics to macroscopic material durability.

**Main result.** Both terahertz spectroscopy and calorimetry locate the BP near 1 THz, revealing a structural crossover marked by the evolution of an elastic heterogeneity parameter ($\gamma$) from 0.98 to 0.48 as Ca/Si increases.

**Method.** The research employs a dual-probe approach combining Terahertz Time-Domain Spectroscopy (THz-TDS) and low-temperature calorimetry to probe vibrational dynamics in C-S-H samples synthesized with varying Ca/Si ratios.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Calcium Silicate Hydrate (C-S-H), the binding phase of hardened cement, which acts as a disordered solid. The analysis involves fitting spectral data to models like Damped Harmonic Oscillators and analyzing the vibrational density of states.

**Key observables.** Boson Peak frequency ($
u_{BP}$), medium-range dynamical correlation length ($\xi$), coherent-potential elastic-heterogeneity parameter ($\gamma$), THz integrated spectral weight, and calorimetric $C_p/T^3$ peak.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ca/Si ratios (0.5 to 1.7); BP frequency near 1 THz; $\gamma$ ranging from 0.98 to 0.48.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of Bruggeman correction for crystalline impurities and the assumption that the dual-probe approach provides a comprehensive view beyond single-technique limitations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in detail, but comparisons are made between THz spectral weight vs. Ca/Si ratio and calorimetric $C_p/T^3$ peak vs. Ca/Si ratio to track structural crossovers.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically characterizes the BP using two complementary vibrational probes (THz-TDS and calorimetry) across varying compositions, analyzes the trends in spectral weight and heterogeneity parameters ($\gamma$), and interprets these findings as evidence of a structural crossover mechanism.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The boson peak (BP), a universal vibrational anomaly of disordered solids, has been predicted but not systematically characterized in calcium silicate hydrate (C-S-H), the binding phase of hardened cement. Building on a preliminary terahertz survey, we characterize the BP across five Ca/Si ratios (0.5-1.7) using terahertz time-domain spectroscopy (THz-TDS) and low-temperature calorimetry, two probes of vibrational dynamics that complement the static picture of conventional structural methods. After Bruggeman correction for crystalline impurities, both probes locate the BP near 1 THz; they agree on frequency but diverge in intensity. The terahertz integrated spectral weight and the calorimetric Cp/T3 peak both fall monotonically with Ca/Si, whereas the apparent terahertz peak height is maximal at Ca/Si = 1.0, where damping is low and oscillator strength still substantial. This decoupling marks a structural crossover between silicate-chain depolymerization and interlayer calcium filling. From the BP we obtain a medium-range dynamical correlation length of order 1 nm (0.3-2 nm) and a coherent-potential elastic-heterogeneity parameter that decreases from gamma = 0.98 to 0.48 as Ca/Si rises; the Debye-normalized BP frequency (nu_BP/nu_D = 0.15-0.17) places C-S-H within the range reported for silicate glasses. Because gamma governs the distribution of energy barriers for local structural rearrangements, it provides a quantitative, composition-resolved descriptor relevant to the intrinsic creep and thermal transport of C-S-H, linking nanoscale vibrational dynamics to the macroscopic durability of concrete. The dual-probe boson-peak approach is transferable to other amorphous solids, including the supplementary cementitious materials of low-carbon cements.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a method to construct high-performance quantum error-correcting codes by adapting Low-Density Generator Matrix (LDGM) codes into Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) structures. The authors analyze the performance of these codes under depolarizing noise using iterative decoding and Density Evolution, showing that careful structural design can yield excellent fault-tolerance capabilities.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of error thresholds and error floors using established coding theory tools (LDGM codes) provides concrete benchmarks for designing robust quantum computation protocols relevant to open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing quantum error-correcting codes suitable for fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Main result.** The authors propose a new family of CSS codes built from LDGM matrices, demonstrating that optimizing the code structure can significantly improve performance thresholds while maintaining low error floors.

**Method.** They construct the codes using row operations on LDGM generator and parity-check matrices, employing iterative message passing decoding (belief propagation) optimized via Discrete Density Evolution (DDE).

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum stabilizer code structured as a CSS code. The construction leverages the sparsity of classical LDGM codes to satisfy necessary mathematical constraints for quantum error correction.

**Key observables.** Residual error rates ($	ext{BER}_1, 	ext{BER}_2$), convergence threshold, and error floor performance under depolarizing noise.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Depolarizing parameter ($p$), LDGM code degree (e.g., $(8, 8)$ to $(12, 12)$), and structural parameters like $N_a$ and $N_b$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the equivalence between iterative message passing decoding simulations and predictions derived from Discrete Density Evolution (DDE).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how optimizing structural parameters ($N_b$) improves thresholds, how code degree affects error floors versus thresholds, and general performance trends across varying noise levels.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the mathematical framework for CSS codes using LDGM matrices (satisfying commutativity conditions) to detailing the decoding process via message passing. It then moves into rigorous performance analysis by simulating residual errors under depolarizing noise, comparing results against DDE predictions while varying structural and code parameters.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct a new family of Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) codes using the generator and parity-check matrices of Low-Density Generator Matrix (LDGM) codes, with row operations applied to both matrices in order to achieve the desired quantum rate. Decoding is performed in an iterative manner, by applying message passing over the associated graph, and discrete Density Evolution (DDE) is used to optimize performance in the depolarizing channel. The proposed construction offers high flexibility and easiness in the design, producing quantum codes that possess excellent error correction capabilities. By properly designing the structure of the code, we are able to control and bound the weight of the stabilizer generators to a small value, which results in codes particularly well suited for fault-tolerant quantum computation. At the same time, these codes achieve very good performance in terms of error correction capability.

</details>

<sub>[↑ back to top](#top)</sub>

**Authors:** Alessio Baldelli, Davide Orsucci, Francisco Lázaro, Massimo Battaglioni  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.14988v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
